When few labeled target data suffice: a theory of semi-supervised domain adaptation via fine-tuning from multiple adaptive starts
Wooseok Ha, Yuansi Chen

Abstract
Semi-supervised domain adaptation (SSDA) aims to achieve high predictive performance in the target domain with limited labeled target data by exploiting abundant source and unlabeled target data. Despite its significance in numerous applications, theory on the effectiveness of SSDA remains largely unexplored, particularly in scenarios involving various types of source-target distributional shifts. In this work, we develop a theoretical framework based on structural causal models (SCMs) which allows us to analyze and quantify the performance of SSDA methods when labeled target data is limited. Within this framework, we introduce three SSDA methods, each having a fine-tuning strategy tailored to a distinct assumption about the source and target relationship. Under each assumption, we demonstrate how extending an unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) method to SSDA can achieve minimax-optimal target performance with limited target labels. When the relationship between source and target data is only vaguely known—a common practical concern—we propose the Multi Adaptive-Start Fine-Tuning (MASFT) algorithm, which fine-tunes UDA models from multiple starting points and selects the best-performing one based on a small hold-out target validation dataset. Combined with model selection guarantees, MASFT achieves near-optimal target predictive performance across a broad range of types of distributional shifts while significantly reducing the need for labeled target data. We empirically validate the effectiveness of our proposed methods through simulations.



1
Introduction

Classical statistical machine learning models are typically trained under the assumption that training and test data are drawn from the same probability distribution. This supervised learning paradigm is well-founded in statistical learning theory and serves as the basis for many modern applications. However, in real-world scenarios, this assumption often fails due to distributional shifts, where the data distribution in the training (source) domain differs from that in the test (target) domain. As a result, models trained under this assumption often experience a significant decline in model performance when deployed in new test domains. For example, modern image classification models, trained on large-scale datasets containing over a million labeled high-resolution images, achieve remarkable classification accuracy
[
30
]
, sometimes surpassing human-level performance
[
23
]
. Nevertheless, these models often struggle to generalize to more challenging testing scenarios such as those with a different background context
[
17
]
. Acquiring and labeling millions of images for every possible deployment scenario is impractical and expensive. This motivates the development of domain adaptation (DA) methods, which aim to transfer knowledge from a source domain to a related but different target domain.

A DA setting that has witnessed a recent surge of interest is unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA), where labeled source data is available, but only unlabeled data is observed from the target domain. UDA methods typically address distributional shifts by aligning feature distributions between source and target domains while optimizing source performance, with the hope that the learned common feature representation generalizes well to target data
[
3
,
16
,
13
]
. While this approach can be highly effective given strong beliefs on the assumed source-target relationship, the absence of target supervision makes UDA inherently unreliable in certain scenarios. There have been settings where feature alignment does not necessarily lead to improved target performance
[
69
]
. What’s worse, without labeled target data, there is no straightforward way to detect such a failure
[
62
]
. Another DA setting, called domain generalization (DG), trains models that are robust to distribution shifts without access to any labeled or unlabeled target data and prepares for worst-case distribution shifts. While DG methods enhance model adaptability in many scenarios, they tend to be overly conservative, often leading to suboptimal performance when the target distribution is not as adversarial as assumed
[
24
,
11
,
15
,
51
]
.

Semi-supervised domain adaptation (SSDA)
[
14
,
52
]
emerges as a more practical DA setting by incorporating a few labeled target examples alongside the source and unlabeled target data. By leveraging a small amount of target supervision, SSDA bridges the gap between UDA, which lacks target labels, and fully supervised learning, which requires abundant labeled target data. For instance, in medical imaging
[
38
]
, models trained on a large clinical routine dataset in one imaging setting may fail to generalize to data in a slightly different setting, due to differences in imaging protocols. While UDA methods may struggle to address such distribution shifts
[
69
]
, SSDA can leverage a few labeled target images to significantly improve adaptation and model performance.

Intuitively, the advantages of SSDA are particularly pronounced in high-dimensional settings where distributional shifts exhibit a low-dimensional structure. In such cases, large amounts of labeled source data are essential to train a base model. At the same time, a few labeled target examples can guide model choices among candidate models which deviate from the base model in low-dimensional directions. However, effective use of the small labeled target dataset in training remains a key challenge in SSDA. Over-reliance on labeled source data can introduce significant bias, whereas depending too heavily on the small target dataset can lead to high variance. Striking a balance between these two sources of information in a structured fashion is crucial. While prior research has explored various strategies for using target labels—such as incorporating them into source model training, using them for model selection, or fine-tuning pre-trained models
[
5
,
68
,
64
,
32
]
—a general guideline remains elusive.

In this paper, we develop a theoretical framework to analyze the feasibility of SSDA with limited labeled target data. Under this framework, SSDA methods are quantitatively compared after distributional shifts are formalized via perturbations of structural causal models (SCMs). Our goal is to rigorously address fundamental questions in SSDA such as: Can SSDA methods provably outperform empirical risk minimization (ERM) trained on target data alone? How many labeled target samples are required for SSDA to also outperform ERM trained on source data alone, or UDA and DG methods? What types of low-dimensional structures in distributional shifts can SSDA exploit? By addressing these questions, we hope to provide theoretical insights and practical guidelines for the effective use of limited labeled target data in SSDA.

1.1
Our contributions

Our contributions are three-fold. First, by extending the classical supervised learning theory, we develop a theoretical framework to analyze the feasibility of SSDA with limited labeled target data. While the classical supervised learning theory assumes that training and test data are drawn from an identical underlying distribution, modeling distribution shift between source and target requires an extension that goes beyond the identical distribution assumption. Second, inspired by UDA methods, we introduce three SSDA methods which deploy fine-tuning strategies tailored to three types of assumptions relating source and target distributions. Under these assumptions, we establish minimax risk upper and lower bounds for our proposed methods, demonstrating the benefits of incorporating UDA methods into SSDA. Finally, we introduce Multi Adaptive-Start Fine-Tuning (MASFT) method to address SSDA scenarios when the source-target relationship is only vaguely known and one is uncertain about the choice of fine-tuning strategies.

1.2
Related work

As a subfield of transfer learning, domain adaptation, also known as transductive transfer learning
[
47
]
, aims to develop effective methods when distribution shifts occur between training (source) data and test (target) data, while the underlying task remains the same. There are three standard settings of DA: unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA), domain generalization (DG), and semi-supervised domain adaptation (SSDA). As the name SSDA suggests, the literature on semi-supervised learning (SSL) and UDA constitute an important part of the prior work on SSDA. For a comprehensive review which treats SSL or UDA in an isolated context other than SSDA, we refer readers to recent surveys
[
58
]
and
[
61
,
70
]
. Here, we highlight the most relevant developments in SSDA.

Empirical advances in SSDA.
SSDA methods are shown to outperform UDA methods when even a few labeled target samples are available. Saito et al.
[
52
]
demonstrated the benefits of incorporating limited labeled target data alongside unlabeled target data with a minimax entropy approach to enhance invariant feature extraction in image classification tasks. Taking advantage of the success of adversarial neural networks in enforcing domain-invariance in UDA
[
16
]
, Jiang et al.
[
27
]
extended it to SSDA by leveraging labeled target data to refine feature alignment.
Yang et al.
[
65
]
incorporated the idea of co-training
[
8
]
to SSDA, which simultaneously train an inter-domain UDA model and an intra-domain SSL model, on the 3-shot learning task on DomainNet dataset
[
45
]
.
Subsequent improvements on DomainNet dataset have been driven by more effective invariance regularization, such as multi-level consistency learning
[
64
]
, and enhanced feature adaptation strategies, such as source label adaptation
[
68
]
. These advancements continue to push the state-of-the-art performance in SSDA, particularly in low-shot learning scenarios in computer vision.

Relation to tranditional fine-tuning.
Fine-tuning (FT) is a widely used approach in transfer learning, where a pretrained model is further trained on a smaller dataset by updating some or all of its parameters to improve its performance on a new domain or task. There is substantial evidence of FT improving accuracy in large-scale investigations in computer vision
[
29
,
10
]
. However, FT is also shown to underperform especially in the present of large distribution shift
[
32
]
. In comparison, SSDA extends traditional FT by explicitly integrating domain-specific assumptions and often leveraging unlabeled target data using UDA methods, thereby enabling more effective alignment between source and target domains.

SSDA ideas in statistics.
SSDA often appears in the literature under the broader framework of transfer learning, which may not explicitly mention the term SSDA. For instance, Bastani
[
4
]
studied SSDA—though they refer to it as transfer learning—in the context of high-dimensional prediction tasks, where the distribution shift between source and target data is captured by a sparse function of features. Li et al.
[
35
]
analyzed a similar transfer learning problem in a high-dimensional linear regression setting, assuming that the regression coefficients change only in a small subset of coordinates between the source and target domains. Under this assumption, the problem reduces to the well-studied high-dimensional sparse regression (see e.g.
[
60
]
), where effective adaptation can be achieved with far fewer target samples than the number of features. Additionally, Xiong et al.
[
63
]
considered a similar high-dimensional sparse linear regression problem under the distributionally robust optimization framework.

Empirical Bayes methods
[
48
]
are also closely related to SSDA. They are Bayesian inference methods which puts data-driven prior on regression coefficients rather than relying solely on expert knowledge. By learning priors from the source data, these methods effectively borrow source information for improved estimation and prediction on the target domain. Recent advances in empirical Bayes methods have extended their applicability to transfer learning
[
33
]
.

A fundamental distinction between these classical methods and our SSDA approaches based on structural causal models (SCMs) lies in how they model the relationship between source and target distributions:

•

Classical methods, such as the two approaches above, assume that the distributional shift from source to target occurs directly in the regression coefficients to be estimated. In other words, they impose structure on how the true model parameters evolve across domains.

•

SSDA methods based on SCMs assume that distribution shifts arise due to interventions in the data generation process, rather than being explicitly defined in terms of model coefficients
[
44
,
46
,
9
]
. These interventions can stem from changes in underlying causal mechanisms, leading to variations in observed data distributions that ultimately would also influence parameters to be estimated. In particular, SCMs enabled quantitative comparison of UDA methods under various distribution shifts
[
11
]
.

SCM-based SSDA methods are particularly advantageous in DA scenarios where direct modeling of parameter shifts is impractical—for instance, in image classification tasks involving complex degradations like resolution loss, noise, and lighting changes. By modeling shifts in the data generation process instead—for example, through explicit noise or smoothing operations—these methods offer a more flexible alternative framework for addressing such distributional shifts. In this work, we build upon this SCM-based perspective to propose and analyze novel SSDA methods.

1.3
Notation

To distinguish subscripts from coordinates, we use
X_{[j]}
to denote the
j
-th coordinate of a random vector
X
. For a matrix
A
, let
\textnormal{col}({A})
denote the column space of
A
, and
A_{*S}
denote the columns of
A
indexed by
S
. We use
\spn(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
to denote the linear subspace formed by all linear combinations of the vectors
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
. The
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
norms of a vector
x
are denoted by
\left\|x\right\|_{1}
and
\left\|x\right\|_{2}
, respectively.

2
Preliminaries and background

In this section, we introduce the semi-supervised domain adaptation (SSDA) problem and present structural causal models as a way to specify the relationship between source and target data distributions. Then, we review several baseline UDA methods, which serve as starting points for building SSDA methods.

2.1
SSDA problem setup

Consider the supervised learning problem with
M

(M\geq 1)
source domains (or environments) and a single target domain (or environment). Here each domain is associated with a data distribution and the
labeled
and
unlabeled
datasets drawn from that distribution. Specifically, for the
m
-th source domain,
m=1,\ldots,M
, we observe

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(m)}=\{(X^{(m)}_{i},Y^{(m)}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}^{(m)}\text{ and }\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{(m)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{X},

where
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}
denotes the
m
-th source distribution, and
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{X}
denotes its marginal distribution on the covariates
X
. For the target domain, we similarly observe

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(0)}=\{(X^{{(0)}}_{i},Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}\text{ and }\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}_{X},

where
\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}
denotes the target distribution, and
\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}_{X}
denotes its marginal distribution on the covariates
X
. We assume that in all domains (
m\geq 0
), the covariates lie in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
X^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the responses (labels) are real-valued,
Y^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
from a function class
\mathcal{F}
that predicts labels from covariates, its prediction performance in the target domain is measured via the population target risk

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell(f(X^{{(0)}}),Y^{{(0)}})}\right],

(1)

where
\ell:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a loss function that measures the discrepancy between a predicted label
f(x)
and an observed label
y
—throughout the paper, unless stated otherwise, we use the squared loss defined as
\ell(f(x),y)\coloneqq(y-f(x))^{2}
and restrict the function class to linear functions
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{\beta}:x\mapsto\beta^{\top}x\mid\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. Similarly, the
m
-th source population risk (
m\geq 1
) is defined as
\mathcal{R}^{(m)}({f})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell(f(X^{(m)}),Y^{(m)})}\right]
.

In SSDA, one would like to make use of both source and target data (unlabeled and limited labeled) to estimate a function
f
such that its population target risk is small. This population target risk is compared with the optimal population target risk, and the difference is quantified as the excess risk, defined as

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f})\coloneqq\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f})-\min_{\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({\widetilde{f}}).

(2)

When shifts between the source and target data can be arbitrary, it is generally impossible to achieve a small excess risk with only a limited number of labeled target samples, even with an infinite source and unlabeled target samples
[
11
]
. Additional assumptions relating source and target data are needed to achieve nontrivial target performance in SSDA.

Anticausal linear structural causal models

To enable theoretical analysis of UDA and SSDA methods, the first assumption we impose is that the data in both source and target domains are generated according to linear structural causal models (SCMs)
[
44
]
. While this assumption is not needed to apply our SSDA methods, it is needed when we quantify their excess risk.

Assumption 1

(Anticausal linear SCMs)
.

For
m=0,1,\ldots,M
, each data distribution
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}
corresponding to the
m
-th domain is specified by the structural equation

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{(m)}\\
Y^{(m)}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}B^{(m)}&b^{(m)}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X^{(m)}\\
Y^{(m)}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}\end{bmatrix}.

(3)

Here
B^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are the connectivity matrices encoding the causal relations among the covariates, and
b^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the anticausal weights connecting labels and covariates. We assume
H^{(m)}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}-B^{(m)}
is invertible for all
m\geq 0
.

In Assumption
1
, by setting the entire row below
B^{(m)}
to zero, we focus on a subclass of SCMs called anticausal models
[
53
]
, where the labels
Y
are the causes and the covariates
X
are the descendants of
Y
. While the SSDA theory developed here can be generalized to mixed causal and anticausal SCMs, anticausal SCMs are sufficiently rich to illustrate our main ideas. Under Assumption
1
, shifts between the source and target distributions may result from interventions on the distribution of noise terms
\epsilon^{(m)}_{X},\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}
, on the anticausal weights
b^{(m)}
, on the connectivity matrix
B^{(m)}
, or from a combination of these factors. Consequently, these interventions lead to several distinct forms of distributional changes, which necessitate distinct adaptation strategies to address.

2.2
Baseline unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) methods

We introduce several basic UDA estimators which serve as a comparison baseline and building blocks for our upcoming SSDA methods. Here, we use the term UDA estimators broadly to refer to estimators that leverage source data and/or unlabeled target data but do
not
rely on labeled target data—in this sense, DG methods are also treated as UDA methods. For clarity, we present these methods specialized to the linear function class; their most generic forms can be found in, e.g.,
[
16
,
24
,
62
]
.

Ordinary least squares (OLS) on individual source datasets is the most basic estimator in UDA: one trains on the source data, and hopes it works on the target data.

•

OLS-Src
(m)
: the population OLS estimator based on the
m
-th (
m\geq 1
) source dataset.

\displaystyle f_{\textnormal{LS}}^{(m)}(x)\coloneqq\beta^{(m)}_{\textnormal{LS}}{}^{\top}x,\text{ where }\beta^{(m)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{(m)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)})^{2}}\right].

(4)

When the source and target domains are sufficiently different, OLS-Src
(m)
may have large target risk. Nevertheless, it serves as a baseline for risk comparison, and a starting point for fine-tuning.

Next, we introduce two nontrivial UDA estimators that incorporate an invariance penalty during the training process.
By enforcing invariance, these estimators seek to align the source and target data distributions under an appropriate feature representation, and thereby reducing bias from using source data.

The first estimator is domain invariant projection (DIP)
[
3
,
16
,
55
]
, also known as domain invariant representation learning, which aims to learn a representation of the covariates that minimizes the source risk while enforcing invariance of feature representations between source and target domains. The main assumption behind DIP is the existence of a representation space shared by the source and target domains. Under the linear function class, DIP is implemented by searching for a common linear projection and by matching marginal distributions.

•

DIP
(m)
: the population DIP
(m)
estimator based on the
m
-th (
m\geq 1
) source dataset and the marginal target covariates.

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{(m)}_{\text{gDIP}}(x)\coloneqq\beta^{(m)}_{\text{gDIP}}{}^{\top}x,\text{ where }&\beta^{(m)}_{\text{gDIP}}{}^{\top}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{(m)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)})^{2}}\right],\\
&\hskip 65.04256pt\text{s.t. }\ \ \mathfrak{D}\left({\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}},{\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}}\right)=0.\end{split}

(5)

The term
\mathfrak{D}\left({\cdot},{\cdot}\right)
denotes a divergence or a distance between two probability distributions, such as moment-based divergence
[
45
]
, maximum mean discrepancy (MMD)
[
20
]
, or adversarial-based domain classifier
[
16
]
.

The second estimator is conditional invariant penalty (CIP)
[
18
,
24
]
, which aligns the conditional distributions of the feature representations given the labels
Y
. Unlike DIP which matches the unlabeled source and target data, CIP incorporates label information across multiple source datasets into distribution matching.

•

CIP
: the population CIP estimator based on the multiple source datasets.

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{\text{gCIP}}(x)\coloneqq\beta_{\text{gCIP}}^{\top}x,\text{ where }&\beta_{\text{gCIP}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{(m)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)})^{2}}\right],\\
&\text{s.t. }\ \ \mathfrak{D}\left({\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{{\beta^{\top}X}|{Y}}},{\mathcal{P}^{(m-1)}_{{\beta^{\top}X}|{Y}}}\right)=0\textit{ a.s.}\text{ $\forall m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}$},\end{split}

(6)

where
\mathfrak{D}\left({\cdot},{\cdot}\right)
is a divergence between two probability distributions. The CIP penalty matches all conditional distributions of the covariates across all source domains. If this conditional invariance also holds in the target domain, then CIP operates as if source and target data share the same conditional distribution.

The assumption behind CIP is the existence of features with invariant conditional distribution given label
Y
across all source and target domains. These features are also called
conditionally invariant components
(CICs)
[
18
,
24
]
. While the use of CIP-type matching penalty ensures conditional invariance across all source domains and has various applications in DA
[
18
,
36
,
24
]
, whether it identifies the CICs which are also conditionally invariant in the target domain is generally far from trivial.
This problem under additive shift interventions was studied recently in
[
11
,
62
]
, which quantifies the conditions and the number of heterogeneous source domains needed for identifiability.

3
Minimax framework in SSDA and benefits of fine-tuning starting from UDA

In this section, we present a minimax framework in SSDA which extends the classical minimax framework in supervised learning. Then we provide simple examples to illustrate the benefits of fine-tuning starting from UDA methods in SSDA.

3.1
Minimax framework in SSDA

To simplify our analysis, whenever a quantitative risk comparion is needed, we restrict our attention to the linear function class with data generated from linear SCMs in Assumption
1
.

First, the oracle estimator is defined as the population target risk minimizer under linear function class and has an explicit form.

•

Oracle estimator
: the population OLS estimator, or oracle estimator, based on the target dataset.

\beta^{\star}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right],

(7)

where
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]
is assumed to be invertible.

Let
\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
denote the class of joint distributions over
n^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
and
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
. The classical minimax excess risk is defined as

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\widehat{\beta}-\beta^{\star})^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}-\beta^{\star})}\right],

(8)

where the minimum is over all estimators based on (both labeled and unlabeled) target datasets, and the expectation is over
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
.

When
\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
corresponds to the class of causal linear prediction models, the minimax excess risk has been extensively studied in the literature
[
25
,
41
]
. However, the minimax framework for anticausal models, with additional unlabeled data, has received relatively limited attention. In the following proposition, we establish a lower bound on the minimax excess risk (
8
) when
\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
is specified by the class of anticausal linear SCMs in Assumption
1
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
denote the class of joint distributions over
\mathcal{D}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
, where each labeled and unlabeled target sample is generated according to the anticausal linear SCMs in Assumption
1
. The minimax excess risk (
8
) over
\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}
satisfies

\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq c{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\min\left\{\frac{d}{n^{(0)}},1\right\},

(9)

where
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\coloneqq\var\left({Y^{{(0)}}}\right)>0
is the variance of
Y^{{(0)}}
, and
c>0
is a universal constant.

The proof of Proposition
3.1
is standard and is given in Appendix
F.1
for completeness. The main technicality is to ensure that unlabeled target data samples do not reveal any information about the parameter of interest. As a consequence, the minimax lower bound in Proposition
3.1
depends only on the number of labeled target samples
n^{(0)}
, and is independent of the number of unlabeled samples
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
. In words, Proposition
3.1
shows that the number of labeled target data samples has to be larger than the number of parameters to achieve a nontrivial excess risk.

In SSDA, it is common to have limited target labels in the sense of
n^{(0)}\ll d
. Under this setting, the lower bound (
9
) indicates that target-only estimators cannot reduce the excess risk below a constant. This leads to the following central question in SSDA: can we improve upon the target-only minimax rate
\mathcal{O}\left({\min\{d/n^{(0)},1\}}\right)
by incorporating additional source data? To study this, consider the class
\mathfrak{P}
of joint distributions over
n^{(m)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}
and
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}
for all
m\geq 0
. We can then naturally extend the definition of minimax excess risk to the SSDA setting

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right],

(10)

where the minimum is now over all estimators that take advantage of both source and target datasets (labeled and unlabeled), and the expectation is over
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}
.

In general, without explicitly specifying the relationship between source and target distributions in
\mathfrak{P}
, it is not clear whether additional source data can help improve the target excess risk compared to using target data alone. Consider the following two simple examples.

Example 1
: Assume
b^{(m)}=0
for all
m\geq 1
while
b^{{(0)}}\neq 0
. That is, the source covariates are fully uninformative for label prediction. In this case, the minimax excess risk (
10
) collapses to the target-only version (
8
), and source data provides no additional benefits.

Example 2
: Alternatively, suppose the source and target distributions are exactly identical. In this case, each source sample is as informative as a target sample. By Proposition
3.1
, the minimax excess risk is lower bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left({\min\left\{\frac{d}{\sum_{m=0}^{M}n^{(m)}},1\right\}}\right)
, and this rate is achieved by OLS on pooled datasets as long as
\sum_{m=0}^{M}n^{(m)}>d
(c.f. see Proposition
C.1
).

The above two simple cases highlight the fact that understanding the benefit of additional source data in SSDA requires careful modeling of the source and target relationship. In practice, most scenarios lie between these two simple cases. The challenge is that there’s no “one-size-fits-all” solution: distributional shifts can take various forms, which impacts the choice of adaptation strategies.

3.2
Illustrative examples on benefits of fine-tuning starting from UDA

We present two nontrivial cases to illustrate how fine-tuning starting from UDA estimators can improve target performance when labeled target data is limited.

Here is the intuition on why UDA estimators are useful in SSDA. Under certain source-target relationship, UDA estimators in Section
2.2
can generalize to target data by learning components shared across both the source and target distributions. However, due to the absence of labeled target data in the UDA setting, they fail to capture target-specific components that could further improve target prediction. When labeled target data becomes available in SSDA, it is therefore natural to fine-tune the UDA estimators in the specific directions to enhance target performance.

In general, a fine-tuning method that starts from a UDA estimator is formulated as follows. Suppose the function class
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
is parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta
, where
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
is a subset of a
D
-dimensional Euclidean space. Let
\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}\in\Theta
denote a UDA estimator trained with source and unlabeled target data.

•

Generic fine-tuning formulation
: Given a subset
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}\subset\Theta
, the model parameter
\theta
is fine-tuned on the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
via the following constrained optimization

\displaystyle\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\ell(f_{\theta}(X^{{(0)}}_{i}),Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\text{ s.t. }\theta-\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}\in\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}.

(11)

Here,
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}
represents the set with low intrinsic dimension or low complexity, such as a low-dimensional subspace of the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, or a subset with low complexity (e.g. measured by Gaussian width
[
59
, Definition 7.5.1]
), tailored to specific problem setting.

Recent empirical studies have shown that in many deep neural networks and language models, fine-tuning happens primarily in a tiny subspace relative to the ambient dimension
D
of the parameter space
[
34
,
19
,
26
]
. The formulation (
11
) is flexible enough to accommodate such settings by appropriately choosing the initial parameter
\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}
and restricting fine-tuning to a low-dimensional subset
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}
. However, the choices of
\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}
and
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}
are typically made in an ad hoc manner with limited theoretical justification.

In the following, we demonstrate that depending on the source-target relationship, different combinations of UDA estimators and low-dimensional subsets
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}
can be deployed so that fine-tuning (
11
) leads to improved target performance in a principled way.

Figure 1:
The causal diagrams and structural equations illustrating Example 3. (a) The causal diagram for the first source domain. (b) The causal diagram for the target domain.
The red edges indicate interventions that modify the causal effect of
X_{[1]}
on
X_{[2]}
and
X_{[3]}
.

Example 3:

As a concrete example, we consider source and target data generated from the linear SCMs in Figure
1
. Here, the way
X_{[1]}
connects to other covariates shifts between source and target, which propagates to change all of its descendants.

Under this data generation scenario, the OLS-Src
(1)
and the oracle estimators can be explicitly derived as

\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}-3,&-1,&1\end{bmatrix}^{\top},\quad\text{and}\quad\beta^{\star}=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}5,&-1,&1\end{bmatrix}^{\top}.

We observe that the two estimators differ only in their first coefficient. This is because although the shift of connectivity of
X_{[1]}
on
X_{[2]}
and
X_{[3]}
changes the marginal correlations with
Y
across source and target domains, the partial correlations after eliminating the effect of
X_{[1]}
are the same. As a result, only the first coefficient of OLS-Src
(1)
needs to be adjusted to match the oracle. By exploiting this sparsity structure, we can adjust OLS-Src
(1)
with only a small fraction of labeled target samples. In contrast, the estimator trained solely on target data requires significantly more samples to achieve a comparable level of excess risk.

This example shows that under interventions affecting the connectivity between covariates, OLS-Src
(1)
can serve as an effective UDA starting point for fine-tuning, which settles many oracle coefficients from source data and leaves only one coefficient to fine-tune so that
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}=\left\{\text{the first coefficient of $\beta$}\right\}
. A generalization and its finite-sample guarantees are given in Section
4.2
.

Figure 2:
The causal diagrams and structural equations illustrating Example 4. The covariates
X
exhibit mean shifts between the first source and target domains.

Example 4
. As another example, we consider source and target data generated from the linear SCMs in Figure
2
. Here, the shift between source and target distributions arises from additive mean interventions, where the means of all three covariates are shifted with deterministic numbers.

Here, the DIP
(1)
and the oracle estimators can be explicitly derived as

\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\frac{1}{272}\begin{bmatrix}47,&59,&71\end{bmatrix}^{\top},\quad\text{and}\quad\beta^{\star}=\frac{1}{4}\begin{bmatrix}1,&1,&1\end{bmatrix}^{\top},

where mean squared difference
\mathfrak{D}\left({\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}},{\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}}\right)=(\mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}X^{(1)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}}}\right])^{2}
is used in (
5
). At first glance, it is not evident how
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
and
\beta^{\star}
are related. For the reasons that become clear later in Section
4.1.1
, we remark that
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}(\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}}\right])=\frac{1}{12}\begin{bmatrix}7,&3,&-1\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
is parallel to the direction
\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
, i.e.,

\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\frac{1}{272}\begin{bmatrix}21,&9,&-3\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\propto v_{\textnormal{DIP}}\coloneqq\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}(\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}}\right]).

The direction
v_{\textnormal{DIP}}
can be accurately estimated with only unlabeled source and target data. Therefore, fine-tuning DIP
(1)
in the one-dimensional subspace can recover the oracle estimator with a constant number of labeled target samples, which substantially reduces the sample complexity bound compared to the target-only methods.

This example demonstrates that under additive interventions on the mean, DIP
(1)
can be an effective UDA starting point for fine-tuning, which settles the shared subspace from source and unlabeled target data and leaves only one direction to fine-tune so that
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}=\spn(v_{\textnormal{DIP}})
. A generalization and its finite-sample guarantees are explained in Section
4.1
.

4
New SSDA methods and excess risk guarantees under linear SCMs

In this section, we demonstrate that, by carefully leveraging both source and unlabeled target data, one can achieve a target risk that improves upon the minimax risk of target-only methods. We analyze three distributional shift scenarios, and propose tailor-made fine-tuning methods to tackle these scenarios. For each method, we derive its excess risk bounds and show that it is minimax-optimal (up to constant or logarithmic factors) under the minimax framework (
10
) given abundant source and unlabeled target data. Table
1
provides a summary of our main results.
