Table 1:
Summary of distribution shift scenarios, our proposed SSDA methods, and the minimax excess risk rates. Each row corresponds to a different distributional shift setting. The last column shows the minimax lower bound on the target excess risk; entry marked with
*
indicates the lower bound without access to unlabeled target data (see Section
4.3.1
). In each case, the target risk upper bound, up to constant or logarithmic factors, is achieved by our proposed SSDA method in the second column given access to datasets in the third column.

To simplify our following finite-sample risk analysis, we impose regularity assumptions on the random variables and parameters involved in data generation.

Assumption 2
.

In Assumption
1
, for all
m\geq 0
, the noise terms
\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}
and
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}
are zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors and zero-mean sub-Gaussian random variables with parameters
\sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{(m)}
and
\sigma_{\epsilon_{Y}}^{(m)}
, respectively. Furthermore, the covariance matrices
\Sigma_{X}^{(m)}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}X^{(m)}{}^{\top}}\right]
are invertible for all
m\geq 0
.

Before proceeding with our main theoretical results, we briefly introduce additional notation used in this section. Under Assumption
2
, the covariates
X^{(m)}
are centered sub-Gaussian random vectors, and we denote their sub-Gaussian parameter by
\sigma_{X}
uniformly across all
m\geq 0
(c.f. see Lemma
A.3
). Similarly, we let
\sigma_{Y}
denote the sub-Gaussian parameter of
Y^{(m)}
across all
m\geq 0
; when Assumption
2
holds, this equals
\max_{m\geq 0}\sigma_{\epsilon_{Y}}^{(m)}
.

We denote by
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}
, respectively, the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{X}^{(m)}
across all
m\geq 0
, and define the associated condition number
\overline{\kappa}\coloneqq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}/\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}
. We also define another condition number
{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\coloneqq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}^{2}
which depends on
\sigma_{X}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}
. Although we make the dependency on these parameters explicit in the upper bounds of our main theorems, to simplify all rate discussions, one may consider all these parameters to be of constant order. We refer the reader to Table
2
in the Appendix for further details.

4.1
Low-dimensional and confounded additive interventions under linear SCMs

We begin with a distribution shift scenario in which interventions are additive, arise due to unobserved confounding, and are restricted to a low-dimensional subspace. We refer to it as
confounded additive (CA) shift interventions
following
[
49
,
54
]
. In this setting, we demonstrate that fine-tuning starting from DIP can outperform target-only methods by constraining empirical risk minimization to a subspace estimated from source and unlabeled target data.

Assumption 3

(Confounded additive (CA) shift interventions)
.

In Assumption
1
, the first source and target domains share the same connectivity matrices and the anticausal weights, i.e.,
B\coloneqq B^{(1)}=B^{{(0)}}
and
b\coloneqq b^{(1)}=b^{{(0)}}
. The noise distributions are shifted as follows: in source,
\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{X}}
and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{Y}}
; while in target,
(\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y})
are additively intervened in the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional subspace (
r_{\textsf{ca}}<d
), i.e., for
\xi_{X}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{X}}
and
\xi_{Y}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{Y}}
,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}=WZ+\xi_{X},\;\;\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}=w_{Y}^{\top}Z+\xi_{Y},

where
Z
is a
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional centered random vector with covariance
\mathbf{I}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}
that is independent of
\xi_{X},\xi_{Y}
, and
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
and
w_{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{ca}}}
are fixed. In words, there is a shared hidden confounder
Z
that affects both distributions of
X
and
Y
. For simplicity, we assume that
W
has rank
r_{\textsf{ca}}
and that
b
is not parallel to any column of
W
.

Assumption
3
extends the deterministic additive mean shift setting considered in Example 4 of Section
3.2
. Before introducing our SSDA method, we first discuss how UDA estimators can handle this distributional shift. For mean shift interventions in Example 4, DIP with mean matching penalty is known to achieve low target risk
[
11
]
. However, under Assumption
3
of confounded additive shift interventions, the mean matching penalty no longer provides any meaningful constraint to align source and target covariates due to the centering of covariates.

As a result, we consider a DIP variant that incorporates second-moment matching, where the difference in covariance matrices is used as a distributional divergence, i.e.,

\mathfrak{D}\left({\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}},{\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}_{{\beta^{\top}X}}}\right)=\left(\cov(\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})-\cov(\beta^{\top}X^{(1)})\right)^{2}=\left(\beta^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})\beta\right)^{2}.

(12)

Observe that under Assumption
3
, the matrix
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
positive semi-definite and is of rank
r_{\textsf{ca}}
, so the covariance matching penalty encourages the prediction model to use covariates in the remaining
(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})
-dimensional subspace.

The corresponding DIP is defined as follows. Let
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
denote the matrix of eigenvectors corresponding to the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
nonzero eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
.

•

DIP
(1)
-cov
: the population DIP
(1)
with covariance matching penalty is

\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y^{(1)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(1)}\right)^{2}}\right]\text{ s.t. }\ \ \left\|\Pi_{V}\beta\right\|_{2}=0,

(13)

where
\Pi_{V}
is the projection operator onto
\textnormal{col}({V})
. Since
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
is positive semi-definite, the constraint
\left\|\Pi_{V}\beta\right\|_{2}=0
is equivalent to
\left(\beta^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})\beta\right)^{2}=0
.

•

DIP
(1)
-cov in finite-sample:
the finite-sample DIP
(1)
-cov based on the first source (labeled and unlabeled) datasets
\mathcal{D}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
, and the unlabeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
, is

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\frac{1}{n^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(1)}}\left(Y^{(1)}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{(1)}_{i}\right)^{2}\text{ s.t. }\ \ \left\|\Pi_{\widehat{V}}\beta\right\|_{2}=0,

(14)

where
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
is an estimate of
V
formed by the top
r_{\textsf{ca}}
eigenvectors (in absolute value) of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
, where
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}},\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
are the empirical covariance matrices from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
, respectively.

The difference between the finite-sample estimator and its population counterpart is expected to be small when the source and unlabeled target sample sizes are large. To quantify it, we provide a finite-sample error analysis of DIP
(1)
-cov under CA shift interventions in Appendix
C.2
.

Remark
.

In general, the covariance matching penalty cannot be written in the form given in (
13
) with the projection operator
\Pi_{V}
. This formulation is specific to the CA shift setting with a common hidden confounder which makes
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
positive semi-definite.

4.1.1
Fine-tuning method starting from DIP

In SSDA, with access to limited labeled target samples, we can fine-tune from DIP
(1)
-cov to achieve lower excess risk in the target domain. The intuition is that DIP
(1)
-cov already identifies an empirical risk minimizer constrained in a
(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})
-dimensional common subspace, then it suffices to fine-tune in an appropriate
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional subspace for further adaptation to the target.

Specifically, let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})}
denote an orthonormal basis for the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({V})
. Under the CA shift intervention in Assumption
3
, we can arrive at the following sequence of equalities:

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}

\displaystyle=Q(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]

(15)

\displaystyle=Q(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\beta^{\star}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V(V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V)^{-1}V^{\top}\beta^{\star}.

The first equality follows directly from solving the constrained minimization problem (
13
) in closed form. The second equality holds because under CA shift, the source and target have matching second moments for both covariates and cross-moments of
X
and
Y
on the common subspace
\spn(Q)
. The last equality follows from a standard identity for projection operators. We provide additional details of this derivation in Appendix
B.1
.

Equation (
15
) shows that the difference between DIP
(1)
-cov and the oracle estimator is constrained to the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional subspace spanned by the columns of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V
. This suggests a natural fine-tuning strategy for SSDA: fine-tune DIP
(1)
-cov in the low-dimensional subspace
\textnormal{col}({\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V})
. When
r_{\textsf{ca}}\ll d
, we can expect that only
\mathcal{O}\left({r_{\textsf{ca}}}\right)
labeled target samples are needed to match the target performance of the oracle.

To formalize this approach in finite-sample, let
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})}
be an orthonormal basis for the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({\widehat{V}})
.

•

FT-DIP
(1)
: the fine-tuning estimator under CA shift interventions initiated from DIP
(1)
-cov based on the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
is

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta:\lVert{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta}\rVert_{2}\leq\varrho}\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\left(Y^{{(0)}}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}}_{i}\right)^{2}\text{ s.t. }\left\|\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}(\beta-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}})\right\|_{2}=0,

(16)

where
\varrho>0
is a regularization parameter to be chosen.

At the population level, the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}})=0
is satisfied due to (
15
). FT-DIP
(1)
replaces the population constraint with its empirical counterpart, and effectively seeks a solution in the subspace spanned by the columns of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}
. Since
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}
and
\widehat{V}
can be estimated from unlabeled source and target data, FT-DIP
(1)
is a two-stage SSDA method which takes advantage of both labeled and unlabeled data across source and target domains.

4.1.2
Finite-sample risk guarantees of FT-DIP

Under CA shift interventions, our first main theorem establishes a finite-sample target excess risk upper bound for FT-DIP
(1)
. To quantify this bound, we introduce a quantity that determines the eigen-gap condition for
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
. Writing
A_{\textnormal{perb}}=(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)^{\top}
, we define

\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}\coloneqq\lambda_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(A_{\textnormal{perb}})\cdot\lambda_{\rm min}((\mathbf{I}-B)^{-\top}(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}),

(17)

where
\lambda_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(A_{\textnormal{perb}})
denotes the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-th largest eigenvalue of
A_{\textnormal{perb}}
(i.e., the smallest nonzero eigenvalue under rank-
r_{\textsf{ca}}
), and
\lambda_{\rm min}((\mathbf{I}-B)^{-\top}(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1})
is the minimum eigenvalue of
(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-\top}(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}
. Note that the quantity
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
increases when (i) the columns of
W
become more linearly independent, (ii) the vector
b
becomes more linearly independent of the column space of
W
, or (iii) the magnitudes of
W
,
b
, and
w_{Y}
become larger.

To formalize the dependence of excess risk upper bound on this eigen-gap, we define the condition number
{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\coloneqq\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}^{2}
, and for any
\delta>0
, define the shorthand
d_{\delta}\coloneqq d+\log(1/\delta)
. We are ready to state the theorem.

Theorem 4.1

(FT-DIP excess risk upper bound in CA shift)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, there exist universal constants
c,c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/10)
, if
n^{(0)}\geq r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)
,
n^{(1)}\geq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d\log(1/\delta),n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}\geq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}d_{\delta},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq c_{1}\max\{1,{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2},{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}\}d_{\delta}
,
and if we choose
\varrho\geq c_{2}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{2}+\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)
in (
16
), then with probability at least
1-10\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}})\leq c\eta{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{n^{(0)}}}\log(1/\delta)+C\left[\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}+{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right],

where the randomness is over
\mathcal{D}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
, and
\eta=\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left[\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right]^{2}\overline{\kappa}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}
, and
C=\mathcal{O}\left({\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\lVert{\beta^{\star}}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}\rVert_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{poly}(\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}},{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})}\right)
.

The proof of Theorem
4.1
is given in Appendix
E.1
. Theorem
4.1
shows that the excess risk of FT-DIP
(1)
is upper bounded by two dominant terms. The first term corresponds to the finite-sample error that arises from fine-tuning in the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional subspace with
n^{(0)}
labeled target samples. The second term accounts for the error from estimating DIP
(1)
-cov based on labeled source samples and unlabeled source and target samples, as well as the error from estimating the subspace
\textnormal{col}({\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V})
. Overall, FT-DIP
(1)
achieves small excess risk with
n^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}\left({r_{\textsf{ca}}}\right)
, provided that
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\gg d
, whereas target-only methods typically require
n^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}\left({d}\right)
as shown in Proposition
3.1
.

To characterize the tightness of the above upper bound, we provide a lower bound on the minimax excess risk rate in CA shift interventions.

Theorem 4.2

(Minimax excess risk lower bound in CA shift)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
denote the class of joint distributions over
\mathcal{D}^{(m)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}
,
m=0,1
, where each source and target (labeled and unlabeled) sample is generated according to the linear SCMs with CA shift interventions in Assumptions
3
. For any
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}>0
, the minimax excess risk (
10
) over
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies

\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq c\sigma_{Y}^{2}\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{n^{(0)}},

(18)

where
c>0
is a universal constant.

The proof of Theorem
4.2
is given in Appendix
F.2
. The lower bound is constructed under the regime where all condition numbers—
\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}},{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}
—as well as the magnitudes of the population parameters
\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2},\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{2},\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}\rVert_{2}
are of constant order, and
\sigma_{X}
is a constant multiple of
\sigma_{Y}
(i.e.,
X
and
Y
are on the same scale). Under this setting, since
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\leq\sigma_{X}^{2}
, the quantity
\eta
in Theorem
4.1
scales with
\eta=\mathcal{O}\left({\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\right)
. In particular, Theorem
4.2
establishes that FT-DIP
(1)
achieves the minimax rate up to constant factors as long as
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\to\infty
which corresponds to the regime of primary interest in SSDA. In other words, given a large amount of source and unlabeled target data, no other estimator can perform substantially better than FT-DIP
(1)
under CA shift interventions.

Remark
.

If there is no confounder affecting the distributions of
X
and
Y
in Assumption
3
, i.e.,
w_{Y}=0
and additive shift only exists in the covariates, the excess risk upper bound of FT-DIP
(1)
in Theorem
4.1
would remain valid, however it would no longer be minimax optimal. This is because
\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]
when
w_{Y}=0
, yielding
\beta^{\star}=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]
. Consequently, a naive plug-in estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{naive}}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{n^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(1)}}X^{(1)}_{i}Y^{(1)}_{i}\right]
without any labeled target samples suffices to achieve a good estimate of the oracle estimator. Nevertheless, when there is confounder with
w_{Y}\neq 0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]
and the naive estimator is no longer consistent.

4.2
Sparse connectivity shift interventions under linear SCMs

Next, we consider a distribution shift scenario where the causal connections among covariates change between source and target domains.
We call this type of intervention as
sparse connectivity (SC) shift interventions
.

Assumption 4

(Sparse connectivity (SC) shift interventions)
.

In Assumption
1
, the first source and target domains share the same anticausal weights and the noise distributions, i.e.,
b\coloneqq b^{(1)}=b^{{(0)}}
, and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{X},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{X}}
and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{Y}}
are independently drawn from the same distributions, while the connectivity matrices
B^{(1)},B^{{(0)}}
differ. Specifically, the intervention modifies
B^{{(0)}}
in
r_{\textsf{sc}}
columns (
r_{\textsf{sc}}<d
) indexed by
S=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\}\subseteq[d]
, so that
B^{(1)}_{*S}\neq B^{{(0)}}_{*S}
, while the remaining columns remain unchanged,
B^{(1)}_{*S^{c}}=B^{{(0)}}_{*S^{c}}
.

Assumption
4
extends the intervention scenario considered in Example 3 of Section
3.2
, where only edges adjacent to the first covariate are intervened. Here, the intervention modifies the causal connections of
r_{\textsf{sc}}
covariates,
X_{[j_{1}]},X_{[j_{2}]},\ldots,X_{[j_{r_{\textsf{sc}}}]}
, with respect to the other covariates. This effect propagates to all descendants of the intervened covariates but does not affect non-descendants. In particular, since
Y
is an ancestor of all covariates in anticausal models, the marginal distribution of
Y
is unaffected.

To see how SC shift interventions partially affect the population OLS estimators, we define the shared parameters
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\coloneqq\var\left({Y^{(1)}{}}\right)=\var\left({Y^{{(0)}}{}}\right)
and
\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}{}^{\top}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}{}^{\top}}\right]
across the source and target domains. Applying the Sherman–Morrison formula to the explicit forms of
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}},\beta^{\star}
, we can deduce (see Appendix
B.2
for further details)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}=\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b}(\mathbf{I}-B^{(1)}{}^{\top})\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b,\\
\beta^{\star}=\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}{}^{\top})\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b.\end{cases}

(19)

Inspecting equations in (
19
), we see that since
B^{{(0)}}-B^{(1)}
differs only in the
r_{\textsf{sc}}
columns indexed by
S
, the difference between
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
and
\beta^{\star}
is sparse with support
S
. This observation leads to a new fine-tuning strategy based on sparsity constraint.

4.2.1
Fine-tuning OLS-Src with finite-sample guarantees

The sparsity structure between OLS-Src
(1)
and the oracle estimator highlights that only the coefficients of OLS-Src
(1)
corresponding to the intervened covariates need to be adjusted to improve target prediction performance over UDA methods. To formulate our fine-tuning method under SC shift interventions, we first introduce a finite-sample version of OLS-Src
(1)
.

•

OLS-Src
(1)
in finite-sample
: the finite-sample version of OLS-Src
(1)
based on the first labeled source dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
is

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \frac{1}{n^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(1)}}\left(Y^{(1)}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{(1)}_{i}\right)^{2}.

(20)

Now we introduce FT-OLS-Src
(1)
, a fine-tuning method that adapts OLS-Src
(1)
to the target domain via empirical risk minimization with the
\ell_{1}
norm constraint.

•

FT-OLS-Src
(1)
: the fine-tuning estimator under SC shift interventions initiated from OLS-Src
(1)
based on the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
is

\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta:\lVert{\beta}\rVert_{2}\leq\rho}\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\left(Y^{{(0)}}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}}_{i}\right)^{2}\text{ s.t. }\left\|\beta-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{1}\leq\gamma,

(21)

where
\gamma>0
controls the proximity to
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
in
\ell_{1}
norm, and
\rho>0
is a regularization parameter to be chosen.

We remark that a method with similar
\ell_{1}
norm regularization appeared in
[
35
]
, but their motivation is substantially different from ours. In particular, their method was proposed to tackle the assumed sparse changes in the parameters of interest. However, we don’t directly impose assumptions on the parameters of interests. The necessity of a sparse structure arises as a consequence of tackling a specific kind of interventions in data generation models. The point of view that a fine-tuning strategy should be tailor-made for underlying assumptions on the type of interventions that cause distribution shifts is a common theme across all our SSDA methods.

In the following theorem, we provide an excess risk upper bound of FT-OLS-Src
(1)
(recall the shorthand
d_{\delta}\coloneqq d+\log(1/\delta)
).

Theorem 4.3

(FT-OLS-Src excess risk upper bound in SC shift)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
, there exist universal constants
c,c_{1}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/5)
and
d\geq 3
, if
n^{(0)}\geq\log d+\log(1/\delta)
and
n^{(1)}\geq c_{1}\max\left\{d_{\delta}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2},d\log(1/\delta)\right\}
,
and if we choose
\rho\geq\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}
and
\gamma=\lVert{\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{1}
in (
21
), with probability at least
1-5\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}}})\leq c\eta\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}\left(\log d+\log(1/\delta)\right)}{n^{(0)}}\log(1/\delta)+C\max\left\{\frac{{d\log d}}{\sqrt{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}},\frac{d^{2}\log d}{{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}}\right\},

where the randomness is over
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
,
\eta=\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\rho+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}
, and
C=\mathcal{O}\left({\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\lVert{\beta^{\star}}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{poly}(\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}})\cdot\log^{2}(1/\delta)}\right)
.

The proof of Theorem
4.3
is given in Appendix
E.2
. In Theorem
4.3
, the first term of order
\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}\log d}{n^{(0)}}
is attributed to fine-tuning on labeled target samples subject to the
\ell_{1}
norm constraint, while the second term is due to the finite-sample error arising from estimating
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
using source data. We observe that the excess risk becomes small when
n^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}\left({r_{\textsf{sc}}}\right)
, given
n^{(0)}n^{(1)}\gg d^{2}
. In the regime where
r_{\textsf{sc}}\ll d
, it suffices to have
n^{(1)}\gtrsim d^{2}/r_{\textsf{sc}}
and
n^{(0)}\gtrsim r_{\textsf{sc}}
to guarantee nontrivial excess risk. This improves upon the order
d
labeled target samples needed for the target-only methods in Proposition
3.1
.

Remark
.

Vanilla fine-tuning in the literature initializes with a pretrained model and applies gradient descent to all or a subset of the parameters, often with early stopping. It is well-known that for the least squares loss with linear prediction model, gradient descent with early stopping is equivalent to imposing an
\ell_{2}
norm regularization
[
22
]
. In contrast, FT-OLS-Src
(1)
employs
\ell_{1}
norm regularization, and its performance can be significantly different depending on the assumptions about the data generation process. While a theoretical comparison of the two approaches is beyond the scope of this work, in Simulation 2 of Section
6
, we empirically demonstrate that in SC shift interventions, there exist data generation scenarios where FT-OLS-Src
(1)
substantially outperforms
\ell_{2}
-based fine-tuning.

To characterize the tightness of the upper bound of FT-OLS-Src
(1)
, we provide a lower bound of the minimax excess risk rate in SC shift interventions.

Theorem 4.4

(Minimax excess risk lower bound in SC shift)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
denote the class of joint distributions over
\mathcal{D}^{(m)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}
,
m=0,1
, where each source and target (labeled and unlabeled) sample is generated according to the linear SCMs with SC shift interventions in Assumption
4
. For any
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}>0
, the minimax excess risk (
10
) over
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies

\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq c\sigma_{Y}^{2}\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}}{n^{(0)}},

(22)

where
c>0
is a universal constant.

The proof of Theorem
4.4
is given in Appendix
F.3
. Analogous to the discussion following Theorem
4.2
, we focus on the regime where all condition numbers and the magnitudes of the population parameters—i.e.,
\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}},\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2},\lVert{\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}}\rVert_{2}
—are of constant order, and
\sigma_{X}
is proportional to
\sigma_{Y}
. In this setting, FT-OLS-Src
(1)
is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors as
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\to\infty
. Importantly, FT-OLS-Src
(1)
achieves this guarantee without making use of any unlabeled target data, indicating that unlabeled target data do not necessarily improve target prediction performance under SC shift when the source sample size
n^{(1)}
is sufficiently large.

4.3
Anticausal weight shift interventions under linear SCMs

The third distribution shift scenario we consider is induced by changes in the anticausal weight vector of the label
Y
. This occurs when the causal mechanism of how
Y
affects the covariates changes. We call this type of intervention
anticausal weight (AW) shift interventions
.

Assumption 5

(Anticausal weight (AW) shift interventions)
.

In Assumption
1
, all source and target domains share the same connectivity matrices and the noise distributions, i.e.,
B\coloneqq B^{(m)}
for all
m\geq 0
, and
\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{X}}
and
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{Y}}
are independently drawn from the same distributions across all
m\geq 0
. The intervention modifies the anticausal weight vector in a way that
b^{{(0)}}\neq b^{(m)}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, while

b^{{(0)}}-b^{(1)}\in\spn(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(M)}-b^{(1)}),

(23)

where
\dim(\spn(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(M)}-b^{(1)}))=r_{\textsf{aw}}
with
r_{\textsf{aw}}<d
. We further assume that
b^{(1)}\notin\spn(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(M)}-b^{(1)})
and
r_{\textsf{aw}}+1\leq M\leq d
.

Unlike Assumptions
3
and
4
in the previous subsections, Assumption
5
considers multiple source domains instead of a single one. Condition (
23
) asserts that the shift in
b^{{(0)}}
from
b^{(1)}
lies in the span of the perturbation directions observed in the source domains.
This condition is more likely to hold when the source domains are sufficiently heterogeneous. It also guarantees the existence of the
(d-r_{\textsf{aw}})
-dimensional subspace orthogonal to
\spn((\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}(b^{(m)}-b^{(1)}),m=2,\ldots,M)
, such that the conditional distribution
X\mid Y
of the covariates projected onto this subspace, given the label
Y
, is invariant across all source and target domains
[
11
]
. These projected covariates represent the conditionally invariant components (CICs), which play an important role in domain adaptation
[
18
,
24
,
62
]
.

In AW shift interventions, since the interventions are label-dependent, DIP-type methods can fail drastically. This failure has been discovered and discussed extensively in prior work (e.g.,
[
28
,
69
,
62
]
), and we provide a concrete example in Section
I
. On the other hand, CIP with conditional mean matching penalty can identify the CICs given many heterogeneous source domains and can provide guarantees on the target population risk
[
62
]
. It is formulated as follows.

•

CIP-mean
: the population CIP estimator with conditional mean matching penalty is

\begin{split}&\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\argmin_{\beta}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y^{(m)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)}\right)^{2}}\right],\\
&\hskip 7.22743pt\text{s.t. }\ \ \mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}X^{(m)}}\ \middle|\ {Y^{(m)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}{X^{(m-1)}}}\ \middle|\ {{Y^{(m-1)}}}\right]\text{ a.s. for all $m\geq 2$}.\end{split}

(24)

Observe that under Assumption
5
, the conditional expectation is
\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}}\ \middle|\ {Y^{(m)}}\right]=(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(m)}Y^{(m)}
. If we write
V_{\textsf{aw}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{aw}}}
to denote an orthonormal basis of the
r_{\textsf{aw}}
-dimensional subspace
\spn\{(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}(b^{(m)}-b^{(m-1)}),\,m\geq 2\}
, the conditional mean matching penalty then constrains
\beta
to lie in the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({V_{\textsf{aw}}})
, i.e.,
\lVert{\Pi_{V_{\textsf{aw}}}\beta}\rVert_{2}=0
where
\Pi_{V_{\textsf{aw}}}
is the projection operator onto
\textnormal{col}({V_{\textsf{aw}}})
. We use this equivalent formulation of the conditional mean matching penalty in the finite-sample version of CIP.

Specifically, for each
m\geq 1
, let
\widehat{(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(m)}}
be the finite-sample estimate of
(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(m)}
by regressing
X^{(m)}
onto
Y^{(m)}
(see Appendix
C.3
for details). And let
\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{aw}}}
denote the matrix of left-singular vectors corresponding to the
r_{\textsf{aw}}
dominant directions of the subspace
\spn\{\widehat{(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(m)}}-\widehat{(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(m-1)}},\,m\geq 2\}
.

•

CIP-mean in finite-sample:
the finite-sample CIP-mean based on multiple source datasets
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, is

\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\argmin_{\beta}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}{\left(Y^{(m)}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)}_{i}\right)^{2}}\text{ s.t. }\ \ \left\|\Pi_{\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}}\beta\right\|_{2}=0,

(25)

where the constraint
\lVert{\Pi_{\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}}\beta}\rVert_{2}=0
represents the finite-sample version of the population-level conditional mean matching penalty.

When the source sample sizes are sufficiently large, the difference between the population and finite-sample CIP-mean estimators is expected to be small. To quantify the error, we provide a finite-sample analysis of CIP-mean under AW shift interventions in Appendix
C.3
.

4.3.1
Fine-tuning method starting from CIP

We now introduce a method that fine-tunes CIP-mean to improve target prediction performance in SSDA. Since CIP already finds an empirical risk minimizer based on
(d-r_{\textsf{aw}})
-dimensional conditionally invariant features, our strategy is to fine-tune in the remaining
r_{\textsf{aw}}
directions with additional target labels.

Recall that the conditional mean matching penalty in (
24
) looks for
\beta
which is orthogonal to
V_{\textsf{aw}}
. This constraint resembles the DIP penalty in (
14
) with
V_{\textsf{aw}}
in place of
V
. Following the same reasoning used in (
15
), we can arrive at the equation (see Appendix
B.3
for detailed derivations)

\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\beta^{\star}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}(V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\beta^{\star}\ \Longrightarrow{Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}})}=0,

(26)

where
Q_{\textsf{aw}}
is the orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({V_{\textsf{aw}}})
. This equation leads to the fine-tuning method analogous to FT-DIP (
16
) based on the empirical version of the constraint
{Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}})}=0
.

While the equation (
26
) involves the target covariance matrix which can be accurately estimated with large unlabeled target data in the SSDA setting (we refer to this method as FT-CIP-Tar in Section
6
), in Assumption
5
with data from multiple source domains, a key advantage is that we can find the relevant subspace directions without using any target data. To see this, in AW shift interventions, simple calculations can verify that (without loss of generality, the first source domain is used)

\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}=\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(1)}\ ;&\ V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\end{bmatrix}\cdot S,

(27)

for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
and
S\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times d}
(see Appendix
B.3
for details). Substituting this into (
26
), rearranging terms, and multiplying both sides by
\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
, we can deduce

\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}(\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta^{\star})=-\left(\mathbf{I}-\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(1)}\ ;&\ V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\end{bmatrix}\cdot S\right)V_{\textsf{aw}}(V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\beta^{\star}.

Now let
Q_{\textsf{aug}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r_{\textsf{aw}}-1)}
be the orthonormal matrix whose columns are orthogonal to both
V_{\textsf{aw}}
and
(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(1)}
. Then it follows

Q_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}(\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta^{\star})=0.

(28)

Comparing with (
26
), we can see that without the target covariance matrix, the difference
\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta^{\star}
now lives in the
(r_{\textsf{aw}}+1)
-dimensional subspace rather than the
r_{\textsf{aw}}
-dimensional subspace. Therefore, fine-tuning must also be performed in the corresponding
(r_{\textsf{aw}}+1)
-dimensional subspace. The extra dimension reflects the price we pay for not using unlabeled target data. In the remainder of this section, we thus focus on this setting—where unlabeled target data is not available, but large source data (both labeled and unlabeled) and a small set of labeled target samples are given.

To formally introduce our fine-tuning method under this setup, let
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}
be the orthonormal matrix that is orthogonal to both
\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}
and
\widehat{(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}b^{(1)}}
, and let
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
denote the empirical covariance matrix from the first unlabeled source data
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
.

•

FT-CIP
: the fine-tuning estimator under AW shift interventions initiated from CIP-mean based on the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
is

\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}=\argmin_{\beta:\lVert{\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}\beta}\rVert_{2}\leq\varrho}\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\left(Y^{{(0)}}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}}_{i}\right)^{2}\text{ s.t. }\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}(\beta-\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}})\right\|_{2}=0,

(29)

where
\varrho>0
is a regularization parameter to be chosen.

Observe that both FT-CIP (
29
) and FT-DIP
(1)
(
16
) are formulated as empirical risk minimizer constrained on a low-dimensional linear subspace. The only difference lies in how the subspace is formed. Since all quantities
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
,
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}
,
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}
can be estimated from multiple source datasets, FT-CIP provides an effective two-stage SSDA method which does not need any unlabeled target data.

4.3.2
Finite-sample risk guarantees of FT-CIP

Our theoretical results establish the optimality of FT-CIP under AW shift interventions when there is no access to large unlabeled target dataset. The upper bound on the excess risk of FT-CIP involves a parameter that plays a role similar to the eigen-gap
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
(
17
) for FT-DIP. Specifically, define the matrix

{P}_{\textsf{aug}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{I}-B\right)^{-1}b^{(1)},\left(\mathbf{I}-B\right)^{-1}\left(b^{(2)}-b^{(1)}\right),\cdots,\left(\mathbf{I}-B\right)^{-1}\left(b^{(M)}-b^{(M-1)}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

(30)

Under Assumption
5
,
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
has rank
r_{\textsf{aw}}+1
, and
Q_{\textsf{aug}}
spans the subspace orthogonal to the (left) singular space of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
. Then we define
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
as the smallest nonzero singular value of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
, i.e.,

{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}\coloneqq\min_{i:\sigma_{i}({P}_{\textsf{aug}})\neq 0}\sigma_{i}({P}_{\textsf{aug}}),

(31)

where
\sigma_{i}({P}_{\textsf{aug}})
denotes the
i
-th largest singular value of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
. Note that
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
measures how well the nonzero singular values of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
are separated from zero. It increases when the perturbation directions
\left(\mathbf{I}-B\right)^{-1}(b^{(m)}-b^{(m-1)})
are more diverse across source domains, when these directions are more linearly independent of the source direction
\left(\mathbf{I}-B\right)^{-1}b^{(1)}
, and when all these directions have larger magnitudes. In particular, a larger
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
improves the identifiability of the singular space of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
, which leads to tighter control and improved risk bounds for FT-CIP.

To formally state the excess risk bound of FT-CIP, let
\nu_{Y}^{2}\coloneqq\var\left({Y^{(m)}}\right)
denote the variance of
Y^{(m)}
(which is common across all source domains under AW shift), and let
\overline{n}\coloneqq\min_{m=1,\ldots,M}n^{(m)}
denote the minimum source sample size across all source domains. We define the condition number
{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\coloneqq\frac{\sigma_{X}}{{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}\nu_{Y}}
that involves
\nu_{Y}
and
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
. Recalling the shorthand
d_{\delta}\coloneqq d+\log(1/\delta)
, here is the main theorem regarding FT-CIP.

Theorem 4.5

(FT-CIP excess risk upper bound in AW shift)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
5
, there exist universal constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/(c_{1}M))
, if
n^{(0)}\geq r_{\textsf{aw}}\log(1/\delta)
,
\overline{n}\geq c_{2}\max\left\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d\log(1/\delta),\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)\right\}
,
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}\geq c_{2}\max\{1,{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\}d_{\delta}
, and if we choose
\varrho\geq c_{3}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\rVert_{2}+\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)
, with probability at least
1-c_{1}M\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}}})\leq c\eta\frac{r_{\textsf{aw}}}{n^{(0)}}\log(1/\delta)+C\left[{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}+{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}\right],

where the randomness is over
\{\mathcal{D}^{(m)}\}_{m=0}^{M},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
,
\eta=\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left[\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right]^{2}\overline{\kappa}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}
, and
C=\mathcal{O}\left({\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\lVert{\beta^{\star}}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}}\rVert_{2}+\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\rVert_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{poly}(\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}},{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}})\cdot\log(1/\delta)}\right)
.
1
1

1

\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}
denotes the pooled estimator trained on the pooled set of source datasets. See Section
C.1
for its formal definition and the finite-sample guarantee.

The proof of this theorem is given in Appendix
E.3
. Theorem
4.5
shows that for
M=r_{\textsf{aw}}+1
, by choosing
\delta=\mathcal{O}\left({1/r_{\textsf{aw}}}\right)
, FT-CIP achieves small excess risk with high probability as long as
n^{(0)}\gtrsim r_{\textsf{aw}}\log r_{\textsf{aw}}
and the source sample sizes satisfy
\overline{n},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}\gg d
.
We remark that unlike Theorem
4.1
which requires unlabeled target data
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
for FT-DIP to find a good subspace to fine-tune, Theorem
4.5
shows that FT-CIP does not need unlabeled target data.

To characterize the tightness of the above upper bound, we provide a lower bound of the minimax excess risk rate in AW shift interventions.

Theorem 4.6

(Minimax excess risk lower bound in AW shift)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
denote the class of joint distributions over
\{\mathcal{D}^{(m)}\}_{m=0}^{M},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
where each source and target (labeled and unlabeled) sample is generated according to the linear SCMs with AW shift interventions in Assumption
5
. For any
n^{(1)},\ldots,n^{(M)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},\ldots,n^{(M)}_{\textnormal{u}}>0
, the minimax excess risk (
10
) over
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies

\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq c\sigma_{Y}^{2}\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{n^{(0)}},

(32)

where
c>0
is a universal constant.

The proof of Theorem
4.6
is provided in Appendix
F.4
. In the setting where all condition numbers—
\overline{\kappa},{\kappa}_{\text{min}},{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}
—and the magnitudes of the population parameters,
\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2},\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}}\rVert_{2},\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\rVert_{2}
, are of constant order, and
X
and
Y
are on the same scale (
\sigma_{X}\approx\sigma_{Y}
), Theorem
4.6
shows that FT-CIP is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors as
n^{(m)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}\to\infty
for all
m\geq 1
. It is important to note that this minimax lower bound result assumes no access to unlabeled target data—under this scenario, no other estimator can substantially outperform FT-CIP in AW shift interventions. However, FT-CIP is no longer minimax-optimal if large unlabeled target data is accessible, since the unlabeled source and target data together can reveal information about the parameter of interest in the AW shift setting.

5
Selecting from multiple fine-tuning strategies in SSDA

Our main results in the previous section require precise formulation of distributional shifts in order for the proposed SSDA methods to achieve minimax optimal rates. In many practical scenarios, the relationship between the source and target is only vaguely known. Consequently, in fine-tuning starting from UDA estimators (
11
), it becomes unclear a priori which choice of the UDA estimator
\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}
and the target-specific subset
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}
yields the best target prediction performance. In this section, we introduce
multi adaptive-start fine-tuning (MASFT)
, a fine-tuning strategy that combines multiple fine-tuning methods with model selection. The main intuition is that model selection from a few candidate fine-tuning methods can be done with a small validation set. This approach reduces dependence on any single assumption about the source-target relationship and allows us to explore a broader range of types of distributional shifts.

Specifically, we introduce an additional hold-out validation dataset from the target domain, which is sampled
i.i.d.
from
\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}

\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}\coloneqq\{(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i},\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}^{{(0)}}.

MASFT algorithm
: The MASFT algorithm is implemented in three steps.

1)

Train initial UDA estimators using the multiple source and unlabeled target datasets
(\{\mathcal{D}^{(m)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)})
. Denote these UDA estimators as
\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{UDA},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{UDA},{L}}\}
.

2)

Fine-tune each UDA estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{UDA},{k}}
,
k=1,\ldots,L
, on the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
by performing the minimization (
11
) where we set
\theta_{\textnormal{UDA}}=\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{UDA},{k}}
and
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo}}=\Theta_{\textnormal{lo},{k}}
. Here
\Theta_{\textnormal{lo},{k}}
represents a subset of the parameter space tailored for each initialized UDA estimator. The resulting fine-tuned estimators are denoted by
\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}
.

3)

Evaluate each fine-tuned estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{k}}
,
k=1,\ldots,L
, on the hold-out validation dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}
by computing its empirical target risk, then select the estimator with the lowest validation risk as the final model:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widehat{k}=\argmin_{k\in\{1,\ldots,L\}}\frac{1}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\ell(f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{k}}}(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}),\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i}),\\
\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}=\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},\widehat{k}}.\end{cases}

(33)

MASFT initializes with multiple UDA estimators and refines them through a tailor-made fine-tuning process. As long as at least one of these fine-tuning succeeds, e.g. due to a correct specification of the source-target relationship, MASFT can output a final model with good target performance without substantially increasing the labeled target sample complexity. The excess risk bound of MASFT is given in Section
D
, which follows from standard model selection results.

In order to apply MASFT to the three distributional shift scenarios considered in Section
4
, we define FT-DIP
(m)
and FT-OLS-Src
(m)
analogously to FT-DIP
(1)
(
16
) and FT-OLS-Src
(1)
(
21
) applied to the
m
-th source domain. Then for each source domain
m=1,\ldots,M
, we run both FT-DIP
(m)
and FT-OLS-Src
(m)
, generating
2M
candidate fine-tuned estimators. Additionally, we run FT-CIP combining all source domains. Overall, this results in a total of
L=2M+1
candidate estimators, denoted
\{\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}
. MASFT then selects the estimator with the lowest empirical risk on the validation set as the final model
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{final}}
. The following corollary provides the performance guarantee of MASFT.

Corollary 1
.

Suppose that at least one of the following conditions hold. (1) There exists at least one source domain
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
that satisfies either the CA shift interventions (Assumption
3
), or the SC shift interventions (Assumption
4
), (2) The source and target domains satisfy the AW shift interventions (Assumption
5
). Then under the conditions of Theorems
4.1
,
4.3
,
4.5
, there exist universal constants
c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/(cM))
, if
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}\geq c^{\prime}\log(M/\delta)
, with probability at least
1-cM\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{final}}
satisfies

\delta\mathcal{R}(\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{final}})\leq c^{\prime\prime}\eta\sqrt{\frac{\log(M/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}+\epsilon_{\text{type}},

where
\eta=\sigma_{X}^{2}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\max_{j=1,\ldots,2M+1}\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{FT},{j}}\right\|_{2}\right)^{2}
, and
\epsilon_{\text{type}}
depends on which assumption holds:

•

If CA shift holds, then
\epsilon_{\text{type}}=\epsilon_{\text{ca}}
;

•

If SC shift holds, then
\epsilon_{\text{type}}=\epsilon_{\text{sc}}
;

•

If AW shift holds, then
\epsilon_{\text{type}}=\epsilon_{\text{aw}}
,

where
\epsilon_{\text{ca}},\epsilon_{\text{sc}},\epsilon_{\text{aw}}>0
are the excess risk upper bounds given in Theorem
4.1
, Theorem
4.3
, and Theorem
4.5
, respectively.

Corollary
1
follows directly from the model selection results (c.f. Proposition
D.1
) combined with the individual excess risk bounds in Theorem
4.1
, Theorem
4.3
, and Theorem
4.5
, so the proof is omitted. The result shows that the excess risk of the model returned by MASFT is no worse than the best excess risk upper bound of the fine-tuned estimator under correctly specified intervention type, plus the additional term of order
\sqrt{\frac{\log M}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}
. Therefore, even when the source-target relationship is not precisely known, the validation sample size of
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}\gtrsim\log M
suffices to guarantee the low excess risk of MASFT while allowing for various types of interventions.

6
Numerical experiments

In this section, we investigate the target performance of our proposed SSDA methods through numerical simulations. The experiments are conducted on synthetic datasets generated according to anticausal linear SCMs in Assumptions
1
.

To compare with our SSDA methods, we implement several existing UDA and SSDA algorithms. For UDA methods, we include OLS-Src, DIP, CIP introduced in Section
2.2
. Additionally, we implement OLS-Pool, which runs OLS on the pooled source datasets (see Section
C.1
), and several widely used UDA methods such as groupDRO
[
51
]
, Invariant Risk Minimization (IRM)
[
1
]
, and V-REx
[
31
]
. For SSDA, we also evaluate a fine-tuning method with
\ell_{2}
norm penalty, denoted FT-OLS-Src-L2, which is closely related to the widely-used fine-tuning strategy using gradient descent with early stopping
[
22
]
. Both FT-OLS-Src with
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
norm penalties are implemented with regularized (rather than constrained) form of optimization. For FT-CIP, whenever unlabeled target data is available, we consider a variant that incorporates this unlabeled target data for fine-tuning (see the discussion in Section
4.3.1
for further details), rather than the formulation given in (
29
), and refer to this variant as FT-CIP-Tar. We also include OLS-Tar, which performs OLS on the labeled target samples only.

Algorithms such as FT-OLS-Src (both
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
norm penalties), groupDRO, IRM, V-REx require hyperparameters to be tuned. We tune these hyperparameters with a hold-out labeled validation dataset from the target domain. This validation dataset is also used to (1) select the best source domain for single-source SSDA methods such as FT-DIP and FT-OLS-Src, and (2) choose the best performing fine-tuning method for MASFT.

Simulation 1: CA shift interventions

Figure 3:
Comparison of target excess risk in Simulation 1 with CA shift interventions. The error bars represent the mean
\pm
one standard deviation over 20 trials. The y-axis is on a logarithmic scale.

We generate three datasets under CA shift interventions with
d=100
while varying the dimension of the unobserved confounders
r_{\textsf{ca}}
with
r_{\textsf{ca}}\in\{5,10,15\}
. We consider
M=4
source domains. For each
m=1,\ldots,4
, the matrix
B^{(m)}
is a lower triangular matrix with diagonal entries zeros and off-diagonal entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,9/d)
, and the vector
b^{(m)}
has entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,0.25/d)
. Each source domain is generated with sample size
n^{(m)}=n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}=6,000
.

For the target domain, we set
B^{{(0)}}=B^{(1)}
and
b^{{(0)}}=b^{(1)}
, and draw the entries of the matrix
W
and the vector
w_{Y}
i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,25/r_{\textsf{ca}})
. We generate
n^{(0)}=100
labeled target samples for training the SSDA methods and the OLS-Tar baseline method. The additional
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}=100
labeled samples are generated for hyperparameter tuning and for selecting the best fine-tuning method in MASFT. We also generate two independent sets of
50,000
labeled samples to calculate the oracle estimator
\beta^{\star}
and the test target excess risk, respectively. For methods that take advantage of unlabeled target data, we generate
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=10,000
unlabeled target samples.

The resulting target excess risks for various methods are shown in Figure
3
, with results averaged over 20 trials. Since OLS-Tar with
n^{(0)}=100
yields very high excess risk and makes it difficult to compare other methods visually, we display it separately in an inset box at the bottom left of the figure. Among our proposed SSDA methods, FT-DIP with the first source domain is selected by MASFT and is highlighted in red.
We additionally include the OLS-Tar-MoreData method (highlighted in green) which runs OLS on a larger labeled target dataset, where the sample size is chosen to match the theoretical bound of FT-DIP (ignoring constant factors), i.e.,
{d/}{n_{\textsf{new}}^{(0)}}={r_{\textsf{ca}}/}{n^{(0)}}
. As shown in Figure
3
, under CA shift interventions, FT-DIP outperforms other UDA and SSDA methods, including OLS-Tar, and achieves performance comparable to OLS-Tar-MoreData. The original DIP method also performs well among UDA baselines, but it generally performs worse than SSDA methods that incorporate labeled target samples for fine-tuning.

Simulation 2: SC shift interventions

Comparison of target excess risk in Simulation 2 with SC shift interventions. The error bars represent the mean
\pm
one standard deviation over 20 trials. The y-axis is on a logarithmic scale.

Next we generate three datasets under SC shift interventions with
d=100
, varying the number of intervened columns
r_{\textsf{sc}}
in the connectivity matrix with
r_{\textsf{sc}}\in\{5,10,15\}
. We consider
M=4
source domains. For each
m=1,\ldots,4
, the matrix
B^{(m)}
is a lower triangular matrix with diagonal entries zeros and off-diagonal entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,0.25/d)
, while the vector
b^{(m)}
has entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,4/d)
. For the target domain, we set
b^{{(0)}}=b^{(1)}
, and construct the connectivity matrix
B^{{(0)}}
as follows: for
j=1,\ldots,r_{\textsf{sc}}
and
i>j
, the
(i,j)
-th entry
B^{{(0)}}_{[i,j]}
of
B^{{(0)}}
is set to
B^{(1)}_{[i,j]}+b^{{(0)}}_{[i]}+\eta_{ij}
, where
\eta_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)
; all other entries remain unchanged from
B^{(1)}
. The sample sizes for the labeled target datasets are the same as Simulation 1 while for the source and unlabeled target datasets, we use
n^{(m)}=n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}=n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=10,000
.

Figure
4
presents the test target excess risks for various UDA, SSDA, and target-only methods, averaged over 20 trials. In this setting, FT-OLS-Src-L1 with the first source domain is selected by MASFT and is highlighted in red. For OLS-Tar-MoreData, we use
n_{\textsf{new}}^{(0)}={dn^{(0)}/}{r_{\textsf{sc}}}
labeled target samples to match the theoretical bound of FT-OLS-Src (ignoring constant factors). We see that while FT-OLS-Src with
\ell_{1}
norm penalty performs slightly worse than OLS-Tar-MoreData, it outperforms all other methods, including the fine-tuning method with
\ell_{2}
norm penalty.

Simulation 3: AW shift interventions

Figure 5:
Comparison of target excess risk in Simulation 3 with AW shift interventions. The error bars represent the mean
\pm
one standard deviation over 20 trials. The y-axis is on a logarithmic scale.

Finally, we generate three datasets under AW shift interventions with
d=100
while varying the dimension of non-invariant features
r_{\textsf{aw}}
with
r_{\textsf{aw}}\in\{4,6,8\}
. We consider
M=11
source domains. The matrix
B
is a lower triangular matrix with diagonal zeros and off-diagonal entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,9/d)
. Once generated,
B
is fixed across all source domains, i.e.,
B^{(m)}=B
for all
m\geq 1
. To construct
b^{(m)}
, we first generate
U\in\mathbb{R}^{70\times r_{\textsf{aw}}}
with entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)
, and set
b_{\text{noninv}}^{(m)}=U\zeta^{(m)}
, where each entry of
\zeta^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
is drawn i.i.d. from
\left|{\mathcal{N}(0,16/r_{\textsf{aw}})}\right|
. We then generate
b_{\text{inv}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{30}/d)
and define
b^{(m)}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{\text{noninv}}^{(m)}{}^{\top};&b_{\text{inv}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For the target domain, we set
B^{{(0)}}=B
, and define
b^{{(0)}}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{\text{noninv}}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top};&b_{\text{inv}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
b_{\text{noninv}}^{{(0)}}=U\zeta^{{(0)}}
, and each entry of
\zeta^{{(0)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
is drawn i.i.d. from
\left|{\mathcal{N}(0,0.25/r_{\textsf{aw}})}\right|
. The sample sizes for the source and target datasets are the same as Simulation 1.

Figure
5
shows the target prediction performance of different methods averaged over 20 trials. FT-CIP-Tar is selected by MASFT and is highlighted in red. We also include FT-CIP (
29
) which does not use the unlabeled target dataset for fine-tuning and is highlighted in blue. As predicted by our theoretical analysis, both methods exhibit comparable target prediction performance. For OLS-Tar-MoreData, we use
n_{\textsf{new}}^{(0)}={dn^{(0)}/}{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
labeled target samples to match the theoretical bound of FT-CIP (ignoring constant factors). We observe that both FT-CIP and FT-CIP-Tar achieve the best prediction performance among all UDA and SSDA methods and perform comparably to OLS-Tar-MoreData.

7
Discussion

In this paper, we present a theoretical framework for SSDA based on SCMs, focusing on settings with limited labeled target data. Under SCMs, we analyze three types of distributional shifts – confounded additive (CA) shift, sparse connectivity (SC) shift, and anticausal weight (AW) shift. We show that each requires a tailor-made fine-tuning strategy to achieve optimal adaptation. Our results also highlight the complementary role of UDA methods in different SSDA settings, providing effective initialization for fine-tuning to attain minimax-optimal performance. While these strategies rely on prior knowledge of the intervention type, our proposed MASFT reduces this reliance by enabling near-optimal performance through model selection on limited target data.

Looking ahead, extending our analysis beyond anticausal linear SCMs to mixed causal-anticausal or nonlinear SCMs is a promising direction. In practical scenarios, data often arise from latent representations transformed via nonlinear mixing, making it unclear how to apply fine-tuning derived from linear models effectively. Integrating ideas from causal representation learning to recover latent structure and adapt in representation space remains an open challenge. Another exciting direction is to extend the SCM-based SSDA framework to meta-learning, where we need to extend the existing single-task-single-risk learning framework to one that involves multiple tasks.

Notation used throughout the proofs.

A.1
Sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential norms

We begin with describing the properties of sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential norms. Recall that for a zero-mean random variable
Z
, the sub-Gaussian norm
[
59
, Definition 2.5.6]
is defined as

\left\|Z\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\coloneqq\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\left[{\exp(Z^{2}/t^{2})}\right]\leq 2\},

and the sub-exponential norm
[
59
, Definition 2.7.5]
is defined as

\left\|Z\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\coloneqq\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\left[{\exp(\left|{Z}\right|/t)}\right]\leq 2\}.

The sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential parameters of
Z
are given by
\left\|Z\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
and
\left\|Z\right\|_{\psi_{1}}
, respectively, up to universal constants
[
59
, Proposition 2.5.2, Proposition 2.7.1]
.
For a zero-mean random vector
W
, the sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential norm are defined as the maximum of the sub-Gaussian norms of one-dimensional projections:

\left\|W\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\coloneqq\sup_{u:\lVert{u}\rVert_{2}=1}\left\|u^{\top}W\right\|_{\psi_{2}},\text{ and }\left\|W\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\coloneqq\sup_{u:\lVert{u}\rVert_{2}=1}\left\|u^{\top}W\right\|_{\psi_{1}}.

We present two lemmas describing the properties of sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential norms. The first lemma shows how the sub-Gaussian norm behaves under linear transformations of sub-Gaussian random vectors. This result follows directly from
[
59
, Exercise 2.7.11]
, and the proof is omitted.

Lemma A.1

(Sub-Gaussianity under linear transformations)
.

Let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameters
\sigma_{W}
and
\sigma_{Z}
, respectively. Then, for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,

\left\|AW+Z\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq c(\sigma_{W}\lVert{{A}}\rVert+\sigma_{Z}),

for some universal constant
c>0
.

The second lemma provides a bound on the sub-exponential norm of the product of a sub-Gaussian random vector and a sub-Gaussian random variable. This is a consequence of the property of the sub-exponential norm
[
59
, Lemma 2.7.6]
, and the proof is omitted.

Lemma A.2

(Sub-exponential norm of the product of sub-Gaussian variables)
.

Let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vector with parameter
\sigma_{W}
, and let
Z\in\mathbb{R}
be a zero-mean sub-Gaussian random variable with parameter
\sigma_{Z}
. Then

\left\|WZ\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq c\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z},

for some universal constant
c>0
.

Consider the source and target covariates
X^{(m)}
,
m=0,1,\ldots,M
, generated from linear SCMs under Assumption
1
. Solving
X^{(m)}
from linear structural equations (
3
) yields the expression

\displaystyle X^{(m)}=H^{(m)}b^{(m)}\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}+H^{(m)}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X},

(34)

where
H^{(m)}\coloneqq(\mathbf{I}-B^{(m)})^{-1}
(see Table
2
for notation). Applying Lemma
A.1
and the inequality
\lVert{Ax}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert{{A}}\rVert\lVert{x}\rVert_{2}
, the following result immediately follows; the proof is omitted.

Lemma A.3

(Sub-Gaussian parameter of the covariates)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then the sub-Gaussian norm of
X^{(m)}
is bounded as

\lVert{X^{(m)}}\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq c\lVert{{H^{(m)}}}\rVert\left(\lVert{b^{(m)}}\rVert_{2}\cdot\sigma^{(m)}_{\epsilon_{Y}}+\sigma^{(m)}_{\epsilon_{X}}\right)\text{ for all $m=0,1,\ldots,M$},

for some universal constant
c>0
. Therefore, if we define

\sigma_{X}\coloneqq\max_{m=0,1,\ldots,M}\left\{c\lVert{{H^{(m)}}}\rVert\left(\lVert{b^{(m)}}\rVert_{2}\cdot\sigma^{(m)}_{\epsilon_{Y}}+\sigma^{(m)}_{\epsilon_{X}}\right)\right\},

(35)

then for any
m=0,1,\ldots,M
,
X^{(m)}
is a sub-Gaussian vector with parameter
\sigma_{X}
.

A.2
Concentration inequalities

To facilitate the use of concentration inequalities for functions of sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential random vectors, we recall them here. Proofs are deferred to Appendix
H
.

The first lemma states a standard concentration of a sample covariance matrix, and its proof is omitted.

Lemma A.4

(Adaptation of Theorem 6.5 of
[
60
]
)
.

Let
W_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, be
i.i.d.
zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameter
\sigma_{W}
. Let
\Sigma_{W}=\mathbb{E}\left[{W_{1}W_{1}^{\top}}\right]
denote the population covariance matrix, and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}W_{i}^{\top}
the sample covariance matrix. There exists a universal
c>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
n\geq\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}\right\|\leq c\sigma_{W}^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right).

A direct consequence of Lemma
A.4
together with Weyl’s inequality is upper and lower bounds on the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of a sample covariance matrix. For
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that if
n\geq c\sigma_{W}^{4}\lambda_{\rm min}^{-2}(\Sigma_{W})\cdot\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{W})/2\leq\lambda_{\rm min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{W})\leq\lambda_{\rm max}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{W})\leq 2\lambda_{\rm max}(\Sigma_{W}).

(36)

The next lemma considers the setting where the leading eigenvalues (in absolute value) of the difference between two covariance matrices are well separated from the remaining eigenvalues. In particular, it shows that with a sufficiently large sample size, the eigenvectors corresponding to these leading eigenvalues can be accurately estimated by those of the corresponding finite-sample quantities.

Lemma A.5
.

Under the settings of Lemma
A.4
, let
\widetilde{W}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,\ldots,\widetilde{n}
, be another set of
i.i.d.
zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameter
\sigma_{W}
, and define
\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\widetilde{W}_{1}\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\top}}\right]
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{n}}\widetilde{W}_{i}\widetilde{W}_{i}^{\top}
. Suppose there exists
\lambda_{\text{gap}}>0
such that the largest
r
eigenvalues in absolute value of
\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}
are greater than
\lambda_{\text{gap}}
, and the remaining eigenvalues have absolute values less than
\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\text{gap}}
. Let
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
be the matrix of eigenvectors corresponding to the top
r
eigenvalues in absolute value, and let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r)}
be an orthogonal complement to the column space of
V
. Similarly, let
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
and
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r)}
be the corresponding matrices from the finite-sample estimate
\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}
. There exists universal constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, if
n,\widetilde{n}\geq c_{0}(\sigma_{W}^{4}\lambda_{\rm gap}^{-2}\vee 1)\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}-VR\right\|\leq\frac{c_{1}\sigma_{W}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\widetilde{n}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\widetilde{n}}}\right),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}-QR\right\|\leq\frac{c_{1}\sigma_{W}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\widetilde{n}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\widetilde{n}}}\right).

Let
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a general set. The Gaussian width of
T

[
59
, Definition 7.5.1]
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({T})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in T}\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle}\right],

where
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
is a standard Gaussian random vector. The Gaussian width is a measure that captures the “size” or “complexity” of a set in terms of how it interacts with Gaussian noise. In our setting, the Gaussian width provides a tool in establishing the restricted eigenvalue condition (
[
7
]
) for a sub-Gaussian ensemble over a set
T
. Using the arguments from
[
37
, Remark 1.2]
, we can obtain the following result.

Lemma A.6

(Adaptation of Remark 1.2 of
[
37
]
)
.

Let
W_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, be
i.i.d.
zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameter
\sigma_{W}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma_{W}=\mathbb{E}\left[{W_{1}W_{1}^{\top}}\right]
. Let
\textbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
denote the matrix with rows
W_{i}
. Then, there exists a universal constant
c_{0}>0
such that for any subset
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
that contains the origin
0\in T
and for
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\textbf{W}x\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{W})}{2}\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{2}-c_{0}\frac{\sigma_{W}^{4}\lambda_{\rm min}^{-2}(\Sigma_{W})\log(1/\delta)\left\|\Sigma_{W}\right\|\mathbb{W}^{2}(T)}{{n}}\text{ for all $x\in T$}.

The following two lemmas provide concentration bounds on the norms of the product of a sub-Gaussian random vector and a sub-Gaussian random variable. Their proofs are provided in Appendix
H
. The
\ell_{2}
norm bound follows from the concentration inequality under sub-exponential condition.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be i.i.d. zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameter
\sigma_{W}
, and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
be i.i.d. zero-mean sub-Gaussian random variables with parameter
\sigma_{Z}
. Define
\textbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
with rows
W_{i}
and
\textbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with entries
Z_{i}
. Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\left(\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}-\mathbb{E}[\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}]\right)\right\|_{2}\leq c\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n}\right\}.

The
\ell_{\infty}
norm bound follows from applying Bernstein’s inequality to each coordinate and taking a union bound.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be i.i.d. zero-mean sub-Gaussian random vectors with parameter
\sigma_{W}
, and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
be i.i.d. zero-mean sub-Gaussian random variables with parameter
\sigma_{Z}
. Define
\textbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
with rows
W_{i}
and
\textbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with entries
Z_{i}
. Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\left(\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}-\mathbb{E}[\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}]\right)\right\|_{\infty}\leq c\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\right).

Appendix B
Relations between UDA and oracle estimators

In this section, we provide details relating the UDA estimators to the oracle estimator discussed in Section
4
.

B.1
DIP
(1)
-cov under CA shift interventions

We consider the data generation according to Assumption
3
. Letting
H=(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}=-H(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)^{\top}H^{\top}.

(37)

Recall the subspace notation given in Section
4.1
, i.e.,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
is an orthonormal basis for the column space of
\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}
, and
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})}
is an orthonormal basis for the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({V})
.

Since the covariance matching penalty
\beta^{\top}(\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}})\beta=0
is equivalent to
\left\|\Pi_{V}\beta\right\|_{2}=0
, any feasible
\beta
under covariance matching penalty lies in the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({V})
and can be written as
\beta=Qu
for some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d-r_{\textsf{ca}}}
. Substituting this into Equation (
13
), the population DIP
(1)
-cov is the minimizer of the following unconstrained problem

u_{\text{DIP}}^{(1)}:=\argmin_{u}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y^{(1)}-u^{\top}Q^{\top}X^{(1)}\right)^{2}}\right].

Setting the gradient of the objective to zero yields the explicit form

u_{\text{DIP}}^{(1)}=\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right].

Transforming the variable back, we get

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Qu_{\text{DIP}}^{(1)}=Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right].

(38)

By construction,
Q
is orthogonal to the column space of
H(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)
, and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{Q^{\top}X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{Q^{\top}Hb\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}{}^{2}+Q^{\top}H\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}}\right]

\displaystyle=Q^{\top}Hb(w_{Y}^{\top}w_{Y}+\var\left({\xi_{Y}}\right))+Q^{\top}HWw_{Y}

\displaystyle=Q^{\top}Hb\var\left({\xi_{Y}}\right)

\displaystyle=Q^{\top}Hb\var\left({\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[{Q^{\top}X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right],

where the first step uses (
34
), the second step follows from Assumption
3
, and the last step again uses (
34
). Together with the covariance matching
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q
, it follows that the DIP estimator (
38
) is equivalently given by

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]\overset{(i)}{=}Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star},

where step
(i)
uses
\beta^{\star}=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]
. Finally, applying the identity for the projection operator
[
40
]

Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}=\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V(V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V)^{-1}V^{\top},

we conclude

\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\left(\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V(V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V)^{-1}V^{\top}\right)\beta^{\star}.

B.2
OLS-Src
(1)
under SC shift interventions

We consider the data generation according to Assumption
4
. For
m=0,1
, let
H^{(m)}=(\mathbf{I}-B^{(m)})^{-1}
,
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}=\var\left({\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}}\right)
, and
\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}{}^{\top}}\right]
. Since
X^{(m)}=H^{(m)}b\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}+H^{(m)}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}
, we can compute

\Sigma_{X}^{(m)}=H^{(m)}({\nu}_{Y}^{2}bb^{\top}+\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}})H^{(m)}{}^{\top},\text{ for $m=0,1$}.

Using the Sherman-Morrison inversion formula, the inverse of
\Sigma_{X}^{(m)}
is

\displaystyle\Sigma_{X}^{(m)}{}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}H^{(m)}bb^{\top}H^{(m)}{}^{\top}+H^{(m)}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}H^{(m)}{}^{\top}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=H^{(m)}{}^{-\top}\left[\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}-\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}bb^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}{b}^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}{b}}\right]H^{(m)}{}^{-1}.

Additionally, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}Y^{(m)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{H^{(m)}b\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}{}^{2}+H^{(m)}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}}\right]={\nu}_{Y}^{2}H^{(m)}b,\text{ for $m=0,1$}.

Combining these, the population OLS-Src
(1)
estimator and the oracle estimator are given by

\begin{cases}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}=\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]=\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b}H^{(1)}{}^{-\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b,\\
\beta^{\star}=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b}H^{{(0)}}{}^{-\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b.\end{cases}

The difference between the OLS-Src
(1)
and oracle estimators is therefore

\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\beta^{\star}=\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b}\left(B^{{(0)}}-B^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{-1}b.

Since
(B^{{(0)}}-B^{(1)})_{*S^{c}}=0
by Assumption
4
, it follows that
(\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\beta^{\star})_{S^{c}}=0
, that is,
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\beta^{\star}
is
r_{\textsf{sc}}
-sparse with support
S
.

B.3
CIP-mean under AW shift interventions

We consider the data generation according to Assumption
5
. Let
H=(\mathbf{I}-B)^{-1}
. Since
\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}
and
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}
are independent for all
m=0,1,\ldots,M
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}}\ \middle|\ {Y^{(m)}=y}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{Hb^{(m)}Y^{(m)}+H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}}\ \middle|\ {Y^{(m)}=y}\right]=Hb^{(m)}y.

The CIP-mean matching penalty (
24
) therefore becomes

\displaystyle\beta^{\top}\left(Hb^{(m)}-Hb^{(m-1)}\right)=0\text{ for all $m\geq 2$}.

(39)

Recall the subspace notation given in Section
4.3
, i.e.,
V_{\textsf{aw}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{aw}}}
represent an orthonormal basis for the subspace
\spn\{Hb^{(m)}-Hb^{(1)},m\geq 2\}
, and
Q_{\textsf{aw}}
represent an orthonormal matrix satisfying
Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}=0
. From (
39
), any feasible
\beta
must be orthogonal to
V_{\textsf{aw}}
, so we can reparameterize
\beta=Q_{\textsf{aw}}u
for some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d-r_{\textsf{aw}}}
. Substituting this into the original CIP-mean problem (
24
), we get

u_{\text{CIP}}\coloneqq\argmin_{u}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y^{(m)}-u^{\top}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{(m)}\right)^{2}}\right].

By definition of
Q_{\textsf{aw}}
, we have
Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{(m)}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{(m^{\prime})}
for all
m\neq m^{\prime}\geq 1
. Further, the assumption (
23
) implies
Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{{(0)}}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{(1)}
, and therefore
Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{{(0)}}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{(m)}
for all
m=1,\ldots,M
. Thus, for any
m\geq 1
, by Assumption
5
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{(m)}

\displaystyle=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{(m)}\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}+Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}

\displaystyle=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{{(0)}}\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}+Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}Hb^{{(0)}}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}+Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}H\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}

\displaystyle=Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{{(0)}},

and additionally the joint distribution of
(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{(m)},Y^{(m)})
is identical to that of
(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})
.
It follows that
u_{\text{CIP}}
is also the minimizer of the least squares problem in the target domain, i.e.,

u_{\text{CIP}}=\argmin_{u}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y^{{(0)}}-u^{\top}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}X^{{(0)}}\right)^{2}}\right]=(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right],

which yields the population CIP

\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}u_{\textnormal{CIP}}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right].

(40)

Following the same argument as in Appendix
B.1
, this leads to
