\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\left(\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}(V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\right)\beta^{\star}.

Relation between inverses of source and target covariance matrices

Since
Hb^{{(0)}}-Hb^{(1)}\in\spn\{Hb^{(m)}-Hb^{(1)},m\geq 2\}
by (
23
), we can write

Hb^{{(0)}}-Hb^{(1)}=V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha,

for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
. Letting
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}=\var\left({\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y}}\right)=\var\left({\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}}\right)
, we observe that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left(Hb^{{(0)}}b^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}H^{\top}-Hb^{(1)}b^{(1)}{}^{\top}H^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left(V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha b^{(1)}{}^{\top}H^{\top}+Hb^{(1)}\alpha^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}+V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\alpha^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\right).

By the Woodbury matrix identity, it follows

\displaystyle\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha b^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}H^{\top}+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}Hb^{{(0)}}\alpha^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\alpha^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}-{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\bigl[\,Hb^{(1)}\;\;V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\bigr]\,S^{-1}\,\bigl[\,Hb^{(1)}\;\;V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha\bigr]^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1},

where

S=\begin{pmatrix}{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\alpha^{\top}V_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha&-(1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{(1)}{}^{\top}H^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha)\\
-(1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{(1)}{}^{\top}H^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aw}}\alpha)&-1+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}b^{(1)}{}^{\top}H^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}Hb^{(1)}\end{pmatrix}.

This proves Equation (
27
).

Appendix C
Finite-sample guarantees of UDA estimators

In this section, we quantify the finite-sample errors for (pooled) OLS-Src, DIP
(1)
-cov, and CIP-mean.

C.1
Finite-sample analysis for OLS-Pool

We begin by introducing OLS-Pool that performs OLS on a pooled set of source datasets. This estimator is arguably the most common baseline method when working with multiple source datasets.

•

OLS-Pool in population:
the population OLS estimator based on the pooled source distributions is

\displaystyle f_{\textnormal{Pool}}(x)\coloneqq\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}^{\top}x,\text{ where }\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{(m)}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)})^{2}}\right].

In the current formulation of OLS-Pool, we use uniform weights for each source distribution, but other weighting schemes can also be applied. For example, the weights can be determined based on the similarity of each source distribution to the target distribution
[
66
]
.

By pooling all source datasets, OLS-Pool can be more robust to the perturbations observed across multiple source domains compared to individual OLS-Src
(m)
. This estimator is often used in the literature as a baseline to evaluate the effectiveness of more advanced UDA methods
[
21
]
. However, OLS-Pool does not leverage any labeled target samples or exploit structural relationships between the source and target, so it does not always guarantee low target risk.

•

OLS-Pool in finite-sample:
the finite-sample version of OLS-Pool based on pooled multiple source datasets is

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{\textnormal{Pool}}(x)\coloneqq\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}^{\top}x,\text{ where }\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\beta}\ \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\left(Y^{(m)}_{i}-\beta^{\top}X^{(m)}_{i}\right)^{2}.

(41)

The following proposition provides finite-sample guarantees for
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}
assuming that the data are generated from anticausal linear SCMs in Assumptions
1
. For the case of a single source domain (
M=1
), such results are well-established in the context of causal linear prediction. However, analogous results for anticausal linear SCMs with multiple source environments are not readily available in the literature.

Proposition C.1
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, there exist universal constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/(2M))
, if
\overline{n}\geq c_{1}\max\left\{d_{\delta}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2},d\log(1/\delta)\right\}
with
d_{\delta}=d+\log(1/\delta)
, with probability at least
1-2M\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}-\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}\leq c_{0}\eta\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}},

where
\eta={\kappa}_{\text{min}}\cdot(\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}})
.

The proof is given in Appendix
G.1
. When
\eta
is of constant order, this result recovers the standard error rate of
\sqrt{d/n^{(m)}}
for a single source domain (
M=1
), and achieves a rate of
\max_{m\geq 1}\sqrt{d/n^{(m)}}
when multiple source domains are used.

C.2
Finite-sample analysis for DIP
(1)
-cov

We assume the CA shift interventions in Assumption
3
and use the notation given in Section
4.1
. Recall
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
is the matrix of eigenvectors corresponding to the top
r_{\textsf{ca}}
eigenvalues (in absolute value) of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
, where
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}
are the empirical covariance matrices given by

\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}{}^{\top}\text{ and }\ \widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}=\frac{1}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}.

(42)

While
\widehat{V}
serves as a finite-sample estimate of
V
, its accuracy depends on the size of the eigen-gap, i.e., the separation between the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
nonzero eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
and zero.

To illustrate how
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
defined in (
17
) relates to this eigen-gap, we can write from (
37
) that
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}=HA_{\textnormal{perb}}H^{\top}
,
where recall from Section
4.1.2
that
A_{\textnormal{perb}}=(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)(bw_{Y}^{\top}+W)^{\top}
. Since both
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
and
A_{\textnormal{perb}}
are positive semi-definite with rank
r_{\textsf{ca}}
, let
\lambda_{1}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})>0
denote the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
nonzero eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
, and let
\lambda_{1}(A_{\textnormal{perb}})\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(A_{\textnormal{perb}})>0
denote the
r_{\textsf{ca}}
nonzero eigenvalues of
A_{\textnormal{perb}}
. By
[
43
, Theorem 1]
, we then have

\frac{\lambda_{i}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})}{\lambda_{i}(A_{\textnormal{perb}})}\geq\lambda_{\rm min}(H^{\top}H)\text{ for all $i=1,\ldots,r_{\textsf{ca}}$}.

This inequality implies that the nonzero eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
are always lower bounded by
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
, and thereby characterizing the eigen-gap condition for
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}
.

In the following, we formalize how the finite-sample error bound of DIP
(1)
-cov depends on this eigen-gap and the sizes of the source and unlabeled target samples.

Proposition C.2
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, there exist universal constants
c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/3)
, if
n^{(1)}\geq c\max\left\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},d\log(1/\delta)\right\}
and
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}d_{\delta}
with
d_{\delta}=d+\log(1/\delta)
,
with probability at least
1-3\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+\eta_{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right),

where
\eta_{1}=c^{\prime\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)
and
\eta_{2}=c^{\prime\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\cdot{\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}}
.

The proof is given in Appendix
G.2
. Proposition
C.2
shows that when
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are all constant orders, and the number of labeled source samples
n^{(1)}
and unlabeled source/target samples
(n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)})
are at least
\gtrsim d
, the estimation error scales as

\sqrt{\frac{d}{n^{(1)}}}\text{ (labeled) }+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\text{ (unlabeled)}.

The bound further shows that a larger eigen-gap
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
leads to more accurate estimation of DIP
(1)
-cov, which is consistent with the fact that estimating
V
reliably requires well-separated eigenvalues.

C.3
Finite-sample analysis for CIP-mean

We assume the AW shift interventions in Assumption
5
. To simplify the analysis, each source dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}
is divided into two subsets of equal size,
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}=\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{2}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, where

\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{\ell}\coloneqq\{(X^{(m)}_{\ell i},Y^{(m)}_{\ell i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}},\quad\ell=1,2.

Note that each subset
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{\ell}
contains
n^{(m)}
samples. When it is clear from context the specific subset being used, we omit the subscript
\ell
and denote the covariates and labels simply as
X^{(m)}_{i}
and
Y^{(m)}_{i}
. In the finite-sample CIP-mean (
25
), the first subset
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{1}
is used to estimate
\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}
for the empirical conditional mean-matching penalty, and the second subset
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{2}
is used to compute the least-squares objective.

Before presenting the finite-sample guarantees of CIP-mean, we introduce additional notation than what was given in Section
4.3
. Let
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(M-1)}
be the matrix whose columns are the differences
Hb^{(m)}-Hb^{(m-1)}
across source domains

{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}Hb^{(2)}-Hb^{(1)},\cdots,Hb^{(M)}-Hb^{(M-1)}\end{pmatrix}.

(43)

According to Assumption
5
, the matrix
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
has rank
r_{\textsf{aw}}
, and further,
V_{\textsf{aw}}
corresponds the left-singular vectors of
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
corresponding to its
r_{\textsf{aw}}
nonzero singular values.

To construct the finite-sample estimate of
V_{\textsf{aw}}
, we first estimate each
Hb^{(m)}
via regressing
X^{(m)}
on
Y^{(m)}
using
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{1}
, i.e.,

\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(m)}\coloneqq\argmin_{w}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\lVert{X^{(m)}_{1i}-Y^{(m)}_{1i}\cdot w}\rVert_{2}^{2},\text{ for $m\geq 1$}.

(44)

We then form a plug-in estimator of
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
as

\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(2)}-\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(1)},\cdots,\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(M)}-\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(M-1)}\end{pmatrix}.

(45)

The matrix
\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}
is then defined as the left-singular vectors of
\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
corresponding to its top
r_{\textsf{aw}}
singular values.

To control the finite-sample error of
\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}
—and hence of
\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}
—it is necessary to make sure there is a sufficient separation between the nonzero singular values of
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
and zero. Comparing
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
with
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
in (
30
), we observe that the smallest singular value of
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
is lower bounded by that of
{P}_{\textsf{aug}}
, i.e.,
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
defined in (
31
). Therefore,
{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}
quantifies the separation of singular values and plays a critical role in controlling the finite-sample error of
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}
.

The following proposition establishes the finite-sample guarantees for CIP-mean (recall
\nu_{Y}^{2}=\var\left({Y^{(m)}}\right)
for all
m=0,1,\ldots,M
).

Proposition C.3
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
5
, there exist universal constants
c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/(4M+4))
, if
\overline{n}\geq c\max\left({\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},d\log(1/\delta),\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)\right)
,
with probability at least
1-(4M+4)\delta
, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{\overline{n}}}+\eta_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},

where
\eta_{1}=c^{\prime\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)
and
\eta_{2}=c^{\prime\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\cdot{\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}
.

Its proof is provided in Appendix
G.3
. Proposition
C.3
shows that when
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are constant orders, the error of
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}
scales as
\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{d/n^{(m)}}
. This rate matches the convergence rate of the OLS-Pool estimator (see Proposition
C.1
). Furthermore, the second term in the bound shows that more diversity in the perturbation directions
Hb^{(m)}-Hb^{(m-1)}
leads to improved estimation accuracy, highlighting the importance of heterogeneity across source domains for achieving better CIP-mean estimates.

Appendix D
Risk upper bounds with model selection

We provide target excess risk bound of MASFT described in Section
5
when the loss function is given by the squared loss. In particular, we show that the model selection step (
33
) in MASFT only introduces a mild overhead in the labeled target sample complexity beyond that needed for individual fine-tuning.

Proposition D.1
.

Suppose that the loss function is the squared loss, and for all
\theta\in\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}
,
Y^{{(0)}}-f_{\theta}(X^{{(0)}})
is zero-mean sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma_{\ell}
. Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}\geq c\log(2L/\delta)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness of the target validation dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}}})\leq\min_{j=1,\ldots,L}\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{j}}}})+c^{\prime}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}},

where
c,c^{\prime}>0
are universal constants.

The proof follows from classical model selection results (e.g.,
[
2
, Section 4.6]
) and is provided in Section
G.4
for completeness. The assumption that
Y^{{(0)}}-f_{\theta}(X^{{(0)}})
is a sub-Gaussian random variable is a mild condition that holds under general settings, such as when both
X^{{(0)}}
and
Y^{{(0)}}
are sub-Gaussian and
f_{\theta}
, for
\theta\in\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}
, satisfies the Lipschitz condition. In this case, the sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\ell}
depends on the sub-Gaussian parameters of
X^{{(0)}}
and
Y^{{(0)}}
, as well as the Lipschitz constant of
f_{\theta}
.

Proposition
D.1
shows that the target excess risk of the estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}
returned by MASFT is at most the minimum excess risk among all fine-tuned estimators, with an additional term of
\sqrt{\log L/n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}
. Therefore, MASFT guarantees that only an extra
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}\approx\log L
labeled target samples are needed to select a near-optimal fine-tuned estimator from the candidate models.

Appendix E
Proof of main theorems on risk upper bounds

In this section, we prove Theorem
4.1
, Theorem
4.3
, and Theorem
4.5
, which provide upper bounds for the fine-tuning methods presented in Section
4
.

E.1
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We begin by observing that the oracle estimator (
7
) satisfies the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}
after multiplying
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}
on both sides of (
15
). This implies that
\beta^{\star}
is an optimal solution to the following constrained optimization problem

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}=\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]\text{ s.t. }Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta.

(46)

Comparing with FT-DIP
(1)
(
16
), the above problem uses population quantities in both its objective and constraint. To deal with finite-sample errors that arise from the objective and constraint separately, we introduce an intermediate estimator, where the objective is still at population level while the constraint is from empirical estimates,

\displaystyle\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}=\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]\text{ s.t. }\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta.

(47)

Therefore, our proof strategy is to decompose the error into two terms by writing
\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}=(\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}})+(\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}})
, and bound each term separately. Specifically, we name the two terms as follows:

\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{1}}+\underbrace{\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{2}}.

(48)

The first term
T_{1}
arises from using the finite-sample estimates
Q,\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}
,
\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
in the constraint. We expect
T_{1}
to depend on the source and unlabeled target sample sizes
n^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
, which are assumed to be large enough to ensure
T_{1}
is small. The second term
T_{2}
captures finite-sample error of least squares on a constrained subspace of dimension
r_{\textsf{ca}}
, so we expect it to scale as
\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}/n^{(0)}}
. In particular, the introduction of
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
allows us to isolate the finite-sample error due to the labeled target samples
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
while conditioning on
\widehat{Q}
,
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}
,
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
being fixed. Since
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
is independent of
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
, combining
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
via a union bound suffices to conclude.

Before we proceed to bound
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, given the subspace notations in Section
4.1.1
, we define high probability events under which the empirical estimates based on source and unlabeled target data concentrate around their population counterparts. Define orthonormal matrices
R_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})\times(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})}
and
R_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{ca}}\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
as

R_{q}\coloneqq\inf_{\widetilde{R}^{\top}\widetilde{R}=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}-Q\widetilde{R}\right\|,\quad\text{and}\quad R_{v}\coloneqq\inf_{\widetilde{R}^{\top}\widetilde{R}=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}-V\widetilde{R}\right\|.

The first event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
ensures the covariance matrix and subspaces are well aligned with their population counterparts.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\leq c_{1}\sigma_{X}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}};\ \ \frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\leq\lambda_{\rm min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}})\leq\lambda_{\rm max}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}})\leq 2\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}};

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{Q}-QR_{q}\right\|\leq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right);

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{V}-VR_{v}\right\|\leq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right)\;\Bigg\}.

Since
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}\geq c_{2}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}d_{\delta}
and
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq c_{2}\max\{1,{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2},{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\}d_{\delta}
, by Lemma
A.4
, Equation (
36
), and Lemma
A.5
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
.

Immediate consequences of being in
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, together with the sub-multiplicative property of the operator norm and the triangle inequality, are the following bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\leq\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}};

(49)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\mathbf{I}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}};

(50)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}VR_{v}\right\|

(51)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}VR_{v}\right\|+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}VR_{v}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}VR_{v}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\widehat{V}-VR_{v}\right\|+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\left({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}};

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QR_{q}\right\|

(52)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\left({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

Next, define the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\coloneqq\left\{\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+\eta_{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right)\right\},

where

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=c_{3}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\right),\quad\eta_{2}=c_{3}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\overline{\kappa}\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}.

(53)

By Proposition
C.2
and the assumption
n^{(1)}\geq c_{2}\max\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},d\log(1/\delta)\}
,
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq c_{2}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}d_{\delta}
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds with probability at least
1-3\delta
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}\coloneqq\left\{\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\leq c_{3}\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right\},

(54)

where
\eta_{\text{LS}}\coloneqq{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\right)
. By Proposition
C.1
with
M=1
and the assumption
n^{(1)}\geq c_{2}\max\{d_{\delta}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2},d\log(1/\delta)\}
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
holds with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
.

Define the adjusted response and projected covariate

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Z^{{(0)}}_{i}\coloneqq Y^{{(0)}}_{i}-X^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}},\\
W^{{(0)}}_{i}\coloneqq\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}_{i}.\end{cases}

(55)

Let
\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{(0)}\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
be the matrix whose
i
-th row is
W^{{(0)}}_{i}
, and
\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{(0)}}
be the vector with entries
Z^{{(0)}}_{i}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})}\right]\right\|_{2}

(56)

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\Bigg\}.

Since
n^{(0)}\geq r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)
, applying Lemma
A.7
for concentration of product of sub-Gaussian vectors around its mean, event
\mathcal{E}_{4}
holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, where
\sigma_{W}
and
\sigma_{Z}
represent the sub-Gaussian parameters of
W^{{(0)}}_{i}
and
W^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-Z^{{(0)}}_{i}=X^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-Y^{{(0)}}_{i}
, respectively.

Let

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\left\{x:\left\|\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x\right\|_{2}=0,\ \left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x\right\|_{2}\leq 2\varrho\right\},

(57)

and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{5}\coloneqq\left\{\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}x\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{c_{5}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}^{4}\overline{\kappa}\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}},\quad\forall x\in\Lambda\right\}.

(58)

Since
\Lambda
contains origin, applying Lemma
A.6
, it follows
\mathcal{E}_{5}
holds with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
.

We define the intersection of the events

\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}\cap\mathcal{E}_{5}.

In the following, we prove our result under the event
\mathcal{E}
.

E.1.1
Bounding
T_{1}

The main steps to bound
T_{1}
in (
48
) are as follows.

(a)

(Decomposition of error) First, we decompose
\beta^{\star}
in the directions of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}Q
and
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V
, and similarly decompose
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
in the directions of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}
.

(b)

(Error in
V
directions) Second, we express the components of
\beta^{\star}
and
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
in the directions of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}
, respectively, as solutions to linear programs and use this to bound the difference between them.

(c)

(Error in
Q
directions) Third, the components in the directions of
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}Q
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}
, are given by
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
and
\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
, respectively, which we control using the inequalities under the event
\mathcal{E}
.

(a) Decomposition of error

Since
\beta^{\star}
satisfies the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}
and
(Q,V)
together form an orthonormal basis, we can write
\beta^{\star}
as

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}(QR_{q}R_{q}^{\top}Q^{\top}+VR_{v}R_{v}^{\top}V^{\top})\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}

(59)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QR_{q}R_{q}^{\top}Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}+\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}VR_{v}\underbrace{R_{v}^{\top}V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}}_{\eqqcolon\alpha^{\star}}.

For notational convenience, we overwrite
Q=QR_{q}
and
V=VR_{v}
for the remainder of the proof. Now consider the objective in (
46
).

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(QQ^{\top}+VV^{\top})\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}{}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}+\underbrace{\beta^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}V}_{\eqqcolon\alpha}V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right],

where step
(i)
holds due to the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta
. Note that
\alpha^{\star}
minimizes the above objective seen as a function of
\alpha
. Therefore, it satisfies the first-order optimality condition,

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}{}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}})}\right]}_{\eqqcolon b^{\star}}

(60)

\displaystyle\quad\quad={\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V}\right]}_{\eqqcolon A^{\star}}}\alpha^{\star}.

Similarly,
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
satisfies the constraint
\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
, which allows us to write

\displaystyle\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}+\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\underbrace{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}_{\eqqcolon\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}.

(61)

And
\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
satisfies the following first-order optimality condition

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}(Y^{{(0)}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}{}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}})}\right]}_{\eqqcolon b_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}

(62)

\displaystyle\quad\quad={\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}}\right]}_{\eqqcolon A_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}.

Subtracting Equations (
60
) and (
62
), we obtain

\displaystyle\underbrace{b^{\star}-b_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}_{\eqqcolon\Delta b}

(63)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(A^{\star}-A_{\textnormal{tar-con}})}_{\eqqcolon\Delta A}\alpha^{\star}+{A_{\textnormal{tar-con}}}(\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}).

In order to obtain the error in the
\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V
direction, it suffices to control
\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
, which reduces to control
\Delta b
and
\Delta A
separately.

(b) Error in
V
directions

To bound
\Delta b
, rearranging terms and apply triangle inequality, we obtain

\left\|\Delta b\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[{V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]\right\|_{2}}_{\coloneqq(A_{1})}\\
+\underbrace{\left\|{V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}-{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}\right\|_{2}}_{\coloneqq(A_{2})}.

To bound
(A_{1})
, note that

(A_{1})

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[{V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\left\|{V^{\top}\beta^{\star}-\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(V-\widehat{V})^{\top}\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}})\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|V-\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{I}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2},

where step
(i)
follows from
\beta^{\star}=\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]
. Under the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\text{$(A_{1})$}\leq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+2c_{1}({\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}+{\kappa}_{\text{min}})\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

(64)

To bound
(A_{2})
, we add and subtract
\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
and apply triangle inequality to get

(A_{2})\leq\left\|(V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}-\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}){QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}\right\|_{2}\\
+\left\|{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}})}\right\|_{2}.

(65)

For the first term on the right-hand side of (
65
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|(V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}-\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}){QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}\right\|_{2}

(66)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\left\|V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}-\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}2c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\overline{\kappa}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+2c_{1}\left({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\right)\overline{\kappa}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}},

where step
(i)
uses the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}
and the inequality
\left\|ABx\right\|\leq\left\|A\right\|\left\|B\right\|\left\|x\right\|_{2}
, and step
(ii)
uses the inequalities under
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. For the second term, observe that by the assumption
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq 4c_{1}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\leq\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}-\mathbf{I}\right\|+\left\|\mathbf{I}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}+1\leq 2,

(67)

where the second step follows from (
49
). Further, using the constraint
Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}
, applying the triangle inequality and the sub-multiplicative property of the operator norm multiple times, we have

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqcolon(D_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}Q-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\right\|\left\|Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\right\|\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{Q}\right\|\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\right\|\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{Q}\right\|\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}.

Under
\mathcal{E}
, this yields, with
\eta_{1}
in (
53
) and
\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}
in (
70
),

\displaystyle(D_{1})\leq 4\overline{\kappa}\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+c_{6}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+c_{6}\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

(68)

Combining (
64
), (
65
), (
66
), (
67
), (
68
), and using
\overline{\kappa}\geq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta b\right\|_{2}\leq 8\eta_{1}\overline{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+c_{7}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+c_{7}\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}},

(69)

where with
\eta_{2}
in (
53
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\coloneqq\overline{\kappa}\left[{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}+\eta_{2}\right],\text{ and }

(70)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\coloneqq\overline{\kappa}\left[({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}+\eta_{2}\right].

To bound
\Delta A
, applying triangle inequality and the sub-multiplicative property of the operator norm repeatedly, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta A\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|A^{\star}-\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V\right\|+\left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V-A_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|V\right\|+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|V\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 65.04256pt+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right\|\left\|V-\widehat{V}\right\|.

Under
\mathcal{E}
, and using (
49
), (
67
), this yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta A\right\|\leq 6c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+6c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

(71)

Now in order to bound
\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
, we use (
63
) together with (
69
), (
71
),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(A_{\textnormal{tar-con}})\left\|\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|A_{\textnormal{tar-con}}(\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Delta b\right\|_{2}+\left\|\Delta A\right\|\left\|\alpha^{\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Delta b\right\|_{2}+\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|\Delta A\right\|\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2},

where the last step uses
\left\|\alpha^{\star}\right\|_{2}=\left\|V^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\leq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}
.
Additionally, applying the inequality
\sigma_{\textnormal{min}}(AB)\geq\sigma_{\textnormal{min}}(A)\sigma_{\textnormal{min}}(B)
for matrices
A,B
with
B
full column rank, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(A_{\textnormal{tar-con}})\geq\sigma_{\textnormal{min}}^{2}(\widehat{V})\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right)=\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right).

(72)

We further observe

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}^{-1}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}-\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{})+\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}2\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}+1\right)\overset{(ii)}{\leq}4\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}},

where step
(i)
follows from the concentration of empirical covariance matrix under
\mathcal{E}
, and step
(ii)
follows from the assumption
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq 4c_{1}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta}
.

Combining the above and simplifying, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}\leq 32\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\overline{\kappa}\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+4c_{8}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+4c_{8}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

(73)

(c) Error in
Q
directions and final bound of
T_{1}

Finally, combining (
59
) and (
61
), and proceeding similarly to the preceding derivations, we can decompose
T_{1}
as

\displaystyle T_{1}=\left\|\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}QQ^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{=(D_{1})}+\underbrace{\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V\alpha^{\star}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon(D_{2})}.

We recognize the bound of
(D_{1})
from (
68
). To bound
(D_{2})
, after applying triangle inequality, observe that everything can be bounded with inequalities in
\mathcal{E}
and (
73
),

\displaystyle(D_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V\alpha^{\star}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\alpha^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\alpha^{\star}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}V-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|_{2}\left\|\alpha^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|\alpha^{\star}-\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}.

Putting these bounds together and simplifying, we conclude, with
\eta_{1}
in (
53
) and
\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2}
in (
70
),

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{9}\overline{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+c_{9}\overline{\kappa}\widetilde{\eta}_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+c_{9}\overline{\kappa}\widetilde{\eta}_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

(74)

E.1.2
Bounding
T_{2}

Bounding
T_{2}
in (
48
) is about bounding finite-sample risk for constrained least squares. We introduce the shorthand
\Delta=\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
. The main steps are outlined as follows.

(a)

(Feasibility and basic inequality) First, after checking the feasibility of
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
in (
16
), we derive a basic inequality using the optimality of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
. The basic inequality contains a quadratic term in
\Delta
(denoted
B_{1}
) and a linear term in
\Delta
(denoted
B_{2}
).

(b)

(
B_{2}
bound) Second, we bound
B_{2}
using sub-exponential concentration and the fact that
\Delta
lies in an
r_{\textsf{ca}}
-dimensional subspace.

(c)

(
B_{1}
bound) Third, we lower bound
B_{1}
using sub-Gaussian concentration and a restricted eigenvalue condition. Then we conclude.

(a) Feasibility and basic inequality

We proceed by conditioning on
\widehat{Q},\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}},\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
and by focusing on the randomness over the labeled target dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
. To check the feasibility of
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
in Equation (
16
), note from (
61
), (
62
) that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|A_{\textnormal{tar-con}}^{-1}b_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}\leq 4\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|b_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2},

where the last step follows from (
72
). Furthermore, we observe

\displaystyle\left\|b_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|V^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\left(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\left\|\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}\right\|\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}2\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+4\overline{\kappa}\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}2\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+8\overline{\kappa}\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\right),

where (i) follows from triangle inequality together with
\left\|Ax\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|A\right\|\left\|x\right\|_{2}
; (ii) uses (
67
) and the bounds on
\mathcal{E}
; and (iii) follows from the closed form expression for
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
(c.f. see Equation (
136
)) and the fact that the projection operator has unit operator norm. Under event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
, we also have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}},

under the assumption
n^{(1)}\geq c_{3}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\cdot d\log(1/\delta)
. Therefore, as long as

\displaystyle\varrho\geq 64\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left(\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\overline{\kappa}\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}+\overline{\kappa}\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right),

(75)

\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
satisfies the constraint in (
16
). By the optimality of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}}-\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}}-\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Rearranging the terms, we arrive at the following basic inequality

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}}_{\eqqcolon(B_{1})}\leq\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})},{\Delta}\right\rangle}_{\eqqcolon(B_{2})}.

(76)

Before bounding
(B_{1})
and
(B_{2})
, we make a preliminary observation. Given the constraint
\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
, we decompose
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
analogously to (
61
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}+\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\underbrace{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}_{\coloneqq\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}.

(77)

Combining (
61
), (
77
), we derive an explicit expression for
\Delta
:

\Delta=\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}(\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}).

(78)

(b)
(B_{2})
bound

Using notation in (
55
), observe that the residual can be written as
X^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-Y^{{(0)}}_{i}=W^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-Z^{{(0)}}_{i}
. Using this expression, we bound
(B_{2})
as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})},{\Delta}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})},{\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}(\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})},{\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})}\right]},{\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{W}^{{(0)}}\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Z}^{{(0)}})}\right]\right\|_{2}\left\|\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}.

Here step
(i)
uses the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[{W^{{(0)}}_{1}W^{{(0)}}_{i}{}^{\top}}\right]\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}=\mathbb{E}\left[{W^{{(0)}}_{1}Z^{{(0)}}_{1}}\right]
which follows from (
62
), and step
(ii)
follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

The first term is further bounded under event
\mathcal{E}_{4}
given in (
56
). Applying Lemma
A.1
and the inequalities under
\mathcal{E}
, we bound
\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
as

\displaystyle\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\leq\sigma_{X}\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left(\sigma_{X}\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}\right\|\left\|\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}+\sigma_{Y}\right)\leq 2\sigma_{X}^{2}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\left(2\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right).

Substituting back, we arrive at the bound for
(B_{2})
:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})},{\Delta}\right\rangle

(79)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\leq 2c_{10}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(2\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\left\|\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}.

(c)
(B_{1})
bound and final bound of
T_{2}

Since both
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}
and
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
are feasible, we have
\Delta\in\Lambda
defined in (
57
). Under event
\mathcal{E}_{5}
given in (
58
), we have

\displaystyle(B_{1})=\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{c_{5}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}^{4}\overline{\kappa}\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}.

(80)

The following lemma provides an upper bound for
\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})
under the event
\mathcal{E}
. Its proof is deferred to Appendix
H.5
.

Lemma E.1
.

On the event
\mathcal{E}
, the Gaussian width of
\Lambda
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})\leq 8\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}}.

Substituting this bound into (
80
), yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}-16c_{5}^{2}\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-3}\sigma_{X}^{4}\overline{\kappa}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}\varrho^{2}r_{\textsf{ca}}.

(81)

Finally, we return to (
76
) and plug in our results in (
79
), (
81
), to have

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}-16c_{5}^{2}\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-3}\sigma_{X}^{4}\overline{\kappa}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}\varrho^{2}r_{\textsf{ca}}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\leq 2c_{9}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(2\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\left\|\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\leq 4c_{9}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(2\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2},

where the last step uses
\alpha_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\alpha}_{\text{FT-DIP}}=\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}\Delta
from (
78
) and the bound
\lVert{{\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}}}\rVert\leq 2\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}
on the event
\mathcal{E}
.
Rearranging terms, using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for
a,b\geq 0
,
{\kappa}_{\text{min}}=\sigma_{X}^{2}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}
,
\overline{\kappa}\geq 1
, and simplifying, we can deduce

\displaystyle T_{2}=\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\leq c^{\prime}\overline{\kappa}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left[\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right]\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}.

(82)

E.1.3
Combining
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
to conclude

Finally, combining (
74
) and (
82
), we conclude that with probability at least
1-10\delta
over the randomness of all datasets
\mathcal{D}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},\mathcal{D}^{(0)},\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
, the following bound holds

\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq T_{1}+T_{2}\leq c^{\prime}\overline{\kappa}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left[\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right]\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\\
+c_{9}\overline{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+c_{9}\overline{\kappa}^{2}\left[{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}+\eta_{2}\right]\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}\\
+c_{9}\overline{\kappa}^{2}\left[({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+({\kappa}_{\text{min}}+{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}})\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}+\eta_{2}\right]\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}.

The excess risk is then bounded as

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}}})=(\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}-\beta^{\star})^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}-\beta^{\star})\leq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-DIP}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

E.2
Proof of Theorem
4.3

We introduce the shorthand notation
\Delta=\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
. To deal with the
\ell_{1}
penalty in FT-OLS-Src (
21
), our proof strategy to bound
\lVert{\Delta}\rVert_{2}
follows closely the literature on sparse linear regression (see e.g. Chapter 7 of
[
60
]
). The main additional technicality in our proof is due to the fact that we only know
(\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})
is sparse in population quantities, however, the
\ell_{1}
penalty in (
21
) is formulated with respect to an empirical estimate
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
. Consequently, we need to make use of the source data samples with size
n^{(1)}
to accurately estimate
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
, and account for its finite-sample uncertainty when deriving the final risk bound.

Based on the above intuition, in the regime where
n^{(1)}
is large, we expect the risk bound which scales as
{r_{\textsf{sc}}}/{n^{(0)}}
where
r_{\textsf{sc}}
is the sparsity level and
n^{(0)}
is the target sample size, as explained in Section
4.2
. The proof is organized as follows.

(a)

(Feasibility and basic inequality) First, after verifying the feasibility of
\beta^{\star}
in (
21
), we obtain a basic inequality using the optimality of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
. The basic inequality contains a quadratic term in
\Delta
(denoted
T_{1}
) and a linear term in
\Delta
(denoted
T_{2}
).

(b)

(Upper bounding
T_{2}
) Second, we bound
T_{2}
using sub-exponential concentration and the sparsity assumption.

(c)

(Lower bounding
T_{1}
) Third, we bound
T_{1}
using sub-Gaussian concentration and a restricted eigenvalue condition.

(d)

(Combining) Finally, we arrive at an upper bound on
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}
after combining above bounds and rearranging terms in the basic inequality.

Before we proceed, we define high probability events under which empirical
estimates concentrate. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})}\right]\right\|_{\infty}

(83)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}\eta_{\star}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\right)\Bigg\},

where
\eta_{\star}\coloneqq{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\right)
.
By applying Lemma
A.8
for concentration of product of sub-Gaussian vectors around its mean, and using the fact that
X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\beta^{\star}-Y^{{(0)}}
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{Y}
by Lemma
A.1
, with
n^{(0)}\geq\log d+\log(1/\delta)
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\coloneqq\left\{\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\leq c_{2}\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right\},

(84)

where
\eta_{\text{LS}}\coloneqq{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\right)
. By Proposition
C.1
with
M=1
and the assumption
n^{(1)}\geq c_{1}\max\{d_{\delta}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2},d\log(1/\delta)\}
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
.

Define the set

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\left\{x:\left\|x\right\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|x\right\|_{2}+2c_{2}\eta_{\text{LS}}d\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right\}\cap\left\{x:\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq 2\rho\right\},

(85)

and the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}\coloneqq\left\{\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}x\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{c^{\prime 2}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}},\quad\forall x\in\Lambda\right\}.

(86)

Since
\Lambda
contains origin, applying Lemma
A.6
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
holds with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}
.

Finally, define the intersection of the events

\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}.

Note that
\mathcal{E}
depends only on the source and unlabeled target datasets and is independent of the labeled target dataset. In the following, we prove our result under the event
\mathcal{E}
.

(a) Feasibility and basic inequality

We begin by noting that
\beta^{\star}
satisfies the constraint in Equation (
21
) by construction: we choose
\rho\geq\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}
and
\gamma=\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\|_{1}
. By the optimality of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\|\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}}-\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\|\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}}-\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.

Rearranging the terms, we arrive at the following basic inequality

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}}_{\coloneqq T_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})},{\Delta}\right\rangle

(87)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})}\right]},{\Delta}\right\rangle}_{\coloneqq T_{2}},

where step
(i)
holds because
\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}}}\right]
, by the definition of
\beta^{\star}
. It remains to bound
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
to arrive at a bound on
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}
.

(b) Upper-bounding
T_{2}

We apply Hölder’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n^{(0)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}(\textbf{X}^{{(0)}}\beta^{\star}-\textbf{Y}^{{(0)}})}\right]\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{1}.

(88)

To relate
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{1}
with
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}
, we make use of the sparsity assumption. Let
S
denote the support of
\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}
as defined in Assumption
4
. Using the feasibility of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\Delta\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\Delta)_{S}\right\|_{1}+\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\Delta)_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\geq}\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S}\right\|_{1}-\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}-\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1},

where step
(i)
uses the triangle inequality twice. Rearranging and simplifying, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|(\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}+\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|(\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1},

where step
(ii)
holds because
(\beta^{\star}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}=0
. Using the fact that
|S|=r_{\textsf{sc}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{1}=\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\Delta_{S}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|(\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}})_{S^{c}}\right\|_{1}

(89)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}+2\sqrt{d-r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}-\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}2\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}+2c_{2}\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}},

where step (i) follows under event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
given in (
84
).
Returning to (
88
), under event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq c_{3}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}\eta_{\star}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\right)\left[\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}+\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right].

(90)

(c) Lower-bounding
T_{1}

Next, we derive a lower bound on
T_{1}
. Due to the feasibility of
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}
and
\beta^{\star}
, we have
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\leq 2\rho
. Hence, on the event where the inequality (
89
) holds, we have
\Delta\in\Lambda
where
\Lambda
is defined in (
85
). Under
\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{1}\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{c^{\prime 2}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}.

(91)

Furthermore, the Gaussian width of
\Lambda
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})\leq c_{4}\left[\rho\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}\log d}+\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}\log(d)\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right].

(92)

This is because for
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{n})
,
\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\left\|x\right\|_{1}\left\|g\right\|_{\infty}}\right]
.
By
[
59
, Exercise 2.5.10]
,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|g\right\|_{\infty}}\right]\leq c\sqrt{\log d}
.
Plugging (
92
) into (
91
), and using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
and simplifying, we obtain

\displaystyle T_{1}\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}-c_{5}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\left(\frac{\rho^{2}r_{\textsf{sc}}\log d}{n^{(0)}}+\eta_{\text{LS}}^{2}\frac{d^{2}\log(d)\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}\right)\log(1/\delta).

(93)

(d) Combining and conclude

Finally, combining (
87
), (
90
), (
93
), and rearranging, leads to

\frac{1}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq c_{3}\eta_{\star}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}\right)\left[\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}+\eta_{\text{LS}}\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}\right]\\
+c_{5}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\left(\frac{\rho^{2}r_{\textsf{sc}}\log d}{n^{(0)}}+\eta_{\text{LS}}^{2}\frac{d^{2}\log(d)\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}\right)\log(1/\delta).

Using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for
a,b\geq 0
together with
\log d\geq 1
,
\log(1/\delta)\geq 1
, we deduce that under
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\left(\eta_{\star}^{2}+{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\rho^{2}\right)\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}\left(\log d+\log(1/\delta)\right)}{n^{(0)}}\log(1/\delta)\right]+

\displaystyle\quad\quad c\left[\left(\eta_{\star}\eta_{\text{LS}}+{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\overline{\kappa}\cdot\eta_{\text{LS}}^{2}\right)\max\left\{\frac{{d}}{\sqrt{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}},\frac{d^{2}}{{n^{(0)}n^{(1)}}}\right\}\log(d)\log^{2}(1/\delta)\right].

Given the bound on the squared
\ell_{2}
norm of
\Delta
, the excess risk is bounded as

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}}})

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}-\beta^{\star})^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-OLS}}-\beta^{\star})\leq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.3
.

E.3
Proof of Theorem
4.5

Since both FT-DIP
(1)
and FT-CIP are constrained least squares problems on the linear subspace, the proof of this theorem closely follows that of Theorem
4.1
. To avoid repeating ourselves, we only provide a sketch here. The main difference in FT-CIP lies in the constraint of (
29
) which is based on different finite-sample estimates—namely,
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}},\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aug}},\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}
—whose concentration properties also differ.

From (
28
), we know
Q_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=Q_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\beta^{\star}
. This implies that
\beta^{\star}
solves the following population-level constrained optimization problem

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}=\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]\text{ s.t. }Q_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=Q_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\beta.

(94)

We also define an intermediate estimator
\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}
that uses the same population objective but uses empirical version of the constraints

\displaystyle\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}=\argmin_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[{(Y^{{(0)}}-\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}})^{2}}\right]\text{ s.t. }\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}\beta.

(95)

We decompose the error into two parts by writing
\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}=(\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}})+(\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}})
and bound each term separately

\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq{\left\|\beta^{\star}-\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}\right\|_{2}}+{\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{tar-con}}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}\right\|_{2}}.

(96)

As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we first define high probability events under which empirical estimates from multiple source domains concentrate around their population quantities. In particular, let
R_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-r_{\textsf{aw}}-1)\times(d-r_{\textsf{aw}}-1)}
and
R_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{(r_{\textsf{aw}}+1)\times(r_{\textsf{aw}}+1)}
be orthonormal matrices defined as

R_{q}=\inf_{\widetilde{R}^{\top}\widetilde{R}=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}-Q_{\textsf{aug}}\widetilde{R}\right\|,\quad\text{and}\quad R_{v}=\inf_{\widetilde{R}^{\top}\widetilde{R}=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aug}}-V_{\textsf{aug}}\widetilde{R}\right\|.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\right\|\leq c_{1}\sigma_{X}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}};\;\;\;\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\leq\lambda_{\rm min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)})\leq\lambda_{\rm max}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)})\leq 2\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}};

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}-Q_{\textsf{aug}}R_{q}\right\|\leq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}};

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aug}}-V_{\textsf{aug}}R_{v}\right\|\leq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}\;\Bigg\}.

By Lemma
A.4
and Equation (
36
), the first two bounds in
\mathcal{E}_{1}
hold with probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
, provided that
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}\geq c_{2}\max\{1,{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}\}d_{\delta}
. For the latter two bounds, we can follow the argument in Lemma
G.1
to show that they hold with probability at least
1-(2M+4)\delta
provided that
\overline{n}\geq c_{3}\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)
for some universal constant
c_{3}>0
.

Under the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we can further establish the following bounds, which are analogous to (
49
), (
50
), (
51
), (
52
),

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}};

(97)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}};

(98)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aug}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}V_{\textsf{aug}}R_{v}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}+2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}};

(99)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aug}}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1}Q_{\textsf{aug}}R_{q}\right\|\leq 2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}+2c_{1}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}.

(100)

Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\coloneqq\left\{\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{\overline{n}}}+\eta_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}\right\},

where
\eta_{1}=c_{4}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}\right)
and
\eta_{2}=c_{4}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}
. By Proposition
C.3
and the assumption that
\overline{n}\geq c_{2}\max\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d\log(1/\delta),\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)\}
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds with probability at least
1-(4M+4)\delta
.

The remaining high probability events can be defined analogously to those in Theorem
4.1
, and we denote their intersection by
\mathcal{E}
.

Then the event
\mathcal{E}
depends only on the source datasets and is independent of the labeled target dataset. Conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, the rest of the proof follow exactly as in Theorem
4.1
and uses the randomness of the labeled target dataset, so we omit the details.

In particular, with probability at least
1-c_{5}M\delta
over the randomness of all datasets, the following bound holds

\displaystyle\left\|\beta^{\star}-\widehat{\beta}_{\text{FT-CIP}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\overline{\kappa}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left[\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}\right]\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{aw}}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(0)}}}

\displaystyle+c^{\prime}\overline{\kappa}^{2}\left[\eta_{1}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}+{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}+\eta_{2}\right]\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}

\displaystyle+c^{\prime}\overline{\kappa}^{2}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left[\left\|\beta^{\star}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\right]\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}.

Translating this bound into excess risk concludes the proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Appendix F
Proof of proposition and main theorems on risk lower bounds

In this section, we prove results related to minimax lower bounds, including Proposition
3.1
, Theorem
4.2
, Theorem
4.4
, and Theorem
4.6
. We begin by recalling Fano’s inequality, which we use to establish the minimax results.

Lemma F.1

(Fano’s inequality)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a class of distributions, and suppose there exists a finite subset
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{N}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
such that
\{\beta(\mathcal{P}_{1}),\ldots,\beta(\mathcal{P}_{N})\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
2\delta
-separated set in
\ell_{2}
norm. If for some universal constant
c>0
, the KL divergences and the number of elements
N
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq c^{2}n\delta^{2},\ \forall j\neq k,\text{ and }\log N\geq 2(c^{2}n\delta^{2}+\log 2),

then the minimax risk in
\ell_{2}
norm is lower bounded as

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}.

Lemma
F.1
restates
[
60
, Proposition 15.12]
using the
\ell_{2}
norm as the semi-metric, together with Equations (15.35a) and (15.35b) of
[
60
]
. To apply this result in our settings, we construct
N
distinct pairs
\mathcal{P}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, of joint (labeled and unlabeled) source and target distributions and define the functional
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\beta^{\star}_{j}
where
\beta^{\star}_{j}
is the oracle estimator under
\mathcal{P}_{j}
, while the specific construction of each
\mathcal{P}_{j}
depends on the class of source and target distributions that we consider. Based on Lemma
F.1
, all upcoming proofs of minimax lower bounds follow the same outline:

1.

Designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}
, via imposing conditions on the parameters of SCMs.

2.

Upper bounding KL-divergence.

3.

Separation of
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})
.

4.

Conclude via Fano’s inequality.

When designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and bounding KL-divergence between two distributions, the following two lemmas are useful.

Lemma F.2

(Varshamov-Gilbert)
.

Let
\Omega=\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}
, where
m\geq 8
. Then there exists a pruned hypercube
\widetilde{\Omega}=\left\{\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N}\right\}
such that
\omega_{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
,

•

N\geq 2^{m/8}
, and

•

\left\|w_{j}-w_{k}\right\|_{1}\geq m/8,\;\;\;\forall 0\leq j<k\leq N
.

See, e.g.,
[
56
, Chapter 2.6]
for a proof.

Lemma F.3
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
