be symmetric positive-definite matrices. Then
\mathbf{I}-AB
and
\mathbf{I}-A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
have the same eigenvalues, and in particular, they are real. Furthermore, if all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-AB
are less than or equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, then

-\log\det(AB)-\textnormal{tr}\left(\mathbf{I}-AB\right)\leq\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-AB)^{2}\right).

The proof of Lemma
F.3
can be found in Section
H.6
.

F.1
Proof of Proposition
3.1

The goal is to apply Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
following the outline stated below the lemma. The main differences to the classical minimax lower bound in linear regression are

•

the anticausal SCM setting, and

•

the use of unlabeled target data.

We need to design the unlabeled data generation such that no extra information of parameters of interest is revealed. Throughout the proof, we assume
d\geq 32
, which allows us to apply Lemma
F.2
to construct the desired data generation models and invoke Fano’s inequality. Note that when
d<32
, this approach is no longer applicable, but a lower bound
\frac{c}{n^{(0)}}
for some universal constant
c>0
can still be obtained by manually constructing a suitable data generation models—where only a single number of parameters varies—and applying Fano’s inequality directly. For brevity, we omit the details for the
d<32
case, as the argument is a straightforward adaptation of the
d\geq 32
case.

1. Designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}

Let
N=2^{d/8}
. Let
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
be the pruned hypercube from Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
F.2
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{1}\geq d/8,\;\;\;\forall 0\leq j<k\leq N.

(101)

Take
\widetilde{\delta}\coloneqq\min\left\{\frac{1}{4},\sqrt{\frac{d}{128n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, and set

\displaystyle b^{{(0)}}_{j}\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{d}}\omega_{j},\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,N.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
be the joint Gaussian distribution of
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})
which satisfies the linear SCM

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b^{{(0)}}_{j}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\end{bmatrix},

where
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X},j})
and
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2})
are independent. Since
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{j}}\rVert_{2}<1
, we can choose the positive-definite matrix
\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X},j}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}+b^{{(0)}}_{j}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y})(\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}+b^{{(0)}}_{j}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y})^{\top}}\right]=\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X},j}+\nu_{Y}^{2}b^{{(0)}}_{j}b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top}=\nu_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{I}.

This choice ensures that the unlabeled target data reveal no information about
b^{{(0)}}_{j}
.

Consequently, the joint distribution of
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix given by

\Sigma_{j}\coloneqq\var\left({\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}}\right)={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b^{{(0)}}_{j}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top}&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}.

Denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
the joint distribution of the
n^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}=\{(X^{{(0)}}_{i},Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
and
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{X,j}
(the marginal on
X
), and let
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{N}\}
.

2. Upper bounding KL-divergence

Since the unlabeled distributions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{X,j}
are the same for all
j
, using the formula for the KL-divergence between two Gaussian distributions, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)=\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\left(-\log\det(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})+\text{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})-(d+1)\right),

(102)

where the unlabeled distributions do not contribute to the KL divergence. We claim that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\ \text{ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,d+1$},

(103)

where
\lambda_{\ell}(A)
denotes the
\ell
-th largest eigenvalue of
A
. Its proof is deferred to the end. Assuming (
103
), and applying Lemma
F.3
to (
102
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right).

(104)

Let’s introduce the shorthand
\Delta\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}
. To upper bound the right-hand side of (
104
), we observe that

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{tr}\left((\Sigma_{k}^{-1}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}))^{2}\right)

(105)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{tr}\left(\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Delta\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Delta\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Delta\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|^{4}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{2\nu_{Y}^{4}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})},

where
(i)
follows from the sub-multiplicative property of the operator norm, and in
(ii)
, we used

{\Delta}={{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}\\
(b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j})^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}}.

Furthermore, since
\lambda_{\rm min}(A+B)\geq\lambda_{\rm min}(A)+\lambda_{\rm min}(B)
by Weyl’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\mathbf{I}_{d+1}+\begin{bmatrix}0&b^{{(0)}}_{k}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{k}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

(106)

\displaystyle\geq{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left(\lambda_{\rm min}(\mathbf{I}_{d+1})+\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&b^{{(0)}}_{k}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{k}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left(1-\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\right).

Returning to (
105
), we obtain

\displaystyle\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right)\leq\frac{2\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\left(1-\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\right)^{2}}\leq 4\widetilde{\delta}^{2},

where the last inequality holds since
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}
and
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{k}}\rVert_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}\leq 1/4
. Plugging it into (
104
), we have

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq 2n^{(0)}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}.

(107)

3. Separation of
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})

Letting
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=b^{{(0)}}_{j}
for
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
, then

\displaystyle\min_{j\neq k}\left\|\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})-\beta(\mathcal{P}_{k})\right\|_{2}=\min_{j\neq k}{\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}}\overset{(i)}{\geq}\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{8}},

(108)

where step
(i)
holds since
\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{1}}\geq\sqrt{d/8}
from the construction (
101
).

4. Conclude via Fano’s inequality

Taking
\delta=\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{2\sqrt{8}}=\min\left\{\frac{1}{8\sqrt{8}},\frac{1}{64}\sqrt{\frac{d}{n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, it follows from (
107
), (
108
) that
\{\beta(\mathcal{P}_{1}),\ldots,\beta(\mathcal{P}_{N})\}
is a
2\delta
-packing set and

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq 64n^{(0)}\delta^{2}.

Since we assume
d\geq 32
, it can be easily checked that

\log N=\frac{d}{8}\log 2\geq 2(64n^{(0)}\delta^{2}+\log 2).

Therefore, applying Lemma
F.1
, the minimax estimation error is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}.

Finally, the minimax excess risk bound can be related to the minimax estimation error via

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))}\right]={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right],

for any
\widehat{\beta}
and
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
. Hence, we obtain the desired minimax lower bound

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}^{{(0)}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{2}\delta^{2}.

Verifying the eigenvalue condition

It remains to verify the condition (
103
). Since the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
and
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}
are identical by Lemma
F.3
, it suffices to show that the operator norm of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}
is smaller than or equal to
1/2
. To see this, observe

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|\leq\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|^{2}\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|

(109)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\overset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{\nu_{Y}^{2}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}}{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left(1-\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)},

where step
(i)
applies the inequality (
106
). Since we know
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{k}-b^{{(0)}}_{j}}\rVert_{2},\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{k}}\rVert_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}\leq 1/4
by definition of
b^{{(0)}}_{j}
s, it follows that
\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|\leq 1/2
. This completes the proof.

F.2
Proof of Theorem
4.2

The goal is to apply Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
following the outline stated below the lemma. The main differences to the setting in Proposition
3.1
are

•

the additional use of source data, and

•

the confounded additive shift interventions.

We need to design the source data and unlabeled target data generation tailored to CA shift interventions such that no extra information of parameters of interest is revealed. Throughout the proof, we assume
r_{\textsf{ca}}\geq 32
, which allows us to apply Lemma
F.2
to construct the desired data generation models and invoke Fano’s inequality. For
r_{\textsf{ca}}<32
, the proof can be easily adapted from the
r_{\textsf{ca}}\geq 32
case, so we omit the details (see also the discussion at the beginning of Appendix
F.1
).

1. Designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}

Let
N=2^{r_{\textsf{ca}}/8}
. Let
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
be the pruned hypercube from Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
F.2
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{1}\geq r_{\textsf{ca}}/8,\;\;\;\forall 0\leq j<k\leq N.

(110)

Take
\widetilde{\delta}\coloneqq\min\left\{\frac{\nu_{Y}}{4},\frac{\nu_{Y}}{8}\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{5n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, and set

\displaystyle w_{Y,j}\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}}}\omega_{j},\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,N.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
be the joint Gaussian distribution of source and target where
(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}
and
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
are specified by the following linear SCMs

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{(1)}\\
Y^{(1)}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\end{bmatrix},\text{ and }\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y},\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}bw_{Y,j}^{\top}+W_{j}\\
w_{Y,j}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}Z,

(111)

and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{X},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}})
,
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2})
, and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}})
are all independent. Here, we first pick and fix
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{\textsf{ca}}}
such that
\left\|b\right\|_{2}\leq 1/4
,
R
is orthonormal matrix (i.e.,
R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}
), and
b
is orthogonal to the columns of
R
. We then set
\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}={\nu}_{Y}^{2}(2\mathbf{I}_{d}-bb^{\top}-RR^{\top})
which is guaranteed to be positive-definite by the construction of
b
and
R
. Finally, we set
W_{j}=\nu_{Y}R-bw_{Y,j}^{\top}
, which is full rank because
\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}\leq\nu_{Y}
. With these choices, we observe

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}bb^{\top}+(bw_{Y,j}^{\top}+W_{j})(bw_{Y,j}^{\top}+W_{j})^{\top}+\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}bb^{\top}+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}RR^{\top}+\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}=2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d}.

In words, the unlabeled target data have the same covariance matrices across different
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
, and thus have the same distribution.

With the above design of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
, observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}
is same for all
j
while the joint distribution of
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j}\coloneqq\var\left({\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&b({\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}^{2})+W_{j}w_{Y,j}\\
\left(b({\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}^{2})+W_{j}w_{Y,j}\right)^{\top}&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,j}\\
\left(b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,j}\right)^{\top}&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix},

where we substitute
W_{j}=\nu_{Y}R-bw_{Y,j}^{\top}
in the last step.

Now let
{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
denote the joint distribution of

•

n^{(1)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}=\{(X^{(1)}_{i},Y^{(1)}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}_{j}
,

•

n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}_{X,j}
,

•

n^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}=\{(X^{{(0)}}_{i},Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
,

•

n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{X,j}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{N}\}
. We verify that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the conditions for applying Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
.

2. Upper bounding KL-divergence

Since the source and unlabeled (both source and target) distributions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{X,j}^{(1)}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{X,j}
are the same for all
j
and do not contribute to KL-divergence, we get the following expression for KL-divergence between two Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)=\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\left(-\log\det(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})+\text{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})-(d+1)\right).

(112)

Assuming that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, for which the proof is deferred to the end, applying Lemma
F.3
to inequality (
112
), we obtain

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right).

Let’s introduce the shorthand
\Delta\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}
. Following the same calculation in (
105
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\frac{\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})}.

Further, we observe

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left\|R(w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left(\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}

(113)

\displaystyle=2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left\|w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left((w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k})^{\top}(w_{Y,j}+w_{Y,k})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left\|w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,j}+w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}+\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{4}\widetilde{\delta}^{2},

where the last step holds since
\left\|w_{Y,j}-w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}
and
\left\|w_{Y,j}\right\|_{2},\left\|w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}\leq\nu_{Y}/4
. By Weyl’s inequality, we also have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&0\\
0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,k}\\
(b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,k})^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

(114)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}2{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&0\\
0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\left\|w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)+\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,k}\\
(b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,k})^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\geq{\nu}_{Y}^{2}-\left\|b{\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\nu_{Y}Rw_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}\geq{\nu}_{Y}^{2}-{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left\|b\right\|_{2}-\nu_{Y}\left\|w_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{2}.

Combining the inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{5n^{(0)}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}}{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}.

(115)

3. Separation of
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})

Letting
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=\frac{b}{2}+\frac{Rw_{Y,j}}{2\nu_{Y}}
for
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
, then

\displaystyle\min_{j\neq k}\left\|\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})-\beta(\mathcal{P}_{k})\right\|_{2}=\min_{j\neq k}\frac{1}{2\nu_{Y}}{\left\|Rw_{Y,j}-Rw_{Y,k}\right\|_{2}}\overset{(i)}{\geq}\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{2\sqrt{8}\nu_{Y}},

(116)

where
(i)
holds since
\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}\geq\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}/8}
by its construction (
110
).

4. Conclude via Fano’s inequality

Taking
\delta\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{4\sqrt{8}\nu_{Y}}=\min\left\{\frac{1}{16\sqrt{8}},\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{40960n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, it follows from (
115
), (
116
) that
\{\beta(\mathcal{P}_{1}),\ldots,\beta(\mathcal{P}_{N})\}
is a
2\delta
-packing set and

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq 640n^{(0)}\delta^{2}.

Since we assume
r_{\textsf{ca}}\geq 32
, it can be easily checked that

\log N=\frac{r_{\textsf{ca}}}{8}\log 2\geq 2(640n^{(0)}\delta^{2}+\log 2).

Therefore, applying Lemma
F.1
, the minimax estimation error is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}.

Finally, the minimax excess risk bound can be related to the minimax estimation error via

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))}\right]=2\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right],

for any
\widehat{\beta}
and
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, where we use the fact that
\nu_{Y}=\sigma_{Y}
since
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}
are Gaussian. Hence we have

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\sigma_{Y}^{2}\delta^{2},

proving the desired minimax lower bound.

Verifying the eigenvalue condition

To verify that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
1/2
, we proceed similarly to (
109
) and observe

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\overset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\nu_{Y}},

where
(i)
applies the inequality
\left\|A\right\|\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}
for any matrix
A
and
(ii)
applies (
113
), (
114
). Since
\widetilde{\delta}\leq\frac{\nu_{Y}}{2}
, this completes the proof.

F.3
Proof of Theorem
4.4

The goal is to apply Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
following the outline stated below the lemma. The main difference to the settings in Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
4.2
is that

•

the distributional shift now lies in the connectivity matrix (the sparse connectivity shift interventions).

We need to design the source data and unlabeled target data generation tailored to SC shift interventions such that no extra information of parameters of interest is revealed. Throughout the proof, we assume
r_{\textsf{sc}}\geq 32
, which allows us to apply Lemma
F.2
to construct the desired data generation models and invoke Fano’s inequality. For
r_{\textsf{sc}}<32
, the proof can be easily adapted from the
r_{\textsf{sc}}\geq 32
case, so we omit the details (see also the discussion at the beginning of Appendix
F.1
).

1. Designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}

Let
N=2^{r_{\textsf{sc}}/8}
. Let
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
be the pruned hypercube from Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
F.2
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{1}\geq r_{\textsf{sc}}/8,\;\;\;\forall 0\leq j<k\leq N.

(117)

Take
\widetilde{\delta}\coloneqq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}}{n^{(0)}}}\right\}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
be the joint Gaussian distribution of source and target where
(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}
and
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
are specified by the following linear SCMs

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{(1)}\\
Y^{(1)}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&b\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X^{(1)}\\
Y^{(1)}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\end{bmatrix},\text{ and }\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}B^{{(0)}}_{j}&b\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\end{bmatrix},

(118)

and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{X},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}})
,
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2})
are independent. Here, we first pick and fix
b
as

\displaystyle b\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}}\begin{pmatrix}1,&1,&\cdots&1,&0,&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}^{\top}.

That is, the first
r_{\textsf{sc}}
entries are
\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}}
and the remaining entries are zero, so that
\left\|b\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}
. Next, we set
B^{{(0)}}_{j}
as a diagonal matrix

\displaystyle B^{{(0)}}_{j}\coloneqq\diag\left(2\omega_{j},0,\ldots,0\right),

with the first
r_{\textsf{sc}}
diagonal entries equal to
2\omega_{j}
and the rest zero. We then set
\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}={\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-bb^{\top})
which is positive-definite because
\left\|b\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}\leq 1/2
by construction. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{j})^{-1}({\nu}_{Y}^{2}bb^{\top}+\Sigma_{\epsilon_{X}})(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{j})^{-\top}={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I},

i.e., the unlabeled target data share the same covariance matrix across
j
and do not reveal any information about the prediction of label.

With the above design of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
, observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}
is same for all
j
while the joint distribution of
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j}\coloneqq\var\left({\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{j})b\\
\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{j})b\right)^{\top}&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
denote the joint distribution of

•

n^{(1)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}=\{(X^{(1)}_{i},Y^{(1)}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}_{j}
,

•

n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}_{X,j}
,

•

n^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}=\{(X^{{(0)}}_{i},Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
,

•

n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}=\{\widetilde{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{X,j}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{N}\}
. We show that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the conditions for applying Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
.

2. Upper bounding KL-divergence

Since the source and unlabeled (both source and target) distributions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{X,j}^{(1)}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{X,j}
do not contribute to the calculation of KL-divergence, we get

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)=\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\left(-\log\det(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})+\text{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})-(d+1)\right).

(119)

Assuming that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, for which the proof is deferred to the end, applying Lemma
F.3
to inequality (
119
), we obtain

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right).

Let’s introduce the shorthand
\Delta\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}
. From (
105
), we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\frac{\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})}.

Further, we observe

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\nu_{Y}^{4}\left\|(B^{{(0)}}_{j}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b\right\|_{2}^{2}\overset{(i)}{=}\frac{2\nu_{Y}^{4}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}}{{r_{\textsf{sc}}}}\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\overset{(ii)}{\leq}2\nu_{Y}^{4}\widetilde{\delta}^{2},

(120)

where
(i)
follows from the definitions of
b
and
B^{{(0)}}_{j}
, and step
(ii)
uses the fact that
\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}
. And

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&0\\
0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b\\
({\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b)^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

(121)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d}&0\\
0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)+\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&{\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b\\
({\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b)^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}-\left\|{\nu}_{Y}^{2}(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b\right\|_{2}={\nu}_{Y}^{2}-\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}}\left\|\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}\overset{(i)}{\geq}\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{2},

where
(i)
follows since
\widetilde{\delta}\leq 1/2
and
\left\|\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}
.
Combining the above, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq{4n^{(0)}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}}.

(122)

3. Separation of
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})

Letting
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=(\mathbf{I}-B^{{(0)}}_{j})b
for
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
, then

\displaystyle\min_{j\neq k}\left\|\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})-\beta(\mathcal{P}_{k})\right\|_{2}=\min_{j\neq k}{\left\|(B^{{(0)}}_{j}-B^{{(0)}}_{k})b\right\|_{2}}=\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{sc}}}}\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{2}\geq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{2\sqrt{2}},

(123)

where the last step follows from (
117
).

4. Conclude via Fano’s inequality

Taking
\delta\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{4\sqrt{2}}=\min\left\{\frac{1}{16},\frac{1}{64}\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}}{2n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, it follows from (
122
), (
123
) that
\{\beta(\mathcal{P}_{1}),\ldots,\beta(\mathcal{P}_{N})\}
is a
2\delta
-packing set and

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq 128n^{(0)}\delta^{2}.

Since we assume
r_{\textsf{sc}}\geq 32
, it can be easily checked that

\log N=\frac{r_{\textsf{sc}}}{8}\log 2\geq 2(128n^{(0)}\delta^{2}+\log 2).

Therefore, applying Lemma
F.1
, the minimax estimation error is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}.

Finally, the minimax excess risk bound can be related to the minimax estimation error via

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))}\right]=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right],

for any
\widehat{\beta}
and
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, where we use the fact that
\nu_{Y}=\sigma_{Y}
since
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}
are Gaussian. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}{2}\delta^{2},

proving the desired minimax lower bound.

Verifying the eigenvalue condition

To verify that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
1/2
, we proceed similarly to (
109
) and observe

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\overset{(i)}{\leq}2\sqrt{2}\widetilde{\delta},

where
(i)
applies (
120
), (
121
). Since
\widetilde{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
, this completes the proof.

F.4
Proof of Theorem
4.6

The goal is to apply Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
following the outline stated below the lemma. The main difference to the settings in Proposition
3.1
, Theorem
4.2
, and Theorem
4.4
is that

•

the distributional shift now happens to
b
, which is the anticausal weights connecting
Y
and
X
.

•

no unlabeled target data is used.

We need to design the source data generation tailored to AW shift interventions such that no extra information of parameters of interest is revealed. Throughout the proof, we assume
r_{\textsf{aw}}\geq 32
, which allows us to apply Lemma
F.2
to construct the desired data generation models and invoke Fano’s inequality. For
r_{\textsf{aw}}<32
, the proof can be easily adapted from the
r_{\textsf{aw}}\geq 32
case, so we omit the details (see also the discussion at the beginning of Appendix
F.1
).

1. Designing
\mathcal{P}_{j}

Let
N=2^{r_{\textsf{aw}}/8}
. Let
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{N})
be the pruned hypercube from Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
F.2
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}\right\|_{1}\geq r_{\textsf{aw}}/8,\;\;\;\forall 0\leq j<k\leq N.

(124)

Take
\widetilde{\delta}\coloneqq\min\{\frac{1}{8},64\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{aw}}}{2510n^{(0)}}}\}
, and set

\displaystyle b_{\text{inv}}\coloneqq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{d-r_{\textsf{aw}}}}\mathbf{1}_{{d-r_{\textsf{aw}}}}\text{ and }\widetilde{b}^{{(0)}}_{j}=\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{\sqrt{r_{\textsf{aw}}}}\omega_{j},

(125)

where
\mathbf{1}_{{d-r_{\textsf{aw}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-r_{\textsf{aw}}}
represents the vector with all entries
1
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(M)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
denote the joint Gaussian distribution of
M=r_{\textsf{aw}}+1
source and target distributions. The source distributions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(M)}
are specified by the following linear SCMs

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{(m)}\\
Y^{(m)}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b^{(m)}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}\end{bmatrix}\text{ for $m=1,\ldots,M$},

(126)

where
\epsilon^{(m)}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d})
,
\epsilon^{(m)}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2})
are independent and for
b_{\text{non}}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
,

b^{(m)}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{\text{inv}}\\
b_{\text{non}}^{(m)}\end{pmatrix},

where
b_{\text{non}}^{(m)}
’s are chosen to satisfy
\spn\left(b_{\text{non}}^{(2)}-b_{\text{non}}^{(1)},\ldots,b_{\text{non}}^{(M)}-b_{\text{non}}^{(1)}\right)=\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{aw}}}
and then fixed. The target distribution
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
is specified by the following linear SCM

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&b^{{(0)}}_{j}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\\
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\end{bmatrix},

(127)

where
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I})
,
\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\nu}_{Y}^{2})
are independent, and

b^{{(0)}}_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{\text{inv}}\\
\widetilde{b}^{{(0)}}_{j}\end{pmatrix}.

With the above design of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(M)}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{{(0)}}
, we observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(m)}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, are the same for all
j
while the joint distribution of
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j}\coloneqq\var\left({\begin{bmatrix}X^{{(0)}}\\
Y^{{(0)}}\end{bmatrix}}\right)

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}b^{{(0)}}_{j}b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top}+\mathbf{I}_{d}&b^{{(0)}}_{j}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top}&1\end{bmatrix}.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{j}
be the joint distribution of

•

n^{(m)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}=\{(X^{(m)}_{i},Y^{(m)}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(m)}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
,

•

n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}=\{(X^{(m)}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(m)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{X,j}^{(m)}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
,

•

n^{(0)}
data points from
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}=\{(X^{{(0)}}_{i},Y^{{(0)}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(0)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{N}\}
. We show that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the conditions for applying Fano’s inequality Lemma
F.1
.

Upper bounding KL-divergence

Since the source distributions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}^{(m)}
,
m\geq 1
, are kept the same, using the formula for KL-divergence between two Gaussian distributions, we have

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)=\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\left(-\log\det(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})+\text{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})-(d+1)\right),

(128)

where the source distributions do not contribute to the KL divergence.
Assuming that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, for which the proof is deferred to the end, applying Lemma
F.3
to inequality (
128
), we obtain

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j})^{2}\right).

Let us introduce the shorthand
\Delta\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}
. From (
105
), we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq\frac{n^{(0)}}{2}\frac{\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})}.

(129)

Further, note that

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\nu_{Y}^{4}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}^{2}+2\nu_{Y}^{4}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{Y}^{4}\left(\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\right)^{2}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\nu_{Y}^{4}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2},

where for the last step, we use the inequality that for two vectors
u
and
v
,

\displaystyle\left\|uu^{\top}-vv^{\top}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}=\left\|uu^{\top}-uv^{\top}+uv^{\top}-vv^{\top}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|uu^{\top}-uv^{\top}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}+\left\|uv^{\top}-vv^{\top}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\|u\right\|_{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{2}\right)\left\|u-v\right\|_{2}.

Using the construction of
\omega_{j}
in (
124
) and
b^{{(0)}}_{j}
in (
125
), it follows

\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\widetilde{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{2},\text{ and }\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{\delta}.

Plugging into the inequality above, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\leq 3\nu_{Y}^{4}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}.

(130)

Additionally, to lower bound the minimum eigenvalue of
\Sigma_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\rm min}\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d+1}+{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}b^{{(0)}}_{k}b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&b^{{(0)}}_{k}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

(131)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\rm min}\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d+1}\right)+\lambda_{\rm min}\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}b^{{(0)}}_{k}b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&b^{{(0)}}_{k}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle={\nu}_{Y}^{2}+\lambda_{\rm min}\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}b^{{(0)}}_{k}b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\right)+\lambda_{\rm min}\left({\nu}_{Y}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0&b^{{(0)}}_{k}\\
b^{{(0)}}_{k}{}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\geq{\nu}_{Y}^{2}-{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\geq\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{2},

where the last step holds since
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{k}}\rVert_{2}\leq 1/2
. Combining (
129
) with (
130
), (
131
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq{6n^{(0)}\widetilde{\delta}^{2}}.

(132)

3. Separation of
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})

Let
\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=(\mathbf{I}+b^{{(0)}}_{j}b^{{(0)}}_{j}{}^{\top})^{-1}b^{{(0)}}_{j}
for
(X^{{(0)}},Y^{{(0)}})\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{(0)}}_{j}
. Applying the Sherman-Morrison inversion formula, we have

\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\frac{b^{{(0)}}_{j}}{1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

For
j\neq k
, note that

\displaystyle\left\|\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})-\beta(\mathcal{P}_{k})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{b^{{(0)}}_{j}}{1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}-\frac{b^{{(0)}}_{k}}{1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}+b^{{(0)}}_{j}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\left(1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\left(1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}-\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\left(\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}\right)}{\left(1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\left(1+\left\|b^{{(0)}}_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)},

where the last step applies the triangle inequality.
Since
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{j}}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\widetilde{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\lVert{b^{{(0)}}_{j}-b^{{(0)}}_{k}}\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{\widetilde{\delta}}{2\sqrt{2}}
by (
124
), we then have

\displaystyle\left\|\beta(\mathcal{P}_{j})-\beta(\mathcal{P}_{k})\right\|_{2}\geq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{32}\widetilde{\delta}.

(133)

4. Conclude via Fano’s inequality

Taking
\delta\coloneqq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{64}\widetilde{\delta}=\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{512},\sqrt{\frac{r_{\textsf{aw}}}{1255n^{(0)}}}\right\}
, it follows from (
132
), (
133
) that
\{\beta(\mathcal{P}_{1}),\ldots,\beta(\mathcal{P}_{N})\}
is a
2\delta
-packing set and

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{j}\,\|\,\mathcal{P}_{k}\right)\leq 24576n^{(0)}\delta^{2}.

Since we assume
r_{\textsf{aw}}\geq 32
, it can be easily checked that

\log N=\frac{r_{\textsf{aw}}}{8}\log 2\geq 2(24576n^{(0)}\delta^{2}+\log 2).

Therefore, applying Lemma
F.1
, the minimax estimation error is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}.

Finally, the minimax excess risk bound can be related to the minimax estimation error via

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}(\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P}))}\right]\geq\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|\widehat{\beta}-\beta(\mathcal{P})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right],

since
\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}})\geq{\nu}_{Y}^{2}
for any
\widehat{\beta}
and
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, and we have
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}=\sigma_{Y}^{2}
under Gaussianity. Hence we have

\displaystyle\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\min_{\widehat{\beta}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\delta\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\beta}}})}\right]\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}{2}\delta^{2},

proving the desired minimax lower bound.

Verifying the eigenvalue condition

To verify that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}
are less than or equal to
1/2
, we proceed similarly to (
109
) and observe

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}\right\|\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|_{\textnormal{F}}}{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{k})}\overset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{\sqrt{3}{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\widetilde{\delta}}{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}/2},

where step
(i)
applies (
130
), (
131
). Since
\widetilde{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{8}
, this completes the proof.

Appendix G
Proof of propositions

In this section, we provide technical proofs for the propositions presented in Section
C
and Section
D
.

G.1
Proof of Proposition
C.1

Denote the average population and empirical quantities across
M
source domains by

\begin{cases}\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{X}^{(m)},\;\;\overline{G}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}Y^{(m)}}\right],\text{ and }\\
\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}X^{(m)}_{i}X^{(m)}_{i}{}^{\top},\;\;\widehat{\overline{G}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}X^{(m)}_{i}Y^{(m)}_{i}.\end{cases}

Since
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}
is optimal for the quadratic program (
41
), we have the inequality

\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}-\textbf{X}^{(m)}\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}-\textbf{X}^{(m)}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Defining
\Delta\coloneqq\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{Pool}}-\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}
and performing some algebra yield the basic inequality

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{(m)}\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{2}{n^{(m)}}\left\langle{\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}(\textbf{Y}^{(m)}-\textbf{X}^{(m)}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}})},{\Delta}\right\rangle

(134)

\displaystyle=2\left\langle{\widehat{\overline{G}}-\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}},{\Delta}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}2\underbrace{\left\langle{\widehat{\overline{G}}-\overline{G}-(\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}-\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X})\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}},{\Delta}\right\rangle}_{\eqqcolon T_{2}},

where step
(i)
holds since
\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}=\overline{G}
. We can lower bound
T_{1}
as follows: if
\overline{n}\geq c_{1}\sigma_{X}^{4}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-2}\cdot\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, then applying (
36
) together with union bound yields

T_{1}\geq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(m)}\right)\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{X}^{(m)})}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}\\
\geq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2},

(135)

with probability at least
1-M\delta
. For
T_{2}
, we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and triangle inequality to get

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq\left\|\widehat{\overline{G}}-\overline{G}-(\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}-\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X})\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{Y}^{(m)}-\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(m)}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}-\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}_{1}Y^{(m)}_{1}-X^{(m)}_{1}X^{(m)}_{1}{}^{\top}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\right]\right\|_{2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}.

For each
m\geq 1
,
Y^{(m)}_{1}-X^{(m)}_{1}{}^{\top}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\coloneqq\sigma_{Y}+\sigma_{X}\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\rVert_{2}
according to Lemma
A.1
. Then, for
n^{(m)}\geq d\log(1/\delta)
, applying Lemma
A.7
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\left\|\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{Y}^{(m)}-\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(m)}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}-\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}_{1}Y^{(m)}_{1}-X^{(m)}_{1}X^{(m)}_{1}{}^{\top}\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\right]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\sigma_{X}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}}\right),\quad\forall m\geq 1,

with probability at least
1-M\delta
. It follows

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq\frac{c_{2}\sigma_{X}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\textnormal{Pool}}}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\leq c_{2}\sigma_{X}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}.

Combining with (
134
), (
135
), rearranging, and simplifying, we conclude

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\leq c_{3}\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sigma_{X}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\textnormal{Pool}}\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}},

with probability at least
1-2M\delta
.

G.2
Proof of Proposition
C.2

First recall from (
38
) that the population DIP
(1)
-cov estimator has the following explicit form

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right].

Using the similar reasoning, the finite-sample DIP
(1)
-cov estimator is given by

\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\widehat{Q}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{n^{(1)}}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(1)}{}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(1)}\widehat{Q}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n^{(1)}}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\textbf{X}^{(1)}{}^{\top}\textbf{Y}^{(1)}\right)}_{\eqqcolon\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}.

(136)

We also define an intermediate population quantity

\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}=\widehat{Q}\underbrace{\left(\widehat{Q}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\widehat{Q}\right)^{-1}\left(\widehat{Q}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(1)}Y^{(1)}}\right]\right)}_{\eqqcolon\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}}.

(137)

By the triangle inequality, we decompose the estimation error as

\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{1}}+\underbrace{\left\|\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{2}}.

(138)

We proceed to bound each term
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
separately.

Bounding
T_{1}

We treat
\widehat{Q}
as fixed and analyze the randomness of
(\textbf{X}^{(1)}
,
\textbf{Y}^{(1)})
. Since
\widehat{Q}
is orthonormal, controlling
T_{1}
reduces to bounding
\left\|\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}
.

Note that
\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
is the population OLS
(1)
estimator regressing
Y^{(1)}
onto
\widehat{Q}^{\top}X^{(1)}
, and
\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
is its plug-in finite-sample estimator. To apply Proposition
C.1
with
M=1
, we verify two conditions: (1) By Lemma
A.1
and
\lVert{{\widehat{Q}}}\rVert=1
, the random vector
\widehat{Q}^{\top}X^{(1)}
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma_{X}
. (2) Since
\sigma_{\text{min}}(AB)\geq\sigma_{\text{min}}(A)\sigma_{\text{min}}(B)
for any matrices
A
and
B
with
B
having full column rank, we get

\lambda_{\rm min}(\widehat{Q}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\widehat{Q})\geq\sigma^{2}_{\text{min}}(\widehat{Q})\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{X}^{(1)})\geq\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}.

(139)

Since
n^{(1)}\geq c\max\left\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},d\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, Proposition
C.1
with
M=1
thus gives

\left\|\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq{c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\left\|\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\right)}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}},

with probability at least
1-2\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}
. Since
\left\|\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\kappa}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}
, defining

\eta_{1}\coloneqq c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\sqrt{\kappa}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}\right),

it follows

\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widehat{Q}\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{Q}\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widetilde{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\widehat{u}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}.

(140)

Bounding
T_{2}

Now we treat
\widehat{Q}
as random and bound
T_{2}
due to the randomness of
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
. Let

\Pi_{Q}\coloneqq\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}},

be the projection matrix onto
\textnormal{col}({\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}Q})
, and define
\Pi_{\widehat{Q}}
analogously. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(\widehat{Q}\left(\widehat{Q}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\widehat{Q}\right)^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}-Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\right)\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

(141)

\displaystyle\leq{\left\|\widehat{Q}\left(\widehat{Q}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\widehat{Q}\right)^{-1}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}-Q\left(Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}Q\right)^{-1}Q^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}\right\|}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1/2}\Pi_{\widehat{Q}}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{1/2}-\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{-1/2}\Pi_{Q}\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}{}^{1/2}\right\|\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Pi_{\widehat{Q}}-\Pi_{Q}\right\|\cdot\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}.

Observe that both
\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}
and
\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}QQ^{\top}\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}
have rank
d-r_{\textsf{ca}}
, and their smallest nonzero eigenvalues are lower bounded by
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}
, as shown in (
139
). Therefore, the eigen-gap between the
d-r_{\textsf{ca}}
leading eigenvalues and the remaining eigenvalues (i.e., zeros) is at least
\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}
. Applying the Davis-Kahan theorem
[
67
, Theorem 1]
, we can control the subspace angle between the column space of the two projection matrices

\displaystyle\left\|\sin\Theta(U,\widehat{U})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}QQ^{\top}\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}-\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}\right\|}{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}

(142)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{max}}\left\|QQ^{\top}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\right\|}{\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}\left\|QQ^{\top}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\right\|,

where
U,\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r_{\textsf{ca}})}
are orthonormal bases for
\textnormal{col}({\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}Q})
,
\textnormal{col}({\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}\widehat{Q}})
, respectively.

By
[
6
, Exercise VII.1.11]
, we further have the equivalence

\left\|\sin\Theta(U,\widehat{U})\right\|=\left\|UU^{\top}-\widehat{U}\widehat{U}^{\top}\right\|\overset{(i)}{=}\left\|\Pi_{Q}-\Pi_{\widehat{Q}}\right\|,

(143)

where step
(i)
holds because
U
and
\sqrt{\Sigma_{X}^{(1)}}Q
span the same column spaces, so
UU^{\top}=\Pi_{Q}
, and similarly,
\widehat{U}\widehat{U}^{\top}=\Pi_{\widehat{Q}}
. Additionally, we have

\overline{\kappa}\left\|QQ^{\top}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\right\|=\overline{\kappa}\left\|\sin\Theta(Q,\widehat{Q})\right\|,

so under the assumption that
n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)},n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}\geq c_{2}^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}^{2}\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, Lemma
A.5
(with the eigen-gap
\lambda_{\textnormal{d-gap}}
defined in (
17
)) implies

\displaystyle\overline{\kappa}\left\|QQ^{\top}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}\right\|\leq c_{2}\overline{\kappa}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right),

(144)

with probability at least
1-\delta
over randomness of
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}
. Combining (
142
) (
143
), (
144
) with (
141
), we get

\left\|\widetilde{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{c_{2}\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}{\kappa}_{\text{d-gap}}\left\|\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{LS}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon\eta_{2}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right).

(145)

Combining

Finally, combining (
138
), (
140
), (
145
) together, we conclude that with probability at least
1-3\delta
over the randomness of all datasets,
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}
satisfies

\left\|\widehat{\beta}^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}-\beta^{(1)}_{\textnormal{DIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(1)}}}+\eta_{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n_{\textnormal{u}}^{(0)}}}\right),

(146)

proving the desired result.

G.3
Proof of Proposition
C.3

The proof proceeds similarly to that of Proposition
C.2
. Define the population and empirical average quantities

\begin{cases}\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{X}^{(m)},\;\;\overline{G}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{(m)}Y^{(m)}}\right],\text{ and }\\
\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}X^{(m)}_{i}X^{(m)}_{i}{}^{\top},\;\;\widehat{\overline{G}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{n^{(m)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}X^{(m)}_{i}Y^{(m)}_{i}.\end{cases}

From Appendix
B.3
, we know the population CIP-mean has the explicit form

\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}=Q_{\textsf{aw}}(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}^{{(0)}}Q_{\textsf{aw}})^{-1}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[{X^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}}\right]=Q_{\textsf{aw}}\left(Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X}Q_{\textsf{aw}}\right)^{-1}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\overline{G}.

Similarly, the finite-sample CIP-mean can be written as

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\underbrace{\left(\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\widehat{\overline{\Sigma}}_{X}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\right)^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\widehat{\overline{G}}}_{\eqqcolon\widehat{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}},

where
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}
is an orthonormal matrix spanning the orthogonal complement of
\textnormal{col}({\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}})
. To facilitate the analysis, we introduce an intermediate estimator

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}=\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\underbrace{\left(\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\overline{{\Sigma}}_{X}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\right)^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\overline{G}}_{\eqqcolon\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}}.

We then decompose the error as

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{1}}+\underbrace{\left\|\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}}_{\eqqcolon T_{2}}.

In the following, the terms
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are bounded separately.

Bounding
T_{1}

We first treat
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}
as fixed. Observe

\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\widehat{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\widehat{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}.

As in the proof of Proposition
C.2
, we apply Proposition
C.1
, and so by the assumption
\overline{n}\geq c^{\prime}\max\left\{{\kappa}_{\text{min}}^{2}d_{\delta},d\log(1/\delta)\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq c{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\left\|\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\right)\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}},

with probability at least
1-2M\delta
over the randomness of
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{2}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
. Since
\left\|\widetilde{u}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}
, defining

\eta_{1}=c_{1}{\kappa}_{\text{min}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{Y}}{\sigma_{X}}+\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}\right),

it follows

\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n^{(m)}}}.

(147)

Bounding
T_{2}

Next we treat
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}
as random with respect to the datasets
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{1}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
. As in the proof of Proposition
C.2
(see Equations (
141
),(
142
),(
143
)), we can deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\Pi_{\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}}-\Pi_{Q_{\textsf{aw}}}\right\|\cdot\sqrt{\overline{\kappa}}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|Q_{\textsf{aw}}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}-\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\right\|\cdot\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\left\|\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}\right\|_{2}.

Further, the following lemma controls the deviation of
\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}
from
Q_{\textsf{aw}}
.

Lemma G.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
5
hold, and let
{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
and
\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
be defined in (
43
) and (
45
), respectively.

There exist universal constants
c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for
\delta\in(0,1/(2M+4))
, if
\overline{n}\geq c\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)
, the following bounds hold with probability at least
1-(2M+4)\delta
:

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}-V_{\textsf{aw}}R\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\sin\Theta(V_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}-Q_{\textsf{aw}}R\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\sin\Theta(Q_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},

where the randomness is over
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{1}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
.

The proof is provided in Appendix
H.7
. By Lemma
G.1
, with probability at least
1-(2M+4)\delta
, we have

\left\|Q_{\textsf{aw}}Q_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}-\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}^{\top}\right\|=\left\|\sin\Theta(Q_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq c_{2}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}.

Putting the pieces together, we obtain

\left\|\widetilde{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{{c_{2}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\cdot\overline{\kappa}^{3/2}\lVert{\beta_{\textnormal{Pool}}}\rVert_{2}}}_{\eqqcolon\eta_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}.

(148)

Finally, combining (
147
) and (
148
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{CIP}}-\beta_{\textnormal{CIP}}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{1}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{\overline{n}}}+\eta_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},

with probability at least
1-(4M+4)\delta
. This completes the proof of the proposition.

G.4
Proof of Proposition
D.1

The proof follows a standard technique for model selection (e.g.
[
2
, Section 4.6]
) with minor modifications. For a fixed
\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}
, define
\ell_{i}(\theta)\coloneqq\ell(f_{\theta}(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}),\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i})=(\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i}-f_{\theta}(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}))^{2}
, and the empirical target risk over the hold-out validation dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}
as

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{{(0)}}({f_{\theta};\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}})=\frac{1}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\ell_{i}(\theta)=\frac{1}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}(\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i}-f_{\theta}(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i}))^{2}.

Since by assumption of the proposition,
\overline{Y}^{{(0)}}_{i}-f_{\theta}(\overline{X}^{{(0)}}_{i})
is a sub-Gaussian random variable with parameter
\sigma_{\ell}
, it follows from Lemma
A.2
that
\ell_{i}(\theta)
is a sub-exponential random variable with parameter
K=\sigma_{\ell}^{2}
. Applying Bernstein’s inequality
[
59
, Theorem 2.8.1]
, we then have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{{(0)}}({f_{\theta};\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}})-\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\theta}})}\right|\geq\frac{t}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}\right\}\leq 2\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}K^{2}},\frac{t}{K}\right\}\right),

where
c_{1}>0
is a universal constant and the randomness is with respect to
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}
. Using the union bound, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\sup_{\theta\in\{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{L}}\}}\left|{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{{(0)}}({f_{\theta};\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}})-\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\theta}})}\right|\geq c_{2}K\sqrt{\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}}\right\}\leq\delta,

(149)

provided that
n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}\geq\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{c_{1}}
, where
c_{2}>0
is a universal constant. It follows that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}}})

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}};\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}})+c_{2}K\sqrt{\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{j}}};\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{\textnormal{val}}})+c_{2}K\sqrt{\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}\text{ for all $j=1,\ldots,L$}

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f_{\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{FT},{j}}}})+2c_{2}K\sqrt{\frac{\log(2L/\delta)}{n^{{(0)}}_{\textnormal{val}}}}\text{ for all $j=1,\ldots,L$},

where step
(i)
uses the inequality (
149
), step
(ii)
follows from the definition of
\widehat{\theta}_{\textnormal{final}}
as the minimizer of the empirical validation risk, and step
(iii)
applies (
149
) again. Subtracting
\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}^{{(0)}}({f})
from both sides and taking the minimum over
j
, we obtain the desired result.

Appendix H
Proof of lemmas

In this section, we provide proofs for all lemmas.

H.1
Proof of Lemma
A.5

Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}
denote the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}
, and let
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\widehat{\lambda}_{d}
denote the eigenvalues of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}
. Note that
\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}
is not positive definite, so
\lambda_{d}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{d}
can take negative values. Lemma
A.4
together with Weyl’s inequality implies that for all
j=1,\ldots,d
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|{\lambda_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}-(\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}\right\|+\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\sigma_{W}^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\widetilde{n}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\widetilde{n}}}\right).

For any
j
with
\left|{\lambda_{j}}\right|<\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\rm gap}
, then

\displaystyle\left|{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right|\leq\left|{\lambda_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}\right|+\left|{\lambda_{j}}\right|\leq\frac{3}{4}\lambda_{\rm gap},

(150)

as long as
n,\widetilde{n}\geq c_{2}{\sigma_{W}^{4}\lambda_{\rm gap}^{-2}\cdot\max\left\{d,\log(1/\delta)\right\}}
for a large enough
c_{2}>0
.

Let
S_{1}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:|\lambda|\geq\lambda_{\rm gap}\}
and
S_{2}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:|\lambda|\leq\frac{3}{4}\lambda_{\rm gap}\}
. Note that
\text{dist}(S_{1},S_{2})=\lambda_{\rm gap}/4
, which together with Eq (
150
) implies that the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}
corresponding to
V
,
\widehat{Q}
lie in
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
respectively. Therefore, the generalization of Davis-Kahan theorem
[
6
, Theorem VII.3.2]
implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\sin\Theta(V,\widehat{V})\right\|\leq\frac{c_{3}\left\|\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}-\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}\right)\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm gap}},

(151)

for some universal constant
c_{3}>0
. Combining with
[
12
, Lemma 2.5, Lemma 2.6]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}-VR\right\|\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|VV^{\top}-\widehat{V}\widehat{V}^{\top}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\left\|\sin\Theta(V,\widehat{V})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{3}\sqrt{2}\left\|\Sigma_{W}-\Sigma_{\widetilde{W}}-\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{W}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{W}}\right)\right\|}{\lambda_{\rm gap}}.

On the same event where Lemma
A.4
holds, we then have

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}-VR\right\|\leq c_{4}\frac{\sigma_{W}^{2}}{\lambda_{\rm gap}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\widetilde{n}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\widetilde{n}}}\right).

Similarly, we can obtain the bound for
\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}-QR\right\|
, completing the proof.

H.2
Proof of Lemma
A.6

Let
\gamma(T)=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in T}|\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]|
denote the Gaussian complexity of
T
.
When
W_{i}
is an isotropic sub-Gaussian random vector (i.e.,
\Sigma_{W}=\mathbf{I}_{d}
), the result of the lemma follows from the matrix deviation inequalities given in
[
59
, Exercise 9.1.8, 9.1.9]
. Without the assumption that
W_{i}
is isotropic, we can use the arguments from
[
37
, Remark 1.2]
to derive:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in T}\left|{\left\|\textbf{W}x\right\|_{2}-\sqrt{n}\left\|\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}x\right\|_{2}}\right|=\sup_{x\in\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T}\left|{\left\|\textbf{Z}x\right\|_{2}-\sqrt{n}\left\|x\right\|_{2}}\right|\leq cK^{2}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\gamma(\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T),

with probability at least
1-2\delta
. Here
\textbf{Z}=\textbf{W}\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}^{-1}}
is the whitened version of
W
, and
K
is the sub-Gaussian parameter of
\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}^{-1}}W_{i}
.

Since
\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T
contains the origin, it follows from
[
59
, Exercise 7.6.9]
that
\gamma(\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T)=\mathbb{W}({\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T})
. Furthermore, by
[
59
, Exercise 7.5.4]
we have

\mathbb{W}({\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}T})\leq\left\|\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}\right\|\mathbb{W}({T})=\sqrt{\left\|{\Sigma_{W}}\right\|}\mathbb{W}({T}).

Together with the bound
K\leq\sigma_{W}\sqrt{\lambda_{\rm min}^{-1}(\Sigma_{W})}
, for any
x\in T
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|\textbf{W}x\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\sqrt{\Sigma_{W}}x\right\|_{2}-\frac{c\sigma_{W}^{2}\lambda_{\rm min}^{-1}(\Sigma_{W})\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\sqrt{\left\|{\Sigma_{W}}\right\|}\mathbb{W}({T})}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{W})}\left\|x\right\|_{2}-\frac{c\sigma_{W}^{2}\lambda_{\rm min}^{-1}(\Sigma_{W})\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\sqrt{\left\|{\Sigma_{W}}\right\|}\mathbb{W}({T})}{\sqrt{n}}.

Using the inequality
(a-b)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-b^{2}
, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\textbf{W}x\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{W})}{2}\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{c^{2}\sigma_{W}^{4}\lambda_{\rm min}^{-2}(\Sigma_{W})\log(1/\delta){\left\|{\Sigma_{W}}\right\|}\mathbb{W}({T})^{2}}{{n}}.

H.3
Proof of Lemma
A.7

Let us introduce the shorthand
Q_{i}=W_{i}Z_{i}
. By
[
39
, Proposition 7]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\left(\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}}\right]\right)\right\|_{2}=\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]\right\|_{2}\\
\leq c_{1}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]\right\|_{2}^{4}}\right]^{1/4}}_{\text{Term (A)}}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+c_{1}\underbrace{\left\|\left\|Q_{1}\right\|_{2}\right\|_{\psi_{1}}}_{\text{Term (B)}}{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}},

where
c_{1}>0
is an universal constant. Since
Q_{1}
is sub-exponential with parameter
\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
by Lemma
A.2
, the centering lemma
[
59
, Exercise 2.7.10]
implies that
Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]
is also sub-exponential with parameter
c_{2}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
for some universal constant
c_{2}>0
. The following lemma bounds the
4
-th moment of the random variable
\left\|Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]\right\|_{2}
.

Lemma H.1
.

There is an universal constant
c_{3}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]\leq c_{3}d^{2}\sigma_{W}^{4}\sigma_{Z}^{4}.

Applying Lemma
H.1
, Term (A) can be bounded as

\displaystyle\text{Term (A)}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\|Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]\right\|_{2}^{4}}\right]^{1/4}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\sqrt{d}\cdot\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}.

To bound Term (B), we use the variational form of
\lVert{Q_{1}}\rVert_{2}
, i.e.,

\lVert{Q_{1}}\rVert_{2}=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}}\left|{v^{\top}Q_{1}}\right|,

where
\mathbb{B}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert{v}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\}
is a
\ell_{2}
ball with unit-norm. Using
[
60
, Example 5.8]
, the metric entropy of
\mathbb{B}
is bounded by

\log\mathcal{N}\left({\epsilon},{\mathbb{B}}\right)\leq d\log\left(1+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right).

Additionally, from Lemma
A.2
, for every
u,v\in\mathbb{B}
,
\frac{(u-v)}{\lVert{u-v}\rVert_{2}}^{\top}Q_{1}
is a sub-exponential random variable with parameter
\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
, that is,

\left\|(u-v)^{\top}Q_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\lVert{u-v}\rVert_{2}.

This shows that the condition of
[
57
, Theorem 2.2.4]
is satisfied with the constant
\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
. Therefore, applying
[
57
, Corollary 2.2.5]
we get

\displaystyle\left\|\left\|Q_{1}\right\|_{2}\right\|_{\psi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sup_{v\in\mathbb{B}}\left|{v^{\top}Q_{1}}\right|\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\int_{0}^{\textnormal{diam}{\left({\mathbb{B}}\right)}}\psi_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{N}\left({\epsilon/2},{\mathbb{B}}\right)\right)d\epsilon,

where
\psi_{1}^{-1}(x)=\log(1+x)
, and
c_{4}>0
is an universal constant. Since
\textnormal{diam}{\left({\mathbb{B}}\right)}=2
, it follows

\displaystyle\left\|\left\|Q_{1}\right\|_{2}\right\|_{\psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\int_{0}^{2}\log\left(1+\mathcal{N}\left({\epsilon/2},{\mathbb{B}}\right)\right)d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\int_{0}^{2}\log\left(1+\left(1+\frac{4}{\epsilon}\right)^{d}\right)d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\int_{0}^{2}\log\left(1+\frac{4}{\epsilon}\right)^{2d}d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\int_{0}^{2}2d\log\left(\frac{8}{\epsilon}\right)d\epsilon\leq 10c_{4}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\cdot d,

where the third step uses the inequality
1+x\leq x^{2}
for all
x\geq 3
. Putting everything together, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n}\left(\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textbf{W}^{\top}\textbf{Z}}\right]\right)\right\|_{2}\leq c\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{d\log(1/\delta)}{n}\right\},

where
c>0
is a universal constant.

Proof of Lemma
H.1
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}=Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{1}}\right]
and denote by
\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}
the
i
-th component of
\widetilde{Q}
. Then we can compute

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{1}-\mathbb{E}[Q_{1}]\|_{2}^{4}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{2}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{4}]+\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{2}\widetilde{Q}_{[j]}^{2}]\\
\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{4}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{4}]+\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[j]}^{4}]\right)=d\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{4}],

where the inequality uses the identity
ab\leq\frac{a^{2}+b^{2}}{2}
. Since
\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}
is a sub-exponential random variable with parameter
c_{2}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
, by
[
59
, Proposition 2.7.1]
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Q}_{[i]}^{4}]\leq c_{3}\sigma_{W}^{4}\sigma_{Z}^{4}
for some constant
c_{3}>0
. Plugging into the above inequality proves the lemma.
∎

H.4
Proof of Lemma
A.8

We use the shorthand
Q_{i}=W_{i}Z_{i}
. From the proof of Lemma
A.7
, there is an universal constant
c_{1}>0
such that
Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right]
is sub-exponential with parameter
c_{1}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
. By the definition of the sub-exponential random vector, it follows that for any standard basis vector
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
j=1,\ldots,d
,
e_{j}^{\top}(Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right])
is also a sub-exponential random variable with parameter
c_{1}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
.

Let
K=\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\left\|e_{j}^{\top}(Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right])\right\|_{\psi_{1}}
. Bernstein’s inequality
[
59
, Corollary 2.8.3]
provides that for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{j}^{\top}(Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right])}\right|\geq t}\right\}\leq 2\exp\left[-c\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{2}},\frac{t}{K}\right)n\right].

Applying the union bound, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\left|{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{j}^{\top}(Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right])}\right|\geq t}\right\}\leq 2d\exp\left[-c\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{2}},\frac{t}{K}\right)n\right],

which implies that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\left|{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{j}^{\top}(Q_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[{Q_{i}}\right])}\right|\leq c_{2}K\left(\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right),

for some universal constant
c_{2}>0
. Since
K\leq c_{1}\sigma_{W}\sigma_{Z}
, this completes the proof.

H.5
Proof of Lemma
E.1

Let
