g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
be a standard Gaussian random vector. Since
\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}(\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{\top}+\widehat{V}\widehat{V}^{\top})\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}=\mathbf{I}_{d}
, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x}\right\rangle+\left\langle{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x}\right\rangle.

By taking the supremum over
x\in\Lambda
on both sides and taking the expectation with respect to
g
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{\widehat{Q}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x}\right\rangle}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}x}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}\left\langle{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{x}\right\rangle}\right],

where
\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)
represent
\ell_{2}
ball in
\mathbb{R}^{r_{\textsf{ca}}}
with radius
2\varrho
.

Let
\Sigma=\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-2}\widehat{V}
. Then we can check that
\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}\left\langle{\widehat{V}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-1}g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}\left\langle{\sqrt{\Sigma}g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}\left\langle{g},{\sqrt{\Sigma}x}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{x\in\sqrt{\Sigma}\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}\left\langle{g},{x}\right\rangle}\right]=\mathbb{W}({\sqrt{\Sigma}\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}).

Applying
[
59
, Exercise 7.5.4]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({\sqrt{\Sigma}\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)})\leq\left\|\sqrt{\Sigma}\right\|\mathbb{W}({\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)})=\sqrt{\left\|\Sigma\right\|}\mathbb{W}({\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)}).

Since
\mathbb{W}({\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)})\leq 2\varrho\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}}
(e.g.
[
60
, Example 5.13]
) and on the event
\mathcal{E}
,
\left\|\Sigma\right\|\leq\lVert{{\widehat{\Sigma}_{X}^{{(0)}}{}^{-2}}}\rVert\leq 4\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}({\Lambda})\leq\mathbb{W}({\sqrt{\Sigma}\mathbb{B}_{r_{\textsf{ca}}}(2\varrho)})\leq 8\varrho\overline{\lambda}_{\textnormal{min}}^{-1}\sqrt{r_{\textsf{ca}}},

which completes the proof of the lemma.

H.6
Proof of Lemma
F.3

Set
C:=A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}\succ 0
. Since
AB=A^{-1/2}CA^{1/2}
, the matrices
AB
and
C
are similar, so they have the same eigenvalues. In particular, the eigenvalues of
AB
(hence of
\mathbf{I}-AB
) real. Then it follows

\det(AB)=\det(C),\text{ and }\textnormal{tr}\left(\mathbf{I}-AB\right)=\textnormal{tr}\left(\mathbf{I}-C\right),\text{ and }\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-AB)^{2}\right)=\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-C)^{2}\right).

Thus, it suffices to prove

-\log\det\left(C\right)-\textnormal{tr}\left(\mathbf{I}-C\right)\leq\textnormal{tr}\left((\mathbf{I}-C)^{2}\right).

(152)

Let
\lambda_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,p
, denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{I}-C
. Then

\det(C)=\prod_{j=1}^{p}(1-\lambda_{j}),\text{ and }\textnormal{tr}\left(\mathbf{I}-C\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}.

Substituting into (
152
), the inequality becomes

-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left(\log(1-\lambda_{j})+\lambda_{j}\right)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}^{2}.

Now, the inequality
-\log(1-x)-x\leq x^{2}
holds for any
{x}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Since the assumption of the lemma guarantees
{\lambda_{j}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all
j
, the result follows.

H.7
Proof of Lemma
G.1

First, we claim that for
\overline{n}\geq c\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}-{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\right\|\leq c^{\prime}\frac{\sigma_{X}}{\nu_{Y}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},

(153)

with probability at least
1-(2M+4)\delta
. Its proof is deferred to the end.

Given the claim (
153
), applying the modified Davis-Kahan-Wedin sine theorem (e.g.,
[
42
, Theorem 19]
), we obtain

\left\|\sin\Theta(V_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq\frac{2\left\|\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}-{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\right\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}}\leq\frac{2c^{\prime}\sigma_{X}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{\textnormal{c-gap}}\nu_{Y}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}=2c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}.

Combining with
[
12
, Lemma 2.5, Lemma 2.6]
, it follows

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}}-V_{\textsf{aw}}R\right\|\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\sin\Theta(V_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq 2\sqrt{2}c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}},

\displaystyle\inf_{R:R^{\top}R=\mathbf{I}}\left\|\widehat{Q}_{\textsf{aw}}-Q_{\textsf{aw}}R\right\|\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\sin\Theta(V_{\textsf{aw}},\widehat{V}_{\textsf{aw}})\right\|\leq 2\sqrt{2}c^{\prime}{\kappa}_{\text{c-gap}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{\overline{n}}}.

Next, we turn back to prove the claim (
153
). We decompose
\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}-{P}_{\textsf{aw}}
into two parts:

\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}-{P}_{\textsf{aw}}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(2)}-Hb^{(2)},\cdots,\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(M)}-Hb^{(M)}\end{pmatrix}}_{\coloneqq P_{1}}\\
-\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(1)}-Hb^{(1)},\cdots,\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(M-1)}-Hb^{(M-1)}\end{pmatrix}}_{\coloneqq P_{2}}.

The proofs for
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are similar, we focus on bounding
P_{1}
.

Define the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\left\{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{2}}{2}n^{(m)},\quad\forall m\in\left\{1,\ldots,M\right\}\right\}.

Since each coordinate of
\textbf{Y}^{(m)}
is sub-Gaussian, applying Hanson-Wright inequality
[
50
, Theorem 1.1]
and the bound
{\nu}_{Y}^{2}\leq 2\sigma_{Y}^{2}
from
[
59
, Proposition 2.5.2]
, for
n^{(m)}\geq c\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)
, the event
\mathcal{E}
holds with probability at least
1-2M\delta
.

We claim that
\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(m)}
defined in (
44
) has an explicit expression,
m=1,\ldots,M
:

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(m)}=Hb^{(m)}+\frac{H\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

(154)

Its proof is deferred to the end.

Now let
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
denote the
1/4
-covers of the spheres
\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathbb{S}^{M-2}
, respectively. By
[
59
, Equation (4.13)]
, the operator norm of
P_{1}
is bounded as

\left\|P_{1}\right\|\leq 2\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M},y\in\mathcal{N}}x^{\top}P_{1}y.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{M},y\in\mathcal{N}
. Using (
154
), we can write
x^{\top}P_{1}y
as

\displaystyle x^{\top}P_{1}y

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{x^{\top}H\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(2)}}\epsilon^{(2)}_{X,i}Y^{(2)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(2)}\right\|_{2}^{2}},&\cdots,&\frac{x^{\top}H\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(M)}}\epsilon^{(M)}_{X,i}Y^{(M)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(M)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\end{pmatrix}y

(155)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=2}^{M}y_{[m-1]}\frac{x^{\top}H\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=2}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}y_{[m-1]}\frac{x^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

Conditioning on
Y^{(m)}_{i}
for all
i
and
m=2,\ldots,M
, each summand in (
155
) is sub-Gaussian and independent to each other. We have

\displaystyle\left\|x^{\top}P_{1}y\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{m=2}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}y_{[m-1]}\frac{x^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}c_{1}\sum_{m=2}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\left\|y_{[m-1]}\frac{x^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}\sum_{m=2}^{M}y_{[m-1]}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\frac{(Y^{(m)}_{i})^{2}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{4}}\left\|x^{\top}H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}c_{2}\sum_{m=2}^{M}y_{[m-1]}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\frac{(Y^{(m)}_{i})^{2}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{4}}\left\|H\right\|^{2}\sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{2}=c_{2}\sum_{m=2}^{M}y_{[m-1]}^{2}\frac{\left\|H\right\|^{2}\sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{2}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}},

where steps (i) and (ii) follow from
[
59
, Proposition 2.7.1]
.

Under event
\mathcal{E}
, it follows

\displaystyle\left\|x^{\top}P_{1}y\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\leq 2c_{2}\max_{m=2,\ldots,M}\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{-2}\left\|H\right\|^{2}\sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}^{2}}{n^{(m)}}\leq\underbrace{2c_{2}\max_{m=2,\ldots,M}\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{-2}\sigma_{X}^{2}}{n^{(m)}}}_{\eqqcolon K^{2}},

where the last inequality uses the fact that
\left\|H\right\|\sigma_{\epsilon_{X}}\leq\sigma_{X}
from (
35
). Conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, applying concentration of sub-Gaussian random variables, for any
u\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|x^{\top}P_{1}y\right|\geq u}\right\}\leq 2\exp(-cu^{2}/K^{2}).

Now it remains to unfix
x
and
y
and take a union bound over
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
. Specifically, following the same argument as Step 3 in the proof of
[
59
, Theorem 4.4.5]
, we have, on the event
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\left\|P_{1}\right\|\leq c_{3}\max_{m=2,\ldots,M}\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{-1}\sigma_{X}}{\sqrt{n^{(m)}}}\left(\sqrt{d_{\delta}}+\sqrt{M-1}\right).

with probability at least
1-2\delta
. Similarly, on
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\left\|P_{2}\right\|\leq c_{3}\max_{m=1,\ldots,M-1}\frac{{\nu}_{Y}^{-1}\sigma_{X}}{\sqrt{n^{(m)}}}\left(\sqrt{d_{\delta}}+\sqrt{M-1}\right),

with probability at least
1-2\delta
. Taking union bound, along with the fact that
M\leq d
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{P}_{\textsf{aw}}-{P}_{\textsf{aw}}\right\|\leq\left\|P_{1}\right\|+\left\|P_{2}\right\|\leq c_{4}\frac{\sigma_{X}}{\nu_{Y}}\cdot\max_{m=1,\ldots,M}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\delta}}{n^{(m)}}},

with probability at least
1-(2M+4)\delta
, given that the source sample sizes satisfy
\overline{n}\geq c\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{4}}{\nu_{Y}^{4}}\log(1/\delta)
.

Finally, we prove the claim (
154
). By regressing
\textbf{X}^{(m)}
onto
\textbf{Y}^{(m)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(m)}=\frac{\textbf{X}^{(m)}{}^{\top}\textbf{Y}^{(m)}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}X^{(m)}_{i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

Since
X^{(m)}_{i}
can be expressed as
Hb^{(m)}Y^{(m)}_{i}+H\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}
under Assumption
5
, substituting this expression, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{\textsf{aw}}^{(m)}=Hb^{(m)}+\frac{H\sum_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}}\epsilon^{(m)}_{X,i}Y^{(m)}_{i}}{\left\|\textbf{Y}^{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

Appendix I
Failure of DIP under anticausal weight shift interventions

When anticausal weight (AW) shift interventions exist (Assumption
5
), the interventions depend on the label
Y
which can cause DIP to fail for achieving good target prediction performance. In such cases, matching the marginal covariate distributions between the source and target domains suffers from an identifiability issue, where multiple projection operators can yield the same marginal distributions, but some projections may result in a large target risk. We illustrate this issue with an example below.

Example of DIP’s failure case:

Consider data generated under Assumption
5
with
M=2
source domains and one target domain. Let
b^{(1)}=(b_{[1]},b_{[2]})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
b^{(2)}=(b_{[1]},0)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
b^{{(0)}}=(b_{[1]},-b_{[2]})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The source and target data are generated from the following SCMs:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y^{(1)}=\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\\
X^{(1)}=b^{(1)}Y^{(1)}+\epsilon^{(1)}_{X},\end{cases}\begin{cases}Y^{(2)}=\epsilon^{(2)}_{Y},\\
X^{(2)}=b^{(2)}Y^{(2)}+\epsilon^{(2)}_{X},\end{cases}\begin{cases}Y^{{(0)}}=\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y},\\
X^{{(0)}}=b^{{(0)}}Y^{{(0)}}+\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X},\end{cases}

where
\epsilon^{(1)}_{Y},\epsilon^{(2)}_{Y},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
\epsilon^{(1)}_{X},\epsilon^{(2)}_{X},\epsilon^{{(0)}}_{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{2})
. Under this data generation model, consider the projection
v^{\top}X
with
v=(1,0)^{\top}
. Since the conditional distribution of
v^{\top}X=X_{[1]}
given
Y=y
is
\mathcal{N}(b_{[1]}y,1)
in all three domains,
X_{[1]}
corresponds to a conditionally invariant component.

Now suppose we apply DIP
(1)
-cov by matching the first source and target covariates via the covariance matching penalty (
12
). Then it imposes the constraint

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}X^{(1)}X^{(1)}{}^{\top}\beta}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\beta^{\top}X^{{(0)}}X^{{(0)}}{}^{\top}\beta}\right]

\displaystyle\iff\beta^{\top}(b^{(1)}b^{(1)}{}^{\top}-b^{{(0)}}b^{{(0)}}{}^{\top})\beta=0,

\displaystyle\iff\beta^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}0&b_{[1]}b_{[2]}\\
b_{[1]}b_{[2]}&0\end{pmatrix}\beta=0.

Both
\beta=(1,0)^{\top}
and
\beta=(0,1)^{\top}
are valid solutions to the above equation. However, while
\beta=(1,0)^{\top}
correctly identifies the conditionally invariant component
X_{[1]}
, choosing
\beta=(0,1)^{\top}
selects only the second covariate
X_{[2]}
, which reverses the sign of the correlation with
Y
across the first source and target domains. This results in significantly degraded target prediction performance. A similar issue arises with DIP
(2)
-cov, which matches the second source and target covariates, where other solutions besides the conditionally invariant component can have larger target risk.
